                         BOARD OF ASSESSORS

Under State mandated legislation, the Board of Assessors is responsible for the full
and fair market valuation of real and personal property for the purposes of levying
the property tax as of January 1 every year.

The Board consists of a three-member elected panel with one seat up for renewal
every year. In 2025, Michael Cannuscio was elected for a 3-year term.

The assessing staff performs all duties of the department under the supervision of the
Town Assessor and review of the Board of Assessors.

In determining assessed property values, the primary goal of the Board is to ensure
fair and equitable assessments to all taxpayers. Assessments for the average single-
family dwelling increased 6.1% to $1,291,507 and the median single-family
assessment increased 7.5% to $998,500. The average single family tax bill increased
roughly 1% to $11,055 and the median single family tax bill increased 1% to
$8,547.16. The classification hearing held on December 2, 2025, resulted in a single
tax rate of $8.56 per thousand dollars of value.

In addition to property valuations, the department also administers statutory
exemptions. Residents who own and occupy their Marblehead property as their
domicile may apply for an exemption as long as they meet the statutory requirements.
Exemptions are available to qualifying disabled veterans, elderly persons, surviving
spouses and blind individuals.

Massachusetts tax law and guidelines for exemptions are constantly changing.
Effective November 2016, under the quarterly tax billing system, the filing deadline
for real and personal property tax abatements is February 2nd, (third quarter tax
bill due date) and April 1st for statutory exemptions (three months from the mailing
of the third quarter tax bill).

The Board of Assessor’s granted 140 statutory exemptions for a total of $166,320.62
to qualified applicants and senior work-off volunteers (as of December 2025).

We urge all taxpayers who may have questions regarding abatements or exemptions
to contact the Assessor’s Office at 781-631-0236.




                                        117
                                        121
                                       121
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


The department is also responsible for excise taxes for boats and motor vehicles.
Annually, the boat owner must certify under the pains and penalties of perjury the
vessel that is listed on their mooring permit. This information is used in conjunction
with the State formula to determine the excise tax amount. For motor vehicles, the
excise tax is based upon the MSRP values provided by Registry of Motor Vehicles.

The following 2 tables show the tax dollars raised. The first table is for property and
the second table is for excise.

REAL & PERSONAL PROPERTY TAX

 CLASS                    % of CLASS           VALUATION               TAX LEVY
 Residential                     95.54%          $9,445,217,359          $80,851,061
 Commercial                       3.22%            $318,478,519           $2,726,176
 Industrial                       0.22%             $21,963,400             $188,007
 Personal Property                1.00%             $99,617,520             $852,726
 TOTAL                             100%          $9,885,276,798          $84,617,969

                            BOARD OF HEALTH

The Board of Health Promotes and Protects the Health, Wellness and Safety of
All Citizens of Marblehead While Ensuring a Clean and Healthy Environment.

A. INTRODUCTION
In the beginning of 2025, the Marblehead Board of Health’s three elected members
were: Helaine R. Hazlett, chair, Thomas Massaro, and Tom McMahon. In June,
Helaine R. Hazlett stepped down and Amanda Ritvo, was elected to serve a three-
year term.
In 2025, the Marblehead Board of Health met in formal session on seventeen (24)
occasions including Town Meeting.
At the May 2024 Town Meeting, the Board of Health proposed expanding the Board
to 5 members. It was unanimously passed by the meeting attendees. Additional Board
members will be voted in during the June 2026 election.
The Board of Health serves the town in several important ways:
     • It is the primary resource for public health discussions, activities, and policy
         development in the Town.
     • It oversees the solid waste and recycling of the town, the Marblehead
         Transfer Station, and curb side collection.
     • It serves as a local and regional resource. BOH members and staff regularly
         participate in and make valuable contributions to activities, groups, and
         offices in our region, our state, and our nation.

B. PUBLIC HEALTH SERVICES
The Board of Health provides several levels of public health services to the Town. It
manages the traditional disease prevention and control requirements and, at the same
time, is becoming more engaged in health promotion driven by the newly emerging
definition of public health:

    “PUBLIC HEALTH IS WHAT WE DO AS A SOCIETY TO CREATE
       CONDITIONS IN WHICH EVERYONE CAN BE HEALTHY”

As examples of that evolution in 2025, the Board began CAHM Creating A Healthier
Marblehead began planning for a Wellness Fair and a Substance Abuse Seminar and
introduced the concept of developing regular assessments of the health status of the
community.
The Board of Health continues to follow the Massachusetts requirements for both
state and local laws and regulations to perform many critical duties related to the




                                         119
                                         123
                                       123
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


protection of public health. These duties cover a wide range of public health control
and prevention activities, including disease surveillance; the promotion of sanitary
conditions in housing, recreational facilities, and food establishments; elimination of
nuisances; the protection of the environment; and manages solid waste and recycling.

BOH members and staff also attended numerous meetings pertaining to finance,
COVID-19, collaboration, communicable disease, tobacco control, grant application,
sanitation code updates, continuing education, emergency preparedness, mental
health as well as other public health-related issues. Meetings were properly and duly
posted with the Town Clerk.

Creating a Healthier Marblehead (CAHM) is a Board of Health initiative launched
to enhance community wellness through data-driven strategies. It focuses on eight
dimensions of health- physical, emotional, social, intellectual, spiritual,
environmental, occupational, and financial- using survey data from UMass Boston to
guide local decisions. The project is funded externally and aims to address mental
health, addition, and community wellness without using town tax dollars.
Key components of the CAHM initiative include:
     • Community Survey: Over 2,550 residents (18+) participated in a survey to
         measure wellness and identify needs, with results being used to form long-
         term strategic goals.
    • Focus Areas: The initiative addresses mental health stigma, addiction
         recovery education, and improving local health resources.
    • Strategic Partnerships: The Board of Health is collaborating with UMass
         Boston for data analysis and exploring partnerships to improve school
         lunches and support local counseling services.
    • The Board of Health received two Best Practice Grants from the
         Lieutenant Governor’s Office. Which helped support the CAHM
         initiative One was to develop a landscape for child and maternal health in
         Marblehead and the second was to help promote mental health services
         recommended by the Marblehead Mental Health Task Force and identified
         in the CAHM Wellness Survey. The Board will pursue the development of
         a 501c3 organization to held raise funds for public health services not
         supported by the town budget.

Vaccines protect you, your family, and your community from serious, preventable
disease. Learn more about how vaccines can help prevent disease, and current vaccine
recommendations.

Vaccination helps prevent the spread of disease, especially to those who are most
vulnerable to serious complications (such as infants and young children, the elderly,
and those with chronic conditions and weakened immune systems). This is a concept
known as herd immunity. By protecting yourself, you also protect members of your
community from the spread of vaccine-preventable diseases.

6. SANITATION INSPECTION SERVICES
All food service and retail food establishments are regulated by the 2013 Federal Food
Code (with 2015 Amendments). These establishments are required by law and by the
MDPH to be inspected bi-annually. Additionally, walk-through inspections are
conducted at the Board of Health’s discretion. These are a less formal visual
inspection for those establishments about which the Board has received complaints,
those making physical changes, or for the purpose of addressing any type of problem
that may arise during the year.

7. BEACH AND SWIMMING POOL INSPECTION SERVICES
Beach samples are taken on a weekly basis from June through September for
Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Gas House Beach, Grace Oliver’s Beach and
Stramski Beach. 61 samples were taken during the 2025 beach season. (Testing is
conducted following bathing beach testing standards set forth under 105 CMR Sect.)
Bathing Beach water quality must meet both the single sample and the geometric
mean1 (geomean) standards. Single sample results are intended to represent current
conditions while the geomean is intended to reflect typical water quality found during
the preceding time (typically a month). Public and semipublic pools are monitored
for mandatory on-site quality control and proper management as required by MA 105
CMR 435.000 Minimum Standards for Swimming Pool.

Trash Strike - Throughout the trash and recycling collection strike, Marblehead
employees demonstrated exceptional hard work, professionalism, and patience.
Despite increased workloads, public frustration, and challenging conditions, staff
stepped up to maintain essential services and support the community. Their calm
persistence, teamwork, and commitment to public service were critical in getting the

C. SOLID WASTE AND RECYCLING
1. THE TRANSFER STATION
The Marblehead Transfer Station is a very significant resource for the community.
Although the decades long transition to become an EPA certified waste management
facility has taken longer than expected, the Town has access to an easy to navigate
space with competent staff available to help with managing household waste,
household recycling and commercial material.

2. WASTE REDUCTION
Marblehead is continually working to maximize waste reduction, recycling and
composting to ensure the safe management, reuse, and disposal of solid and
hazardous waste. It is a difficult task to address the increasing and environmental
cost controls placed on the town by state and federal agencies. The Town needs to
focus on decreasing the amount of waste we generate and increasing the amount
recycled.

    •   Marblehead processed 9,238.49 tons of trash in 2025.
    •   Marblehead recycled a total of 3,031.59 tons of paper, commingled material,
        cardboard, and metal out of the waste stream, realizing diversion savings of
        $385,001.93.
    •   2,500 gallons of waste oil were collected for processing. (This includes
        Town garages.)

4. REDUCE, REUSE, RECYCLE, REFUSE AND ROT
Most Americans are familiar with the phrase “Reduce, Reuse and Recycle.” These
ideas have been promoted over the past decades to reduce the amount of trash and
litter here on planet Earth.

In practice, however, most of the public effort has been focused on encouraging
consumers to recycle. In Massachusetts, there has been an additional focus on
teaching consumers how to recycle correctly and to reduce contamination.
Contamination occurs when non-recycling items get mixed in with recyclables.

Despite good intentions and considerable investments in education, most American
consumers don’t manage household trash and recycling correctly. Most haul our trash
to the curb or place it in a dumpster each week with little thought as to where it goes
or what it costs to dispose of it. Yet each year, consumers, municipalities, and
businesses in Massachusetts spend millions on solid waste disposal fees. Most of this
waste ends up in landfills or incinerators.

According to the Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection,
Marblehead incinerates about 80% of its trash each year while about 20% goes to
landfills. Historically, there were more than 300 operating landfills in Massachusetts.




                                         125
                                         129
                                       129
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Today, that number is down to 6 -all of which are due to reach capacity and close by
2030. The state now ships approximately 20% of its trash to other states which is
costly and inefficient.

The goal is to move toward a zero-waste system. The Zero Waste International
Alliance (ZWIA) defines zero waste as “the conservation of all resources by means
of responsible production, consumption, reuse, and recovery of products, packaging,
and materials without burning and with no discharges to land, water, or air that
threaten the environment or human health.”

REDUCE- Buy Less.
REUSE- Use items multiple times.
RECYCLE- Give items a second life.
REFUSE- Consciously consider your consumption habits
ROT- Compost all organic waste.

Become a smart recycler by following these guidelines:
    • Empty and rinse all food and beverage containers.
    • Remove all plastic and Styrofoam packing from cardboard boxes before
       recycling.
    • Bottles, jars, jugs, and tubs: empty, rinse and replace cap.
   • Ignore the recycling symbol (with a number) that’s on most plastic items –
       it does NOT mean it’s recyclable.
   • Consult the online “Recyclopedia” for recycling answers to over 500 items:
       RecycleSmartMA.org.
   • Place it in loose – Please do not put your recyclables in a plastic bag.
   • Please NO thin-filmed Plastics such as produce bags, packaging, and bubble
       wrap.
   • Wrapping paper and tissue paper are not recyclable

5. COMPOSTING
Composting is the fifth step to achieving zero waste. Even when all actions have
been taken to use your wasted food, certain inedible parts will remain and can be
turned into compost to feed and nourish the soil. Like yard waste, food scraps can
also be composted. Composting these wastes creates a product that can be used to
help improve soils, grow the next generation of crops, and improve water quality.
Food composting could divert as much as 50% of the residential waste stream. For a
small fee, curbside composting is available in Marblehead. Please call the
Marblehead Health Office for more information.

6. SWAP SHED
A temporary Swap Shed is in place until the new one is constructed. The Swap Shop
was established by the Marblehead Board of Health, and it is run by volunteers who
are citizens of Marblehead. The Swap Shed is open on Saturdays from 9-11:30.
During the winter it is only open the first Saturday of the month. The Swap Shed
volunteers should be commended for the work they do to support the idea of Zero
Waste.

Guidelines for Use of Swap Shed:
Residents must have a current Facility Sticker to use the Swap Shed. No walk-ins are
permitted.
Items must be approved by an attendant.
Items must be functional and have all parts.
Area in front of the Swap Shop is for short term parking – 15 minutes Items left there
are intended for Reuse – Not Resale

1. MARBLEHEAD COUNSELING CENTER

In its 57th year, the Marblehead Counseling Center (MCC) continues to strengthen its
collaboration with the Board of Health to provide the Town with counseling and
community social services. These comprehensive services provide a unique
opportunity to any resident or town employee regardless of their ability to pay a
fee. Clients work with our experienced, dedicated clinicians to address a myriad of
challenges and diagnoses. Additionally, wraparound social services are provided to
enhance household stability and create connections to essential resources that help to
sustain long-term health and wellness.

During fiscal year 2026, MCC provided direct therapy to 272 different individuals
who live or work in Marblehead providing 4620 sessions for individual, couple, and
family counseling and social services.

This year MCC continued raising awareness of the Counseling Center’s mission
through a multi-pronged strategy to ease the process of connecting community
members to support. We continued to improve our new website, social media
outreach, community presentations, and more as we aimed to reinforce a steady
presence as a partner to those seeking assistance. The immense need for quality local
mental health services has not abated. New intakes have in fact surpassed pandemic-
era levels and complex, trauma-informed interventions for clients of all ages require
specialized clinical skills and extended lengths of treatment. MCC’s staff persists in
rising to meet these needs.

The Marblehead community continues to rely on the Marblehead Counseling Center
to address the many critical issues that place great strain on local households. Last
year, over 240 hours were dedicated to connecting such households to emergency
financial assistance, eviction diversion protection, utility stabilization and more
through MCC’s social service program. With the generous support of the Marblehead
Female Humane Society, our social service worker helps to carry out these efforts to
shore up resources that help our neighbors afford to live with dignity while navigating
the ever-increasing costs of living.

The Counseling Center continues to collaborate with community organizations to
enrich the network in providing programming and responsive services for a healthier
Marblehead. Working locally with the Marblehead Mental Health Task Force, the
Ministerial Association, the schools, first responders, local physicians and more
ensures a seamless, no-wrong-door approach to accessing assistance. Additional
regional partnerships with prevention planning, adult education, financial coaching




                                         128
                                         132
                                       132
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


and career development services have further expanded avenues toward longer term
sustainability of community wellness.

2. HAWC (Healing Abuse Working for Change)
The mission of HAWC is to create social change by acting against personal and
societal patterns of violence and oppression. HAWC strives to help abused women,
children, men, and nonbinary people live free from violence and fear. As a leading
domestic violence agency in Massachusetts, HAWC provides a domestic violence
helpline, emergency shelter, legal services, advocacy, and counseling services to
more than 2,500 families in the North Shore each year. HAWC understands the
complexity of abuse, and has expanded its language capacity so abused women, men,
nonbinary people, LGBTQ+ individuals, older adults, undocumented individuals, and
people with disabilities can feel comfortable accepting its services.

The Board of Health also participates in several state collaboratives and
consortia to participate in efforts to improve the overall health of the
Commonwealth. These include:

1. NORTH SHORE/CAPE ANN TOBACCO PROGRAM
The North Shore Tobacco Control Program strives to change community and social
norms regarding the use of tobacco products by supporting proven public health
efforts and initiatives to reduce the use of tobacco. This is accomplished by raising
public awareness about tobacco issues, such as youth access and secondhand smoke,
through prevention, intervention, policies, and enforcement initiatives.

The program is funded through the Massachusetts Tobacco Control Program (MTCP)
of the Department of Public Health and is part of Smoke Free Solutions North, the
MTCP Community Mobilization Network. MTCP was created through a statewide
referendum in 1992, which placed a 25-cent per pack tax on cigarettes and smokeless
tobacco products.

In accordance with grant requirements, the North Shore/Cape Ann Tobacco Alcohol Policy
Program accomplished the required program assessments for the Town of Marblehead. No
violations were identified in 2024.

In addition to the compliance checks, the North Shore/Cape Ann Tobacco Alcohol Policy
Program conducted one (1) full round of retailer inspections at each permitted
establishment as well as a pricing survey to access pricing trends across the state.

2. North Shore Public Health Collaborative (NSPHC)




                                        129
                                        133
                                       133
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


 The North Shore Public Health Collaborative (NSPHC), a collaborative of eight local
health departments: Beverly, Danvers, Lynn, Marblehead, Nahant, Peabody, Salem,
and Swampscott. Our work focuses on enhancing and expanding the services our
local health departments provide to prevent disease and promote health across our
communities.

NSPHC is funded through a state Public Health Excellence grant, which provides
local health departments with the funding for shared staff and resources, with a focus
on adopting recommendations from the statewide Blueprint for Public Health
Excellence.

Regional Training Hub
NSPHC has a separate training program, called the Northeast Training Hub. Its
primary objective is to provide standardized and consistent training to local
environmental health staff across participating municipalities. These trainings
encompass various areas, including food safety, housing inspections, camp
regulations, pool safety, septic systems, and soil assessments. Learn more about the
program here:
https://www.northshorepublichealth.com/northeastpublichealthtraininghub

3. NORTH SHORE-CAPE ANN EMERGENCY PREPAREDNESS
COALITION
The North Shore-Cape Ann Emergency Preparedness Coalition works to prepare its
communities to respond to public health threats and emergencies, which include
terrorism and outbreaks of infectious diseases as well as emergencies caused by a
force of nature. This coalition, established in 2004, is a sub-coalition within region 3
of the Massachusetts Department of Public Health (MDPH) regional Public
Emergency Preparedness Coalitions and meets monthly. The coalition receives
limited funding from a grant provided by the Center of Disease Control (CDC) and is
mandated to attain certain standards in emergency preparedness. The North Shore-
Cape Ann Emergency Preparedness Coalition has established a website with
information for the public and links to other sites that can provide valuable
information to our residents. To date, the Board of Health has provided MDPH with
24/7 contact information, submitted a Continuity of Operations Plan, participated in
numerous drills, trainings, and conferences, trained key personnel in NIMS (National
Incident Management System) and ICS (Incident Control System), updated the All-
Hazards Plan, and has submitted a template of our Special Populations Plan.

The coalition is continuing the process of increasing its database of medical and non-
medical volunteers through our website and is offering training opportunities for these




                                         130
                                         134
                                        134
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


volunteers. In the event of an infectious disease epidemic, pandemic, or a natural
disaster, volunteers will be greatly needed. Volunteers can register online and/or by
calling the Marblehead Board of Health.

4. CODE RED EMERGENCY NOTIFICATION SYSTEM
News and notifications concerning public health, trash and recycling are posted on
the Town’s website in a timely fashion. This useful media tool allows the posting of
links to sites relating to public health, emergency planning, solid waste, and beach
water quality. A subscription service on Marbleheadma.gov allows people to sign up
for email alerts regarding solid waste, recycling, public health emergencies, as well
as other important Town information.

In addition, the Board of Health members and staff attended numerous meetings
pertaining to finance, collaboration, communicable disease, tobacco control, grant
application, sanitation code updates, continuing education, emergency preparedness,
mental health as well as other public health-related issues. Meetings were properly
and duly posted with the Town Clerk

In closing, the Board of Health commends its office staff and transfer station staff for
their adaptability and dedication to their positions and work.

As we enter 2026, the Board of Health and its employees will continue to conduct
compliance inspections, investigate complaints, and promote policies and programs
to protect public health and the environment.

The Board’s long-term goal for the Town is to ensure that our community is
successful and consistently meets core mandates while striving to improve the overall
health of the Town.

The Board wishes to thank other Town Departments for assistance and cooperation
in the form of manpower, equipment, and other resources. The Board would like to
thank outgoing board member Helaine Hazlett for her years of service, dedication and

member Dr. Amanda Ritvo. The Board of Health and the Town are fortunate to have
a willing and able group of volunteers who assist in all aspects of our operation. The
Board of Health thanks all those volunteers.

BUILDING COMMISSIONER & INSPECTIONAL SERVICES

MISSION:
The Building Department ensures public safety, health and welfare by regulating
construction through Federal, State and Local Codes and By-Laws. The Building
Department, in conjunction with the Fire Prevention Office, conducts annual
inspections of public and private schools, churches, temples, day care centers, food
service establishments, nursing homes, private clubs and places of assembly.

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS:
To the Citizens of Marblehead and the Board of Selectmen, the Building Inspectional
Services Department respectfully submits our 2025 Annual Report.

PERSONNEL:
Department staff provide high-level customer service and maintain consistent
enforcement of life and building safety.

The department wishes to extend a special thanks to Brad Smith, and Eric Chisolm.
These individuals provide 24/7 behind-the-scenes support and maintenance for all
Town buildings. The work includes ongoing maintenance, updating lighting fixtures
to LED bulbs, electrical service upgrades, repairs and maintenance to the new HVAC
system at Abbot Hall and maintaining stop lights. Together, they worked
approximately 648 manhours at an estimated value of $80,000.00 to the Town.

WORK ENVIRONMENT:
Viewpoint, the digital scheduling platform provides cross departmental inspection
data, complaint tracking and an improved process for annual inspections. This system
provides an intuitive UI, convenience to the public that allows uninterrupted access
to permit applications and payments, public viewing of planning, zoning, building,
fire, electric and plumbing permits, permit fees and monetary data.

To better manage and coordinate the ongoing duties of the department, we continue
meeting weekly to discuss and document the recurring issues related to code
enforcement for multiple projects and to update staff of codes as they occur. We meet
monthly to review internal policy, workflow improvements and technical efficiencies
to benefit all staff members.

FINANCIAL DATA:

and is broken down as follows:

                       CEMETERY DEPARTMENT
The Marblehead Cemetery Department respectfully submits their Annual Report for
the Calendar year ending December 31, 2025.

Following Chapter 114 of the Mass General Laws, the Cemetery Department has
jurisdiction over all Town owned cemeteries.

The Cemetery Department is administered by a Superintendent, who is appointed by
a Cemetery Board of Commissioners. The elected three-member Board serves three-
year staggered terms and meets monthly to set policy, establish regulations and
oversee the cemetery budget.

A set of Rules and Regulations was adopted in 1945 to protect and promote the
general welfare of the Cemetery as a whole. These rules are updated periodically as
needed. All changes are carefully thought through and voted on by the Board.
Current Rules and Regulations are posted on Marbleheadma.gov under Cemetery
Department.

This Department’s top priority is the delicate task of burying the deceased in a caring
and timely manner with as little disruption and discomfort as possible for families
and friends of the deceased. The department is also responsible for the complete care
and maintenance of Waterside and Harborview Cemeteries along with developing
available land for new grave space and reclaiming open land for future cemetery use.

Within Waterside Cemetery, our full-time office clerk works diligently arranging
services, answering all questions related to Rules and Regulations, memorials,
genealogy, lot sales, lot location and all other general questions. The clerk is overseen
by the Superintendent assisting in the day-to-day operations of a very busy office.

Four full-time employees are responsible for all burials that occur, as well as the
complete maintenance of approximately forty acres within Waterside Cemetery and
Harborview Cemetery. This maintenance includes cutting grass, trimming all hedges,
trimming back all trees, trimming around each upright memorial and flush marker
along with Spring and Fall cleanup of all leaves, flowers, baskets and wreaths on lots.
This small crew is also responsible for the maintenance of all buildings, equipment,
roads, water system, walls, fences, gates and benches within our Town cemeteries.
Cemetery personnel also install all cement foundations for upright memorials and
markers as well as repair stones where an expert is not required.




                                          136
                                          140
                                        140
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Cemetery vehicles and personnel also assist the Department of Public Works plowing
the streets of Marblehead during winter months along with other Town Departments,
while making sure burial services can occur year-round. We also assist in curbside
pickup of Christmas trees for the annual tree burning.

The maintenance staff for the Recreation and Parks Department is responsible for the
care and maintenance of our historical cemeteries, Old Burial Hill, Hooper Tomb,
Harris Street and Green Street Cemeteries.

There are no dogs allowed in our Town cemeteries. Signs are posted at most
entrances. Dog bylaws and ordinances apply and are in effect within the cemeteries
and will be enforced.
§ 13-11. Seasonal prohibition of dogs from certain areas. [Added 5-4-1992 ATM by
Art. 51; amended 5-1-2006 ATM by Art. 39; 5-7-2007 ATM by Art. 31]
No person owning, harboring or having custody or control of a dog shall permit
such dog to be on any public cemetery of the Town at any time throughout the year,
nor shall such person permit such dog to be on any public beach, athletic area,
playground or park of the Town between May 1 and October 1 of each year, provided,
however, that this prohibition shall not apply to Chandler Hovey Park, Crocker Park,
Fort Sewall, Fountain Park, Upper Seaside Park, Riverhead Beach or the Town
designated Dog Park. Whoever violates the provisions of this section shall pay a fine
in accordance with the following schedule:
         A. First offense in calendar year: $25.
         B. Second or subsequent offense within calendar year: $50

Due to its deteriorating state, the hourglass pool is filled and a temporary flame lit
for the Memorial Day services only. The pool is shut down shortly following
Memorial Day. We welcome donations to the Eternal Flame Fund. We hope to
raise enough to replace or repurpose the hourglass pool.

The Cemetery Department’s operating budget comes from Annual Town Meeting
appropriations from taxation, department fees, and Perpetual Care Trust Fund
interest.

The Cemetery Sale of Lots Fund is a non-interest-bearing fund which we rely on for
the development of future grave space and much needed repairs to all buildings and
roads. The purchase of all major equipment is also paid out of this fund which is
generated by the actual sale of cemetery lots only. The Fund’s use is authorized by
the Cemetery Board of Commissioners and subject to Town Meeting appropriation.
The Perpetual Care Fund is an interest-bearing fund. Typically interest from that fund

is transferred to the General Fund to help offset the budget. This interest too is voted
on by the Cemetery Board and approved for appropriation at Town Meeting.

This Department routinely surveys other Commonwealth Communities and Private
Cemeteries as to the fees that are charged and then adjusts to our fees accordingly.

After eighteen years we saw the retirement of the Cemetery Board Chairman Richard
F. Coletti. On behalf of all Cemetery Board members and employees past and present

This year, cemetery personnel were able to have Waterside ready for Memorial Day

we were hit by a Nor’easter the Friday before the weekend. This small four-man crew
pulled off the cleanup, Memorial Day preparations and attended to emergency burial
services. This Department commends them for their hard work; this truly was a team
effort.

Locust Avenue East development began in 2015. Two sections of that area were

Town owned land for future burial space.

The new Creesy Green Veterans Cemetery, which was slated to begin development
plans in 2028, was fortunate to have a little assistance in 2022. For Arbor Day, Kelly
Tree Service generously donated their time and crew to begin Phase One of three by
taking down the trees needed. We have also been able to fund the stump grinding of
those trees just this year, which moves us further ahead of schedule in this phase.

Thank you to the Tree Department for their continued assistance with the aging trees
within our Town’s cemeteries. We also wish to thank all other Departments who
have helped in any way during the year.

        COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT AND PLANNING

MISSION:
The Community Development and Planning department (the Department) was
officially created by Article 34 of the 2024 Annual Town Meeting in May of 2024.
The Director and Department are responsible for a wide range of activities and duties,
including land use planning, housing policy, transportation projects, historic
preservation, community development, sustainability efforts, open space
conservation programs, administration of regulatory boards, neighborhood
improvement efforts, and other activities.

DEPARTMENT STAFF:
Following the creation of the Community Development and Planning department at
the 2024 Annual Town Meeting, the Select Board and Town Administrator worked
diligently to ensure it was fully staffed by the start of 2025. A key milestone was the
hiring of Brendan Callahan, the Town’s first Director of the Department of
Community Development & Planning. This appointment marked the completion of
the Department’s staffing, which had begun with the integration of longtime Senior
Clerk, Lisa Lyons, and the Sustainability Coordinator, Logan Casey into the newly
formed department, as well as the hiring of Grant Coordinator, Donna Cotterell, and
former Town Planner, Alex Eitler, in late 2024. With the Director and department
now in place, the staff is positioned to carry out its mission of guiding Marblehead’s
development in a community-focused way.

DIRECTOR OF COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT & PLANNING:
The Director of Community Development & Planning plays a pivotal role in shaping
the future of Marblehead by guiding the town’s growth and redevelopment in a
thoughtful, sustainable, and community-focused manner. Beyond overseeing day-to-
day operations, the Director is deeply involved in the long-term planning and
implementation of key initiatives that directly benefit Marblehead residents.

A significant aspect of the Director’s role is the development of comprehensive plans
that align with the town’s vision for growth, preserving its historic charm while
accommodating future needs. This includes providing technical assistance on
complex development proposals, ensuring that projects brought before the Regulatory
Boards are well-understood, thoroughly vetted, and aligned with Marblehead’s goals
for responsible development.




                                         140
                                         144
                                       144
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


The Director plays an essential part in supporting capital improvement projects,
working closely with various town departments to enhance infrastructure, parks and
open space, and town buildings. By collaborating with these departments, the
Director ensures that projects are planned and executed efficiently, leveraging
resources to maximize the town’s benefit. This collaborative approach extends to the
Director’s role in securing funding for these initiatives, where the Director works
alongside the departmental staff and from other town departments to apply for state
and federal grants aimed at enhancing the town’s assets.

In addition to the Director’s work on development and planning, the Director chairs
the newly established Marblehead Housing Committee, bringing together community
stakeholders to address the town’s housing needs and ensure that new development
reflects the community’s values. The Director also serves as a key advisor to the
Town Administrator and senior leadership team, providing strategic input on matters
ranging from policy to operational challenges.

The Director’s leadership extends to managing the department’s daily operation and
staff. This includes overseeing the preparation of the annual operating budget,
ensuring responsible allocation of resources, and administering department funds to
ensure that projects and programs are delivered on time and within budget. The
Directors guidance and coordination with the department create an environment that
fosters collaboration, accountability, and forward-thinking solutions that benefit the
town’s residents and its organizational operations.

TOWN PLANNER & SENIOR CLERK:
The establishment of the Department of Community Development & Planning was
also marked by the expansion of the Town Planner role, which has undergone
significant growth and transformation. Under the guidance of former Town Planner,
Alex Eitler, responsibilities have broadened, including the incorporation of the
Conservation Agent role into the Town Planner position. This shift reflects the
Town’s evolving approach to land use, zoning, and environmental stewardship.

The Town Planner is responsible for supporting the functions of several key
regulatory boards. This includes the Planning Board, Zoning Board of Appeals,
Design Review Committee, and Conservation Commission. The role provides
essential technical guidance to both boards and prospective applicants, ensuring
development in Marblehead adheres to established regulations while supporting the
town’s long-term planning goals. The Town Planner’s responsibilities include a
variety of tasks, such as:




                                        141
                                        145
                                       145
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


    •    Provide technical review and interpretation of planning procedures and both
         state and local legislation for regulatory boards and public bodies.
    •    Overseeing the administration of the regulatory boards, attending all
         meetings, and ensuring that the boards are fully supported in their decision-
         making processes.
    •    Conducting site visits with prospective applicants and collaborating with
         developers, landowners, and others to provide guidance on the town’s
         zoning bylaws and subdivision rules.
    •    Maintaining ongoing communication with regulatory boards, ensuring
         planning activities are aligned with boards’ priorities and keeping them
         informed of developments.
    •    Preparing and assisting with the presentation of Town Meeting, ensuring that
         all proposed initiatives are clearly communicated to residents and decision-
         makers.

The Senior Clerk, Lisa Lyons, remains integral to the operations of the Department.
The Senior Clerk primarily assists the Town Planner with processing regulatory board
applications and managing administrative duties for the Department. The Clerk
handles numerous applications filed with the regulatory boards and are a vital
resource for the public, providing guidance and answering questions related to
zoning, regulations, and board procedures.

Together, these two positions reflect the Department’s commitment to ensuring
Marblehead remains a vibrant, well-managed community, responsible for both
growth and preservation needs. At the conclusion of the 2025 calendar year, Alex
Eitler left his position as Town Planner, and the Department initiated the re-hiring
process to bring in a new Town Planner for 2026. In the interim, the department
remains dedicated to continuing its planning work and ensuring a smooth transition.

SUSTAINABILITY COORDINATOR:
The Sustainability Coordinator advances the Town of Marblehead’s environmental
and energy goals by developing and implementing programs that reduce energy use,
lower greenhouse gas emissions, and promote long-term sustainability. The position
leads the implementation of the Town’s Net Zero initiatives and works across
municipal departments, with local businesses, and with residents to support climate-
resilient and energy-efficient practices.

Key responsibilities include researching and securing grants and rebates, coordinating
and staffing the Green Marblehead Implementation Committee, conducting energy
and emissions analyses, and tracking progress toward community sustainability




                                        142
                                        146
                                       146
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


goals. The Sustainability Coordinator also supports energy efficiency improvements
in municipal buildings, contributes to capital planning efforts, and explores
opportunities such as, electrification, electric vehicle charging infrastructure, and
cross-department projects.

In addition to technical and planning work, the position emphasizes public
engagement and education. The Sustainability Coordinator designs and manages
outreach programs, collaborates with other departments and community groups, and
communicates sustainability initiatives happening in the community.

GRANT COORDINATOR:
The Grant Coordinator plays a pivotal role in supporting the town's growth and
sustainability through the identification, development, and administration of grant
funding. Established in 2024, this position is integral to securing financial resources
for projects that improve infrastructure, enhance public services, and promote
community development.

Throughout 2025, the Grant Coordinator was responsible for overseeing a wide range
of tasks, including coordinating grant applications, managing compliance with
funding requirements, and supporting interdepartmental collaboration. A significant
portion of the role involved managing American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) funding.
This included ensuring that projects met eligibility criteria, tracking progress, and
maintaining proper documentation and reporting in coordination with department
heads, project managers, and the Finance Department.

The Grant Coordinator also managed the Devereux Beach ADA Improvements
Project, coordinating grant requirements, timelines, and communication between the
Community Development & Planning Department, Recreation & Parks, and the
Disabilities Commission. This project focused on improving accessibility to public
infrastructure for residents and visitors at Devereux Beach.

In addition to these responsibilities, the Grant Coordinator led or supported the
development and submission of numerous federal, state, and private grant
applications. These applications supported a wide range of municipal priorities,
including transportation infrastructure improvements such as the Village Street
Bridge Replacement and bicycle infrastructure enhancements; historic preservation
initiatives including the Abbot Hall Attic & Accessibility Project and related
preservation planning efforts; cultural heritage programming such as the Marblehead
Rev250 initiative and the “We Were There” public humanities project; and capital

   ______________________________________________________________


and infrastructure investments tied to waterfront resilience, accessibility, and
municipal facilities.
The Grant Coordinator also played a key role in coordinating interdepartmental
collaboration during the application process, working closely with the Director, Town
Planner, Sustainability Coordinator, Finance Department, DPW, Harbors & Waters,
Recreation & Parks, the Housing Authority, and external partners to assemble project
scopes, budgets, supporting documentation, and compliance materials necessary for
competitive submissions.

The role also involved securing grants for historic preservation and cultural projects.
Notably, the Grant Coordinator contributed to the "We Were There" project, which
seeks to highlight the histories of Black and Indigenous Marbleheaders during the
Revolutionary War. This initiative, along with others, supports the town's efforts to
preserve its cultural heritage and engage the community in meaningful ways.

Moreover, the Grant Coordinator was actively involved in advancing climate
resilience and sustainability initiatives, including the development of grant
applications for the Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Action Grant, the
MAPC Accelerating Climate Resiliency Municipal Grant (Resilient by Design:
Cooling Marblehead’s Housing for the Future), coastal resilience planning for State
Street Landing and Tucker Wharf, and related sustainability-focused funding
opportunities. These projects are designed to address the increasing risks associated
with sea-level rise, extreme heat, and storm events, while also improving public
infrastructure, protecting vulnerable populations, and strengthening long-term
municipal resilience.

Through these efforts, the Grant Coordinator supported the integration of climate
adaptation strategies into broader planning, housing, and infrastructure initiatives,
helping the Town to remain competitive for future state and regional resilience
funding.

In addition to individual project management, the Grant Coordinator worked to
strengthen the town's internal systems for grant tracking and administration. This
included streamlining workflows and providing support to other departments to
improve the efficiency and effectiveness of grant management.

Through these efforts, the Grant Coordinator has been essential in securing funding
for a wide variety of projects that contribute to the town's infrastructure, cultural
preservation, sustainability, and community development goals. The position remains
a critical component of Marblehead 's ability to access external resources, reduce the

M.G.L CHAPTER 40A, SECTION 3A: MBTA COMMUNITIES ACT AND
IMPACTS OF NON-COMPLIANCE
One of the major focuses of the Department’s work in 2025 involved continued
compliance efforts with the Massachusetts General Law Chapter 40A, Section 3A:
MBTA Communities Act (Section 3A). Over the past two years, Town Meeting has
considered adopting compliant Section 3A Zoning Districts.

This effort began in 2023, responding requirements of the law, which designated
Marblehead as an “Adjacent Community”. The Town’s Planning Board and Town
Planner lead the development effort, soliciting community feedback of locations
through several public engagement sessions. The proposed plan aimed to create
zoning districts that would not only satisfy state requirements but also align with the
needs and character of the community.

The original proposal for the compliant zoning districts was presented at 2024 Annual
Town Meeting but was rejected. In the winter and spring 2025 the Planning Board
and Town Planner engaged in an extensive public outreach campaign leading up to
May 2025 Town Meeting to converse with and answer questions about the proposed
overlay plan to provide residents with a clear understanding of the Section 3A
legislation and the proposed plan. The Director and Town Planner organized several
informational sessions, including detailed PowerPoint presentations, Q&A
opportunities, and a “pop-up” engagement session at State Street Landing. These
forums were designed to ensure transparency, allow residents to ask questions, and
address misinformation about the proposal, with the goal of helping residents make
informed decisions at Town Meeting.

In May 2025, Town Meeting successfully adopted Article 23, a compliant Section 3A
zoning district plan at the Annual Town Meeting. However, in July 2025, a
referendum on the adopted Article was called under Chapter 405 of the Acts of 1954.
The Select Board, acting in accordance with the legislation, set a special election date
for July 8, and the resulting vote turned over the article approved at 2025 Town
Meeting. By July 14, the Town of Marblehead having no approved compliance plan,
failed to submit a district compliance application with the EOHLC by the regulatory
deadline and became considered non-compliant with Section 3A

Despite these efforts to engage and address concerns, the proposal faced significant
opposition from various segments of the Marblehead community. A key point of




                                         145
                                         149
                                        149
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


contention was the potential for increased density, which many residents feared
would alter the town’s character and quality of life. These concerns were particularly
pronounced in the proposed Tioga Way and Pleasant Street Districts.

The Town’s non-compliance status with Section 3A has created immediate and
significant repercussions for Marblehead’s ability to access state discretionary grant
funding, including revocation of funding and denial from grant programs the Town
had previously been awarded.

After the Town became considered non-compliant with Section 3A, the
Massachusetts Office of Travel & Tourism rescinded a Massachusetts Revolution 250
grant, originally awarded on July 2nd, 2025, for $50,000. The celebration, Sails &
Stories, was planned to recognize Marblehead’s crucial role in the American
Revolution and its identity as the “Birthplace of the American Navy.”

The Town was denied access to funding and support from programs the municipality
has historically relied upon. Notable shutouts included $519,193 in funding
supporting improvements to Town’s seawalls, operations funding for the Council on
Aging’s transportation programs, and funding for improvements to meet the Town’s
ADA compliance requirements. The Town’s recent application to continue design
work at State Street Landing and Tucker’s Wharf was denied by the State’s Coastal
Resilience Grant program, after five previous years securing over $1.1 million in
State provided funding. Additionally, the Council on Aging (COA) was advised not
to apply for the Community Transit Grant program due to the Town’s Section 3A
non-compliance, which had previously funded the purchase of three transportation
vans as well as operations funding for the COA’s senior transportation program. In
December, the Town was denied a $250,000 grant request for ADA improvements
for the Mary Alley Building from the State Office of Disability’s Municipal ADA
Improvement Grant program. The Town had previously received three awards from
the Municipal ADA Improvement Grant program, most recently in 2024 with the
award and implementation of the Devereux Beach ADA Improvements project.

The Town’s non-compliance status has been used to deny over $3.8 million in grant
proposals submitted through various grant programs listed in the Tables below.
Notable projects applied for included the Abbot Hall Attic and Accessibility
Improvement Project ($999,320), the Washington Street Corridor Rehabilitation
Project ($301,050), the Coffin School Reuse Study ($50,000), the Vacant Storefront
Program ($50,000), Downtown Parking Analysis Study ($30,000), the Marblehead
Rail Trail ($866,930), and the State Street Landing & Tucker Wharf Coastal
Resilience Project ($519,193).




                                        146
                                        150
                                       150
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



The Town’s continued non-compliance also puts at risk funding awards promised but
not yet disbursed. In October 2025, the Town applied for an $11.25 million federal
grant request to support the Marblehead Shipyards Resilience Project. As part of the
grant application, the department successfully secured a $1.125 million matching
fund commitment from the State’s Federal Funds & Infrastructure Office Matching
Funds program. If the Town was successful with the federal grant request, the Town
may not be able to access the State matching fund commitment due to Section 3A
non-compliance.

The loss of these critical funds has had far-reaching consequences for the Town. Not
only has it hindered the advancement of key projects, but it has also diminished
Marblehead’s ability to leverage equitable shares State funding resources designated
for all municipalities. As a result, the Town must continue to consider relying more
heavily on local funding or deferring these essential initiatives to future years. The
loss of these state grant funds due to Section 3A non-compliance represents a
significant setback in the town’s long-term planning and development efforts.

MARBLEHEAD SHIPYARDS RESILIENCY IMPROVEMENTS PROJECT
The Department continued to advance the Marblehead Municipal Shipyards
Infrastructure Improvements Project, a major resiliency and infrastructure initiative
focused on protecting and strengthening six Town-owned waterfront properties in the
Shipyard Neighborhood, including, Parker’s Boatyard, Cliff Street Boatyard,
Hammond Park, the Municipal Light Department, Commercial Street Pier, and the
Marblehead Yacht Club. The project is designed to address documented risks from
sea level rise, storm surge, and aging seawall infrastructure at these facilities that
support commercial fishing, marine trades, electric utility operations, and public
waterfront access, making them critical to Marblehead’s local economy and
community life. This project represents a strategic investment in climate resilience,
economic stability, public safety, and long-term preservation of Marblehead’s
historic working waterfront.

Proposed improvements developed with the community throughout 2022, 2023, and
2024 include reconstruction and elevation of deteriorating seawalls, elevation and
repaving of boatyard surfaces, installation of wave-attenuating floating docks,
stormwater management upgrades, and creation of a continuous ADA-accessible
waterfront pathway connecting key public sites. In 2025, the project reached 75%
design completion, secured key state and local environmental permitting approvals,
and advanced federal and state permitting coordination.




                                        147
                                        151
                                       151
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


In October 2025, the Town submitted an $11.25 million funding request to the U.S.
Department of Transportation’s Port Infrastructure Development Program to begin
construction phases of the project. The Department also secured a $1.125 million state
funding commitment toward required project matching funds. In 2024, the estimated
final design and construction costs were projected to be $13.8 million.

STATE STREET LANDING AND TUCKER’S WHARF RESILIENCY
IMPROVEMENTS PROJECT
In 2025, the Town advanced a critical coastal resilience planning initiative focused
on State Street Landing and the Harbormaster’s Office at Tucker’s Wharf, two Town-
owned waterfront assets central to harbor operations, the commercial fishing fleet,
recreational boating, and public access to Marblehead Harbor. The project was
supported by a $210,502 Massachusetts Coastal Zone Management (CZM) Coastal
Resilience Grant with $29,216 in in-kind Town staff matching support.

The site, identified as one of Marblehead Harbor’s most vulnerable flooding hot spots
underwent detailed, site-specific flood projections through 2070. Analysis confirmed
high exposure to sea level rise, storm surge, extreme precipitation, and heat. The
Town developed Design Flood Elevations consistent with state climate standards,
completed a waterfront infrastructure inspection, and prepared a near-term seawall
maintenance plan with cost estimates to stabilize critical assets while long-term
improvements are pursued. Building on earlier conceptual work, the project refined
resilience alternatives, including structural protection measures, nature-based
solutions, and phased adaptation pathways. It also produced conceptual design
drawings, cost estimates, and a permitting framework to guide future implementation.
Extensive stakeholder and public engagement ensured that resilience strategies reflect
operational needs, community values, and long-term fiscal responsibility. This work
positions Marblehead to continue to pursue implementation funding and advance
short and long-term capital improvements that will protect vital harbor infrastructure,
support the local maritime economy, and preserve public waterfront access in the face
of accelerating climate impacts.

In 2025, the department requested $519,193 for the FY 2026 CZM Coastal Resilience
Grant program to advance the State Street Landing and Tucker’s Wharf project to a
60% design competition stage but was denied due to the Town’s Section 3A non-
compliance status. The department plans to re-apply to continue design activities in
future grant opportunity rounds.

                                         152
                                       152

CLIFF STREET UNDERGROUND STORAGE TANK CLOSEOUT
PROJECT
In 2025, Collins Engineers, Inc. supported the Town of Marblehead in submitting a
Permanent Solution Statement and analyzing conditions of a past petroleum spill at
Cliff Street Boatyard (formerly Cloutman’s Boat Yard). As part of the Marblehead
Shipyards Resiliency Improvements project work, during historical file review and
project due diligence, the team identified an unresolved underground storage tank
(UST) release at Cliff Street Boatyard dating back to 1986. The release had never
been formally closed with the Massachusetts Department of Environmental
Protection (MassDEP) and remained listed as an active compliance matter. Cliff
Street Boatyard was historically utilized as a fuel depot dating back to 1928. The
historical on-Site fuel depot utilized petroleum USTs in its operations, including a
1,000-gallon gasoline UST installed circa 1970. Having been reportedly abandoned
for approximately ten years, the gasoline UST was removed from the Site in 1986.
Upon removal of the abandoned UST, an apparent gasoline release to the subsurface
was identified, and a subsequent subsurface investigation confirmed the presence of
elevated concentrations of petroleum hydrocarbons in soil samples.

To address this issue, Collins engaged The Vertex Companies, a Licensed Site
Professional (LSP), to evaluate the site conditions and determine the regulatory path
to resolution. Following coordination with MassDEP, the Town received a Notice of
Noncompliance (NON) requiring formal action under the Massachusetts Contingency
Plan, including submission of either a Permanent Solution Statement (PSS) or a Phase
I Initial Site Investigation (ISI) and Tier Classification. In January 2025, a subsurface
investigation was performed at the site to assess the reported subsurface impacts in
the vicinity of the 1,000-gallon gasoline UST that was removed in 1986. In April
2025, an additional subsurface investigation was carried out to delineate the nature
and extent of the soil impacts identified during the January 2025 investigation. As a
result, the soil impacts were discovered to be limited to an estimated 380 square feet
(0.01 acres) of asphalt-paved areas located between two on-site buildings occupied
by the Marblehead Yacht Club and the Marblehead Trading Co. Additionally, during
Vertex’s subsurface investigation performed in January 2025, three groundwater
monitoring wells were also installed at the site, which resulted in no identified
groundwater impacts exceeding accepted standards. Concentrations of petroleum
chemicals in soil have been reduced such that a condition of No Significant Risk
(NSR) has been achieved. On August 20th, 2025, the Town submitted its report to
demonstrate the condition of NSR to human health, safety, public health, and the
environment. The Town submitted a permanent solution statement, with no
conditions attached. Completion of this work will resolve the long-standing
compliance issue and update the site’s regulatory status in MassDEP’s database to




                                          149
                                          153
                                        153
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


reflect a clean standing, supporting both environmental stewardship and ongoing
resiliency improvements at the harbor.

$62,120 in ARPA funding was used to support the close out permitting of this project.
The Harbors and Waters Board provided an additional $16,650 in funding to support
additional drilling work required to complete the project.

VILLAGE STREET BRIDGE REPLACEMENT PROJECT
The Village Street Bridge (M-04-001) has been identified as structurally deficient
through MassDOT evaluations, with deterioration of the bridge deck, steel supports,
and drainage systems. The bridge currently has weight restrictions and limited
roadway width, constraining larger vehicles and affecting emergency response routes
between the east and west sides of Town. The Town Engineer and Department have
proposed a project that includes full replacement of the existing superstructure with
a precast concrete rigid frame designed for a 100-year service life. The new design
would provide two 11-foot travel lanes, 5-foot shoulders, 6-foot sidewalks on both
sides, ADA-compliant curb ramps, and a new accessible ramp connection to the
Marblehead Rail Trail. These improvements would enhance pedestrian and bicycle
safety, improve access to nearby schools, and eliminate current load limitations.

The Town has advanced the project through the MassDOT review process and
applied to the Boston Region MPO for Transportation Improvement Program (TIP)
construction funding. While the project has not yet been selected for construction
funding, the Town has continued to invest Chapter 90 funds towards the design of the
project to position the Town for future funding opportunities. Thank you to the Town
Engineers and the Department of Public Works for their continued efforts to advance
the design and improvements of this critical Town asset.




                                        150
                                        154
                                      154
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


ELM STREET PLAYGROUND PHASE I AND II




The Department partnered with the Friends of the Park on Elm Street to redevelop
the former Gerry School Playground area. The project was divided into two phases.
$223,490 in ARPA funding and $54,592 in a donation from the Friends of the Park
Elm Street supported the Phase I design and construction activities. Phase I involved
clearing the site and constructing a new open space grass area, walking path, an
improved public parking lot, and basketball and four-square court. During Phase I
construction, the contractor uncovered an abandoned cistern measuring 24 feet in
circumference and 24 feet deep. It is believed the cistern was used in the late 1800’s
for firefighting purposes in the neighborhood then known as “Back Street.” Large
granite blocks that had been meticulously placed over the cistern were repurposed
throughout the site as benches, steppingstones, and stairs. Following the completion
of Phase I, the Town began Phase II, which includes the final design of the playground
area and the procurement of a contractor to install new playground equipment. A
generous donation of $433,000 from the Friends of the Park on Elm Street funded the
Phase II construction activities. Phase II construction is expected to begin in Spring
2026. Thank you to the Friends of the Park on Elm Street for their continued
community support. Thank you to Alex Eitler, Town Planner, for his work managing
the project.

DEVEREUX BEACH ADA IMPROVEMENTS PROJECT




In February of 2025, the Town was awarded $92,000 by the State’s Municipal ADA
Grant. In February 2025, the Town was awarded $92,000 through the
Commonwealth’s Municipal ADA Improvement Grant Program to advance
accessibility improvements at Devereux Beach. The project focuses on upgrading
accessible parking areas, improving walkway surfaces using Mobi-Mat products,
enhancing signage, new Mobi-Chair equipment and implementing restroom
improvements including regrading, ramp installation, and fixture height adjustments.
Devereux Beach serves approximately 20,000–30,000 visitors per month during peak
season and is a premier waterfront destination for residents and visitors alike. These
improvements will provide safer, ADA-compliant access to parking areas, restrooms,
playground facilities, and the shoreline, ensuring that individuals with disabilities can
access the beach with greater independence and dignity. Thank you to the Recreation
& Parks Department and the Marblehead Disabilities Commission for their
collaboration in developing the grant proposal and supporting project
implementation.

STATE STREET LANDING RESTROOMS HEATING, 2024 TOWN
MEETING ARTICLE 51




The Community Development and Planning department lead the installation of an
air-source heat pump to enable access to the State Street Landing Restrooms year-
round. The Harbors & Waters Board and the Select Board worked to devise an
updated operating plan to enable year-round operation, including the installation of a
heat pump heating system to support winter operations. Finally, the Town has also
collaborated with local leader Phil Blaisdell and the Friends of State Street Landing
to assist the Town with additional monitor and future improvements. Future
improvements to ensure longevity of the property include sealing on the doors and
improved insulation for the building.

COFFIN SCHOOL ADAPTATIVE REUSE
At the May 2025 Town Meeting, voters approved the transfer of the former Coffin
Elementary School to the Select Board. In the fall of 2025, the Department issued an
Expression of Interest (EOI) to municipal departments, seeking potential reuse
options for the building. In addition to the EOI, a Request for Information (RFI) was
sent to developers to solicit proposals for redeveloping the site. The Department
received five responses to the EOI, which included ideas for affordable housing, a
dog park, a cemetery, and temporary boat storage. One RFI response focused on
redeveloping the site for affordable housing. The Department held two community
meetings, one with direct abutters and neighbors, and a public listening session open
to all residents. The meetings were well attended and solicited feedback from
neighbors and public. Housing was identified as a priority, with focus on affordability
for seniors, veterans, and workforce housing for Marblehead residents. In December,




                                         153
                                         157
                                       157
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


the town appropriated $5,950 to conduct a Hazardous Building Materials
Investigation to assess asbestos and lead-based paint risks in the building.

ACCELERATING CLIMATE RESILIENCY PROJECT
In September 2025, the Town of Marblehead, in partnership with the Marblehead
Housing Authority and Sustainable Marblehead, was awarded an $80,000 grant
through the Metropolitan Area Planning Council’s (MAPC) Accelerating Climate
Resilience Program to advance Resilient by Design: Cooling Marblehead’s Housing
for the Future. The initiative will focus on reducing extreme heat impacts at four
public housing sites, New Farrell Court, Powderhouse Court, Green Street, and
Barnard Hawkes Court, through nature-based design strategies and community-
centered planning. The project prioritizes elderly, low-income, and disabled residents
who are disproportionately vulnerable to extreme heat. Potential improvements
include expanded tree canopy, green infrastructure, permeable surfaces, and
enhanced shaded gathering areas designed to reduce the urban heat island effect while
strengthening social connections among residents. Project objectives include
conducting a robust community engagement process, completing site assessments
and conceptual landscape designs at all four housing locations, and developing
implementation-ready plans to pursue future construction funding. The Town’s Grant
Coordinator and Sustainability Coordinator, along with Marblehead Housing
Authority staff, are contributing in-kind support for project coordination and
outreach. As an ACR grantee, the project team will also participate in MAPC’s
regional Resilience Community of Practice to share best practices and advance
climate equity goals. The project is set to kick off and engage the public in 2026.

FIVE CORNERS INTERSECTION REDESIGN PROJECT
In May 2025, the Town was awarded $135,000 from the State’s MassWorks Program
to fund improvements at the Five Corners intersection. Additionally, a local match of
$15,000 will be provided through Article 11 of the 2022 Town Meeting. This project
aims to enhance traffic flow, improve safety, and upgrade sidewalks and pedestrian
infrastructure to ensure full ADA compliance. Design and public outreach are set to
begin in spring 2026. Thank you to the Town Engineer and the Department of Public
Works for their continued dedication and collaboration in advancing the design and
improvements for this important intersection in the heart of the town’s business
district.

MARBLEHEAD RAIL TRAIL DESIGN IMPROVEMENTS PROJECT
The Town has made significant strides in advancing the Marblehead Rail Trail, a
transformative project that will enhance connectivity and recreational opportunities
for residents. The design is progressing on two key rail trail branches – the




                                        154
                                        158
                                       158
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Swampscott Branch and the Salem Branch, both of which will offer distinct and
valuable access points to the community. The Swampscott Branch extends from
Smith Street to Seaview Avenue, at the boundary between Marblehead and
Swampscott, while the Salem Branch runs from West Shore Drive to Lead Mills.
Together, these segments will create a network that links neighborhoods and provides
much-needed access to natural areas, local amenities, and transportation routes. The
trail’s design, led by Toole Design, has reached a major milestone with the
completion of the 25% design phase this calendar year.

The proposed 10-foot-wide path with 2-foot shoulders will improve neighborhood
connections and enhance access points for Marblehead residents. In addition,
expanding mobility options, the design includes key site improvements such as
enhanced trail crossings, benches, trail markers, signage, and landscaping. These
amenities will ensure that the rail trail is not only functional but also welcoming and
accessible, fostering a sense of community while promoting healthy outdoor
activities.

Beyond the design efforts on the Swampscott and Salem Branches, the Town has
secured crucial funding for the Wye Junction segment of the Rail Trail. This segment,
which spans Bessom Street to Pleasant Street and from Bessom Street to West Shore
Drive, will provide a critical link in the overall rail trail network. With $140,000
federal earmark and an additional $35,000 from MassDOT, the design phase for the
Wye Junction segment is set to begin in 2026. This new section will further enhance
the benefits of the rail trail, creating even more opportunities for residents to explore
Marblehead by foot, bike, or other non-motorized means.

                                          159
                                        159
