MARBLEHEAD RAIL TRAIL SHOWCASE PROJECT




The Pleasant Street to Smith Street Rail Trail Showcase Project adds a newly
constructed 650-foot section of the Marblehead Rail Trail in a highly visible part of
town. As part of the construction project, the trail was widened to 11 feet where
possible and now provides a two-foot section of grass on each side of the trail for
increased visibility and safe passing. The project also implemented green stormwater
infrastructure and grading to reduce water pooling on the trail and removed tripping
hazards and built upon the pedestrian crossing improvements that were completed at
Pleasant and Smith Streets in December 2022.

Construction was completed by Raffaele Construction Corp of Swampscott. The total
project cost was $143,395, which was made possible through the support of state
grant programs and federal funding, including a MassTrails grant in the amount of
$60,000 and residual support from American Rescue Plan (ARPA) funding. Designed
and built in accordance with the Town’s Rail Trail Master Plan, this project
demonstrates what a safe, accessible, and connected trail system can look like in
Marblehead. With the completion of the project, the trail now improves the safety and
accessibility for residents and community members of all ages and abilities. The trail
also helps promote and encourage participation in active transportation, such as
walking and biking. The Showcase Project represents an important step forward in
Marblehead’s vision for a fully improved Rail Trail. It highlights the community’s
commitment to the Complete Streets Policy and Rail Trail Master Plan, ensuring that
people walking, biking, and rolling are supported with modern, safe, and inviting
infrastructure. The Rail Trail connects residents to schools, conservation areas, civic




                                         156
                                         160
                                       160
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


spaces, and downtown Marblehead, making it both a recreational asset and a
sustainable transportation option.

MARBLEHEAD RAIL TRAIL CLIFTON/ROCKAWAY AVENUE
PROJECT
The Clifton/Rockaway Avenue Rail Trail project adds a 475-foot section of the
Marblehead Rail Trail along the Swampscott Branch. This section, identified in the
2020 Marblehead Rail Trail plan for improvements, includes two street crossings and
retains granite from the former Clifton Train Depot. The department worked with the
Historical Commission and local train enthusiasts to preserve part of the depot’s
granite, which led to a redesign of the trail alignment. Today, a section of the original
granite remains. The project also widened the trail to 11 feet where possible, realigned
crosswalks, added a raised crosswalk at Rockaway Avenue, and included new
landscaping, green stormwater infrastructure, bike racks, benches, and signage. The
crossings included 85 feet of newly paved trail. Constructed by Raffaele Construction
Corp of Swampscott, the project was designed and built in accordance with the
Town’s Rail Trail Master Plan, following the same standards as the Showcase project
to ensure a safe and accessible trail. The total project cost was $396,335, which was
made possible through the support from American Rescue Plan (ARPA) funding.

MARBLEHEAD 2037: MASTER PLAN
In 2025, the Town Planner and department embarked on a crucial initiative to start
the development of the Marblehead 2037 Comprehensive Master Plan. This plan will
serve as the strategic roadmap for the town’s growth and redevelopment over the next
two decades, ensuring that Marblehead maintains its unique character while planning
for necessary changes. With the last comprehensive plan dating back to the 1970’s
and only updated in 1989, this new Master Plan is long overdue. The town’s evolving
needs and the rapidly changing landscape of our community demand a thoughtful,
well-structured framework for the future. The Marblehead 2037 Master Plan will
provide clear guidance on how to manage growth, preserve valuable resources, and
enhance the quality of life for residents. The Master Plan will focus on seven key
areas: Land Use, Housing, Economic Opportunities, Natural and Cultural Resources,
Open Space and Recreation, Services and Facilities, and Transportation and Mobility.
These areas will guide the town’s future planning, ensuring that growth and
development are thoughtfully integrated, sustainable, and beneficial for the entire
community.

The process of creating a Master Plan will be a community-driven effect, prioritizing
broad public engagement to ensure that it reflects the value and priorities of our
residents. A Master Plan Advisory Committee was formed, bringing together a




                                          157
                                          161
                                        161
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


diverse group of stakeholders, including members from town boards and departments
such as the Planning Board, Select Board, Conservation Commission, DPW, Schools,
Housing, as well as business owners, residents, and youth representatives. This
collaborative approach will ensure the plan is comprehensive and inclusive,
addressing the needs of all sectors of the community. It’s anticipated to be an 18-24
month long process and lead to the creation of a vision that positions Marblehead for
continued success, maintaining the town’s charm while addressing the evolving needs
of residents. The completion of this plan will be a pivotal moment for Marblehead,
enabling the town to approach future challenges with a clear, strategic framework that
aligns with the community’s aspirations. As the years come, the Master Plan will be
instrumental to help guide the decisions that will shape Marblehead’s future. The
Master Plan will equip the town with a clear framework to guide growth, preserve
our unique character, and help position Marblehead for continued success and vitality
for generations to come.

TOWN-WIDE BICYCLE FACILITIES PLAN
The Town of Marblehead commissioned Toole Design Group, LLC to develop a
town-wide Bicycle Facilities Plan through 2024 and 2025. The objective of the Bike
Facility Plan is to provide the framework for a network of high-comfort bike facilities
(of all types and based on context) that are safe, convenient, attractive, and accessible
to residents and visitors of all ages and abilities. This framework will allow the Town
to guide future investments in an efficient way and consider other Town priorities in
combination with bicycle infrastructure improvements. Three goals, developed
through public input and Town guidance, will help to work toward this objective:
     1. Make bicycling a safe, efficient, and practical option for all residents and
          visitors, with specific focus on the safety of children biking to school.
     2. Expand the bike network in Town to increase safety, reduce traffic, improve
          public health, and raise awareness for people bicycling, walking, and rolling.
     3. Incorporate safety and bike planning into Town policies.

There were several community engagement and feedback sessions held through 2024.
In February of 2025, the final draft of the bicycle facilities plan was presented to the
Traffic Safety Advisory Committee. The department hopes to present the final plan
to the Select Board in 2026.

MARBLEHEAD SUSTAINABLE HERITAGE PROJECT
The Department advanced the Marblehead Sustainable Heritage Project to work to
align the Town’s historic preservation framework with its commitment to achieve
100% carbon-free energy by 2040. Understanding that residential buildings account
for 44.5% of the Town’s greenhouse gas emissions, and many fall within the




                                          158
                                          162
                                        162
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


jurisdiction of the Old and Historic Districts Commission (OHDC), the Green
Marblehead Implementation Committee and the Old and Historic Districts
Commission met to discuss potential solutions. In partnership with the Metropolitan
Area Planning Council (MAPC) and regional preservation and clean energy experts,
the Town developed proposed updates to OHDC guidelines to reduce regulatory
barriers while maintaining the character-defining features of Marblehead’s historic
districts. The project combined comparative research, national best practice review,
peer community analysis (Salem, Nantucket, and Concord), local walking tours,
expert consultation, and a community charrette. Public engagement revealed strong
support for clearer, more consistent standards and greater transparency in the review
process.

The project’s objective was to provide a balanced approach that integrates
environmental sustainability with the preservation of the town’s architectural
heritage. The resulting proposed recommendations provide a potential path forward
on guidance that allows for air source heat pumps, electric vehicle charging
equipment, renewable energy generation, and energy-efficient windows under
defined conditions that protect historic character. By working proactively to
modernize its guidelines and strengthening alignment with state and national
preservation standards, Marblehead can position itself as a model community
demonstrating that historic districts can meaningfully maintain community character
while allowing homeowner’s choice and the ability to meet the larger Town
sustainability goals. Community members were engaged throughout the process,
ensuring that local perspectives helped shape the final guideline
recommendations. The full report is available at https://www.mapc.org/resource-
library/marblehead-sustainable-heritage/

TOWN-WIDE MUNICIPAL FIBER INSTALLATION – PHASE II
In May 2025, the Town was awarded $89,792 from the State’s Community Compact
Municipal Fiber program for Phase II of the Town-wide Municipal Fiber project. The
project, originally identified as a priority in the 2022 Marblehead ARPA Spending
Prioritization Plan, seeks to provide a complete fiber optic loop that would reliably
connect all municipal, school and municipal light facilities as well as other utility sites
and major intersections to allow for enhanced network capabilities. In 2024, the Town
began working with Comm-Tract Corporation to design and procure a fiber “loop”
that encompasses a majority of the Town’s physical assets using $500,000 in
distributed ARPA funds. Weighing the cost of the additional cabling to the facilities
that would be served, it was decided that the Marblehead Neck would not be included
in the first phase of the project. Phase II of this project is preparing to complete the
Town network by adding a fiber extension to the Marblehead Neck. The Neck is home




                                           159
                                           163
                                         163
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


to over eight hundred residents and has 2 municipal facilities, including a public park
and sewage pumping station.

The Town sought support from the Commonwealth’s Municipal Fiber program to
complete the Town-wide municipal fiber network that is becoming the
communications backbone of the community. The Town is committed to providing a
minimum of five percent of the project cost, $4,870 in a combination of local
matching funds and in-kind support. The project is anticipated to be implemented in
2026.

GREEN COMMUNITIES PROGRAM
The Sustainability Coordinator in coordination with the Green Marblehead
Implementation Committee advanced the Town’s application to become a designated
participant in the Massachusetts Department of Energy Resources (DOER) Green
Communities Program. This designation will make the Town eligible for state grant
funding to support energy efficiency improvements, long-term reductions in
municipal energy costs, and accomplish a key goal set out in the Marblehead Net Zero
Roadmap. To qualify, municipalities must meet five State established criteria. The
Town has successfully completed Criteria 1, 2, 3, and 5. These include allowing as-
of-right zoning for renewable and alternative energy facilities; having a reasonable
permitting process for qualifying energy projects; adopting a municipal fuel-efficient
vehicle policy; and having adopted the Stretch Building Code.

Work is ongoing to complete Criterion 4, necessary for full designation. The Town is
in the process of formally adopting a five-year Energy Reduction Plan designed to
help reduce municipal energy consumption by 20 percent. This plan outlines targeted
energy conservation measures across municipal buildings, vehicles, as well as water
and sewer operations. The Select Board will consider adoption of an Energy
Reduction Plan in early 2026.

Because the Town is served by the Marblehead Municipal Light Department,
designation also requires adoption of the Renewable Energy Charge. The Town will
need to secure approval for the Marblehead Light Board to request the
implementation of a Renewable Energy Charge to satisfy program requirements and
enable eligibility for grant funding.

Once all remaining criteria are met and the application is approved, the Town will be
positioned to receive grant funding to implement energy efficiency and renewable
energy projects that reduce operating costs, lower greenhouse gas emissions, and
advance local sustainability goals. Upon acceptance into the program, the Town is




                                         160
                                         164
                                       164
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


expected to receive $150,000 in the form of a designation grant. The Town would
further become eligible for the Green Communities Competitive Grant program,
which awards up to $500,000 per round, to advance energy efficiency and
decarbonization project across municipal buildings.

MESSAGE FROM THE DIRECTOR
As newly appointed Director of Community Development & Planning, I am proud to
lead a department that is already making meaningful contributions to the growth and
sustainability of Marblehead. In 2025, our newly established department focused on
laying the groundwork for a balanced and sustainable future, preserving our town’s
historic character while accommodating future needs.

One of the department’s key achievements this year was the advancement of the
Section 3A Zoning District initiative. After extensive public engagement, the zoning
district was adopted at the May 2025 Town Meeting. Unfortunately, the outcome of
a special election in July placed the town in noncompliance with the state’s zoning
mandates. This development has impacted several critical projects, but we remain
committed to working with the community to find solutions that align with both state
requirements and the values of Marblehead residents.

Our sustainability efforts, led by Logan Casey, have made significant progress,
particularly through Marblehead’s Net Zero initiatives. With Logan’s leadership,
we’ve worked across departments to plan to reduce energy consumption and increase
the town’s resilience to climate change.

I also want to acknowledge the invaluable contributions of Donna Cotterell, our Grant
Coordinator Donna has played an essential role in advancing several important
projects, including managing the Devereux Beach ADA Improvements Project and
supporting funding efforts for various climate resilience initiatives. Her expertise and




                                         164
                                         168
                                        168
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


diligence have been instrumental in advancing these initiatives and enhancing
Marblehead’s infrastructure.

I would also like to extend my deepest appreciation to Lisa Lyons, our Senior Clerk,
Lisa’s exceptional management and administrative duties for the regulatory boards
have been nothing short of outstanding. She has been the backbone of our department,
ensuring that all applications are processed efficiently, deadlines are met, and the
boards are fully supported in their decision-making. Lisa’s ability to stay organized
and navigate complex regulatory matters with ease has allowed the department to
function seamlessly, and her dedication to the town has been a key factor in our
success. Her contributions are invaluable, and I am truly grateful for her hard work.

A special thank you goes to Alex Eitler, Town Planner, who departed in December
2025. Alex’s leadership was integral in advancing critical zoning and planning
efforts, and his contributions will be missed. We are grateful for his dedication to
Marblehead’s growth and preservation.

Looking ahead, I am optimistic about the continued success of our department as we
navigate the challenges of balancing growth and preservation. We will continue to
focus on thoughtful, sustainable development, ensuring Marblehead remains a
wonderful place to live, work, and thrive for future generations.

Respectfully submitted,

Brendan Callahan
Director of Community Development & Planning

                    CONSERVATION COMMISSION

This report is for Calendar Year 2025. The purpose of the Marblehead Conservation
Commission is twofold. First, to implement the safeguards specified in the state
Wetlands Protection Act, the state Stormwater Management Policy and the
Marblehead Wetlands Protection Bylaw in order to help preserve and protect the
wetlands and water resources in our Town. Second, to maintain and protect the
conservation lands held in trust for all residents of our Town. Of the six communities
adjacent to Salem Sound, Marblehead and Salem have the highest density of
population, with corresponding pressures on our open space and water resources. The
quality of life in Marblehead and its attractiveness is in part dependent on these
resources, and we urge all Marblehead residents to join in our efforts to preserve and
protect them.

The Commission held regular meetings and public hearings throughout the year 2025.
We continued to assist residents in their construction projects while guiding them in
their endeavors to protect the wetland resources potentially affected by their
construction. During 2025, the Commission received and processed the following:

Notices of Intent                    19
Certificates of Compliance           19
Orders of Conditions                 17
Requests for Determinations          1
Minor Activity Permits               19
Enforcement Orders                   4
Emergency Certificates               0
Extension requests                   20
Minor Modification requests          3

A major ongoing effort of the Commission is the management and mitigation of
pollution caused by storm water runoff. This polluting runoff comes from streets,
parking lots, driveways and lawns. Lawn chemicals, oil and coolant leaks from
vehicles, animal waste, paints disposed of in catch basins (storms drains in our streets)
- all these pollutants/nutrients eventually end up in our streams and ocean. The
Marblehead Water & Sewer Department already follows the guidelines established
by the state to mitigate stormwater pollution runoff into our ocean waters. However,
the effort to mitigate this pollution will require a long-term change in the thinking
and actions on the part of the general public and in our approach to public
infrastructure, including the Historical Commission approving.




                                           166
                                           170
                                          170
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



The Commission reminds everyone that our ponds are all in the process of filling in
with vegetation. This is a natural process, especially with shallow ponds, and is
caused or accelerated, in part, by the inflow of chemicals and fertilizers from abutting
lawns and storm water runoff. If you live directly on one of our ponds, please do not
fertilize your lawn.

Marblehead has exhausted (read developed) more than 95% of its buildable land.
With all the roofs, driveways, streets, parking areas and other impervious surfaces we
have built ourselves into one huge catch basin. This has exacerbated our drains
problems. All those little open fields, land depressions and pocket wetlands where
storm water used to be able to collect and gently seep into the ground are, for the most
part, gone. The Commission passed tree replacement regulations in 2023 to encourage
planting of additional tree canopy and to discourage removal of healthy trees. The
Commission recommends that other departments and the Select Board follow suit to
promote a healthy tree canopy in our Town.
                                                                                       .
Again, this year, we wish to express our gratitude to the Marblehead Conservancy for
its continuing efforts to restore, improve and expand walking paths in our
conservation lands and for its ongoing program to remove invasive plant species,
which are strangling native vegetation in the conservation lands. The ongoing efforts
of the Conservancy to preserve and protect these very valuable resources are crucial
to the health of these areas, but the Commission will need to start requesting funding
to better maintain these important resources available for every resident and visitor
to enjoy. The Commission encourages you to explore the many walking trails in
Marblehead maintained by the Conservancy. Trail maps are available on the
Conservancy's web site.

Please visit us on the Town web site, www.marbleheadma.gov, to find information
on our meeting schedules, help with filing permit applications, and accessing the
minutes of our meetings.


Respectfully submitted, Marblehead Conservation Commission
David VanHoven, Chair

                           COUNCIL ON AGING

Many ask us, what does the COA do for the town of Marblehead. The COA is a
municipal agency, a center which oversees the programming and services supporting
older adults in Marblehead. We provide local outreach, social and health services,
advocacy, information, and assistance for older adults, their families and caregivers.
We are supported by a volunteer network, we offer meals, transportation, fitness and
recreational programming, wellness activities, lifelong learning and more to MHD
residents age 55 and older. (https://www.mass.gov/info-details/aging-services-
network#councils-on-aging-coa-and-senior-centers)

The Town of Marblehead funds approximately seventy-six percent (76%) of COA
salaries and thirty-two (32%) of COA expenses. Additional grants and private
donations are received by The Executive Office of Aging & Independence (EOAI),
(formerly the Executive Office of Elder Affairs (EOEA) State’s Formula grant, the
Friends of the Council on Aging (FCOA), the Harold and Elizabeth Shattuck
Memorial Fund (Shattuck Fund), the Marblehead Female Humane Society (MFHS),
the Massachusetts Council on Aging (MCOA), Mass. Dept of Transportation
(MassDOT), the Federal Older Americans Act of 1965 Titles IIIB & IIIC, and private
donations fund the remaining salaries and expenses.

In 2025, the EOAI increased funding from $15/pp to $16/pp for residents age 60+
based on the 2020 census and the MFHS provided $42,000 in direct support of the
COA’s Transportation program.

The Federal Older American’s Act of 1965 granted $9,000 in Title IIIB funding for
the COA’s Transportation program and $14,000 in Title IIIC funding towards the
COA’s nutrition program. Amounts fluctuate annually and are utilized for salaries.

The FCOA provided $38,000 in direct funding to the COA for several programs and
fitness equipment. An additional $8,000 was provided to purchase two round metal
picnic tables, chairs, and umbrellas for Bibbo Park.

The Shattuck Fund generously awarded $35,000 to help replace the COA Fitness
Center equipment and an additional $66,490 towards the completion of Bibbo’s Park.
This funding provided a Founder’s Boardwalk and fencing to secure the Park.

Perhaps the most significant celebratory event hosted this year was the dedication of
BIBBO PARK, in honor of Pat Bibbo. A formal dedication ceremony was held in




                                        168
                                        172
                                       172
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


August 2025 with more than 180 people in attendance. The event was sponsored by
the FCOA and several members of the community participated: Lisa Hooper, COA
Director, Rossana Ferrente, Chair, Recreations & Parks Dept, Janine Glabicky and
John Capellotti both personal friends of Pat Bibbo and Liz Michaud. State
Representative Jenny Armini and Senator Brendon Creighton honored both Liz
Michaud and Pat Bibbo with House of Representatives and State Senate Citations
recognizing their commitment and leadership to Marblehead.

Community Education: In 2025, 1027 people attended a total of 290 educational
events. These included weekly sessions of Coffee with a Cop, and monthly sessions
with State Rep Jenny Armini and iPad/iPhone training by KevTech. Programs offered
were “Rediscovering the MBTA” with MBTA representatives discussing the latest
updates, and participants applied for Charlie Cards. Peter Bowen presented the 2026
Medicare plan changes, and The Curley Law Firm educated participants about Elder
Law. Essex County Registry of Deeds informed 46 participants about the Homestead
Act and provided each of them with a copy of his/her deed. Eric Knight, retired IT
Specialist/Operations Mgr. at the Mass. Office of the Inspector General, visited twice
and spoke to more than 40 people about Identity Theft and provided an overview of
Cybersecurity. A program on the Jeremiah Lee Mansion with Lauren McCormack
was presented along with, the Life of Amelia Earhart with Margaret Arnold, PhD.
Town Administrator Thatcher Kezer and Town Moderator Jack Attridge hosted their
annual Town Meeting Q&A session. Matt Martin of the National Grand Bank spoke
about Scams and a discussion about Slavery in New England by local historian,
Joseph DiPioli was given.

A monthly Veterans Coffee Hour was added. This is an informational collaborative
program with MHD Veterans Services Agent Roseann, Trionfi-Mazzuchelli and the
COA. Attendance ranges from 12–26 Veterans per session.

Health/Wellness: Weekly Blood Pressure Clinics run in partnership with the Dept of
Public Health Nurse and COA Volunteer Nurses. A total of 48 blood pressure clinics
were held during 2025. A flu clinic held on October 8th provided high dose flu
vaccines to 92 seniors. Bi-monthly foot care appts were offered with foot care nurse
Cheryl Wall, RN, BSN, DNS, CFCN, CWOCN completing 117 appointments.

A big thank you to the Parkinsons Fitness Foundation for sponsoring a “free”, bi-
weekly Parkinsons Fitness Class offered at the COA, and to Gail Perry-Borden for
offering a monthly support group.

Fitness/Exercise: Through the overwhelming generosity of the Shattuck Fund, our
Fitness Center equipment was replaced. The Center consists of three treadmills, two
elliptical bikes, a recumbent bike and a cross trainer. Partakers enjoyed the Fitness
Center more than 1,287 times this year. Additionally, 726 COA members participated
in 18,834 Fitness classes. This is a 16% increase from 2024.

There is opportunity for all ability levels in our Fitness programs, including Balance
& Mobility, Chair Yoga, Line Dancing, Muscle Conditioning, Osteo Prevention,
Parkinsons Fitness, Strength & Stretch, Step It Up, Strong Bones/Strong Bodies,
Weight Training, Yoga, and Zumba Gold. Our instructors include Certified Personal
Trainers and Physical Therapists.

Health Insurance Counseling: The Medicare “SHINE program” (Serving the
Health Insurance Needs of Everyone) is a federally funded program, and volunteers
are trained in Medicare health insurance as well as prescription coverage. SHINE
counselors Peter Bowen, Lu-Ann Gabel, and Deb Ventresca met with a total of 587
seniors in 2025.

Intergenerational: The COA continues its relationship with MHS students,
including the afterschool MHS Film Club. The season was hosted by MHS Juniors
Matias, Phineas, and Quin at the High School. Transportation to MHS was provided
by the COA and the students welcomed their guests in the lobby and escorted them
to the conference room. The season kicked off in December with It's A Wonderful
Life and continued through April showing films such as La La Land, 12 Angry Men,
The Truman Show, Fantastic Mr. Fox, and Jurassic Park, to name a few. A core group
of eight COA members were joined by several students. Each meeting concluded
with students leading an engaging, intergenerational discussion about the plot,
characters, and special effects of the film sharing interesting ideas and perspectives.

MHS students who began as a scrapbooking club in 2024, quickly morphed into visits
for “friendly competition” in Chair Volleyball and Indoor Curling with Pat Bibbo and
his team-mates, as well as participating in our Zumba dance class with Instructor Kim
Crowley. All three activities are thriving today. MHS Ninth Grade Volunteer Day
was held on October 22. Approximately fifteen freshmen assisted the FCOA with
their annual fundraising campaign by labeling over 6,000 envelopes.

Nutrition: AgeSpan’s Grab-n-Go and Traveling Chef programs provided 6,752
meals to 281 seniors. Grab-n-Go meals are offered three days a week and are collected
at the COA. The monthly Traveling Chef program is a special sit-down luncheon.
This year 3,926 meals were prepared and served to 407 seniors at our Tuesday lunch

and special events such as our annual St. Patrick’s Day & Thanksgiving luncheons as
well as our bi-annual Veterans breakfasts. A new monthly pop-up lunch program
called the Doug & Mike Road Show was designed in 2025 and has received great
reviews. Louise Moore and the MHD Masons support the annual Thanksgiving
luncheon event and bi-annual Veterans breakfasts. The FCOA supports all the other
programs listed in this paragraph.

The Marblehead Rotary Club generously donated funding to provide a special lobster
roll lunch for 75 Meals On Wheels participants. Meals were delivered by members of
the COA Board and Rotary members. The COA provides weekly transportation to
the MHD Food Pantry, Market Basket, Crosbys, and to the COA for lunch.

Outreach: The Outreach Coordinator (OC) receives referrals from various town
offices: the Assessor’s Office, Marblehead Police Department (MPD), Marblehead
Fire Department (MFD), Marblehead Counseling Center (MCC), Jail Diversion
Clinician at MPD, Marblehead Housing Authority (MHA), Town Clerk, Veterans
Office, Animal Control Officer and Board of Health. In addition, the (OC) continues
to work collaboratively with many local medical offices as well as our regional Aging
Service Access Point (ASAP), AGESPAN. The (OC) and MFD have established
mutual respect and alliance over the last five years. Due to state and federal budget
cuts, the Home Safety Team applied again for, and was awarded, $3,000 from the
MFHS. This permitted MFD to purchase equipment (smoke detectors, lockboxes,
C02 detectors) to be used in seniors’ homes. We also provided helpful safety
information and education. This year over 35 Home evaluations were performed.

The (OC) receives and responds to a steady influx of calls from residents, and others,
seeking information or assistance on a variety of subject matters. Many calls begin as
information gatherings but conclude in an appointment for a MassHealth application,
Public Housing, SNAP, Fuel Assistance, and similar types of concrete support
services. The (OC) recorded 46 of these types of applications. Calls requesting
information or assistance ranged from 81-244 monthly for an annual total of 1,839.

While many of these issues can be resolved over the phone; others may result in an
office/home visit and possible ongoing case management. Case management is a
client-centered process where a social worker assesses needs, plans, coordinates,
advocates for and monitors a package of services to help the person navigate complex
issues or systems with the goal of improved well-being and achievement of goals.
The (OC) maintained a case load of 85 clients this year.




                                        171
                                        175
                                       175
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


The (OC) makes home visits and schedules office visits for elders in the community.
It cannot be stated enough that a home visit is a great assessment tool to evaluate how
a person manages in their environment. Discussions are often centered around
Behavioral Health issues, Dementia-related questions, Caregiver Support, Adult Day
health referrals, isolation, & loneliness. The (OC) conducted 411 home, 622 office
visits and delivered holiday goody bags and wreaths to 40 seniors.

To combat social isolation, collaboration was established with other agencies and
businesses to ensure our population can do something special and enjoyable. Through
a generous donation from the late Pru Garniss’ sisters, we were able to finance a trip
to Woodman’s of Essex in May for “Lunch on Pru” for 40 people. This event was a
huge hit and brought together a handful of people who had not seen each other since
high school. Transportation was provided by MHD Public Schools. 15 seniors
enjoyed lunch at The Essex Tech Maple Street Bistro. In August, through the
generosity of the Garniss sisters and the FCOA, the annual trip to Boston via Ferry
with lunch and a Trolley Tour was financed for 50 people.

The COA also offers movies twice per month which gets people out of the house to
socialize have a snack and schmooze about the movie afterwards. The (OC) and
Program Manager (PM) rely on the movie suggestion box that is filled monthly by
seniors. Movie afternoons attract 8-10 people.

The (OC) partnered with artist Siobhan McDonald, a certified Dementia Practitioner
and OMA Facilitator with the intent of bringing the “Opening Minds Through Art”
program to the COA and MHS. This award-winning program was developed at the
Scripps Gerontology Center at Miami University in Ohio. It is an evidenced-based
intergenerational art making program designed to provide self-expression and social
engagement for people living with dementia and memory loss. Each older adult is
paired with a trained student volunteer who provides guidance through the art-making
process. Siobhan and the (OC) recruited the students and provided training over a 4-
week period. We had a core group of 12 seniors and 15 students. This six-week
program culminated with an art show and was featured in local newspapers and
MHTV. It is really something to watch when a 76-year-old and a teenager bond over
their shared love of rock and roll bands. The students and seniors highly anticipated
coming weekly. Some of the students’ parents also came to the art show to meet the
“artists”. This program permitted caregivers to take 2 hours each week for
themselves. MHS has shown willingness to work with us on a second program in
2026 and we are fortunate to have the support of the MFHS for this program.

The (OC) attended 40 meetings this year. Among them were: Agespan’s Farmers
Market Coupons Training, MFHS, MPD and detective Theresa Gay concerning
specific cases, OMA grant writing, presentations and trainings, Attorney Pat Lausier
with (OC) and a few of her clients, Housing Authority and Lafayette Nursing Home
Meetings.

The (OC) facilitated and attended 10 presentations at the COA. “Join My Journey” a
look at Dementia through the eyes of the patient co-hosted with The Mariner and 25
people attending. MBTA Charlie Card training and sign-up was very well attended.
Other presentations were attended by the (OC), mostly surrounding the topics of
homelessness, housing and lack thereof and Behavior Health Webinars. The (OC)
presently has five homeless senior citizens on her caseload. The (OC) attends the
monthly Veterans Coffee for referrals or assistance.

Through the generosity of “Making Ends Meet”, the (OC) was given the opportunity
to deliver gift certificates from Crosbys to 50 people/families for Thanksgiving and
an additional delivery of coupons for 100 people for the December Holidays. The
(OC) and (PM) volunteer annually at Wreaths Across America in our local
cemeteries.

Recreation: Our very popular Recreation programming provides a welcoming
setting. This year 929 people participated in a total of 6,779 recreational events that
included Drop in Bridge, Canasta, Cribbage, Knitting, Mah Jong and Quilting. Our
Knitters Holiday Boutique generously donated all their proceeds to the FCOA. Bocce,
Chair Volleyball & Indoor Curling once again surpassed expectations. Bocce’s
members increased to 86, and our Travel team hosted several players from Beverly,
Peabody and Wenham. The COA hosts Anchor to Windward participants who enjoy
weekly Bocce games, and friendly competitions with our members. Indoor Curling
and Chair Volleyball both had a record growth year. Indoor Curling increased from
18 to 35 players & Chair Volleyball grew from 20 to 28 players.
Every year during the months of May through September, our Golf Team led by
Maureen Horan plays twice weekly either at Salem Green or Wenham Golf Course.
Member numbers totaled a record of 40 golfers. For the first time in COA golf, one
golfer made a hole – in – one! We are pleased to announce Brian O’Connor did this
on the 2nd hole at the Wenham Golf Club. Congratulations!

Social/Celebration Events: Our annual volunteer appreciation luncheon was held at
the Hawthorne Hotel on May 27th, with 77 of our volunteers in attendance. This
delightful event is made possible through a grant as well as the FCOA.

Among our most important events is our bi-annual Veterans Breakfast, held each May
& November. This event is generously funded and prepared by Louise Moore and the
Masons. Our May event served 59 Veterans, while our November Veterans breakfast
provided breakfast to 77 Veterans. The Star-Spangled Banner was sung by Lizzie
Tauro, who provided a very moving, flawless rendition of our National Anthem.
Our annual Thanksgiving Day at the Masons was attended by 131 participants and
more than 25 volunteers. The dedication of Louise Moore and her Team of Masons,
Police Chief Dennis King and several officers, as well as the COA Director and her
staff, is one of the most anticipated highlights each year. December brought 108
people to our annual FCOA Holiday Open House, where everyone enjoyed a
delectable buffet served by members of the FCOA Board.

Transportation: More than 6,144 rides for 303 seniors were provided. This is a forty-
one (41%) increase from 2024. Thirty-two percent (32%) of rides were to access
nutrition services such as the food pantry, grocery stores and to the COA for lunch.
Access to both out-of-town and in-town medical and dental appointments was
provided to thirty-two percent (32%) of riders. The remaining thirty-seven percent
(37%) of rides brought residents in-town to access the Abbot Public library, banks,
hairdressers, JCC, pharmacies, Post Office, Public Housing Office, YMCA, and to
the COA for all programming. The COA & Abbot Public Library began a Pilot project
this year where a Library volunteer and COA driver delivers books to homebound
residents.
MassDOT’s Community Transit Mobility grant program has provided three of the
COA’s four current vehicles. Since 2001, the COA has received a total of seven (7)
vehicles. The MassDOT Transit Operating grant awarded the COA $45,000 towards
salaries and fuel in FY24/25. The COA will not be granted any future MassDOT
cash funding or vehicles unless the Town becomes 3A compliant.

Volunteer Opportunities: 20 seniors participated in the Sr. Tax Work Off Program.
Volunteers provided 2,102 hours of service to eight Town departments. More than
100 volunteers provided over 6,000 hours of time in 2025. Volunteers provide Fitness
Center monitoring, Gardening, handing out grab-n-go meals, fitness instructors,
FCOA members, COA Board members and lunch servers to assist in keeping the
COA in line with its goals. If the COA were to pay for this assistance the cost would
be $90,000 based on minimum wage.
Students from Brown, Glover, Tower & Veterans schools made special cards
throughout the year both for holiday and special events. In December, the Driftwood,
Marblehead and Swampscott Garden Clubs also donate their time to make beautiful

                DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS

The mission of the Town of Marblehead Department of Public Works (DPW) is to
maintain and improve the Town’s public ways, stormwater infrastructure, public
shade trees, vehicular fleet and to coordinate public way snow and storm operations
in a safe, cost effective, and environmentally sustainable manner.

REGULATORY REQUIREMENTS MS4 NPDES PERMIT NUMBER

The Municipal Separate Storm Sewer System (MS4) permit is administered by the
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and the Massachusetts Department of
Environmental Protection (MassDEP) (MS4 NPDES Permit Number MAR041047).
Annual data was compiled and submitted to EPA and MassDEP in September 2025.

DIVISION REPORTS
HIGHWAY DIVISION
The Highway Division maintains nearly 70 miles of public streets and sidewalks in
Town, including but not limited to, repairing trenches and patching potholes,
sidewalk maintenance and rehabilitation, street sweeping, storm and accident debris
removal, maintenance and repair of Town fences and street signs, pavement marking,
roadside landscaping, and Christmas tree removal. The Division also has oversight of
all Public Way projects and supplies equipment or labor as required for other Town
Department’s tasks.

STORMWATER (DRAIN) DIVISION

The storm water system includes over 4,000 catch basins and manholes. The Storm
Water Division’s crew responds to flooding issues, blocked storm lines, resident
inquiries, the repair of manholes and catch basins, the cleaning and maintenance of
stormwater lines and the re-stablishing of drainage ditches. The Division also has
oversight of all Storm Water Projects

    Stormwater Metrics


 Drain Structure Repair (Catch Basin/Manholes)            16
 Drain Repairs (linear feet)                              75
 CCTV Inspections of Drainage Pipe (linear feet)          600
 Animals Rescued from Town Drains                         14

TREE DIVISION

Public shade trees located on public ways are under the jurisdiction of the Tree
Warden and are guided by the Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 87 and the Town
of Marblehead Tree Bylaws. Since 1899, Massachusetts General Law has mandated
that all cities and towns in the Commonwealth have a Tree Warden, a requirement
that remains in effect today under MGL Chapter 41, Sections 1 and 106.

The Tree Warden duties include inspection of all tree inquiries, whether made by
phone, email, or through the online reporting system at marbleheadma.gov. The tree
crew is responsible for day-to-day operations including tree removal, pruning,
planting and stump removal. Once again, the state experienced significant drought
conditions that directly impacted its forests and street trees. While conditions
fluctuated, several regions reached Level 2 (Significant Drought) or even Level 3
(Critical Drought) status by late summer and fall, causing visible stress and long-term
health concerns for trees.

The Tree Division purchased 100 seedlings from the Massachusetts Tree Wardens
Association that were planted and cared for in conservation areas by the Marblehead
Conservancy and other volunteers.

The Tree Division would like to thank residents that made donations to the Town’s
tree planting fund. Funds go directly to purchasing and planting trees in Town.

Set Back Tree Planting
At Town meeting residents approved Article 42 Public Shade Trees; Protection: to
amend the General Bylaws, Chapter 177 Article IV to add a new Section-9, Set Back
Tree Planting. The Town now may plant trees within 20 feet of the public right of
way, provided that written permission from the adjoining property owner is obtained
first consistent with Massachusetts G.L. c 87, sec. 7. The Article will aid in creating
optional tree planting locations to help maintain tree canopy as the Town works to
upgrade the sidewalks to ADA compliance.

SNOW AND STORM OPERATIONS

The DPW is the Town’s lead
Department for all winter storm
operations. The Town of Marblehead
winter storm response is conducted
by Town staff only. Departments that
comprise the Snow Team are: DPW (all
Divisions),   Water    and    Sewer
Departments, Recreation and Parks
Department, Cemetery Department,
Health Department, and School
Department.

The winter of 2025 had approximately 10.6 inches of snow and no blizzard
categorized storms. Ice control was part of every winter weather event. The increase
demand for road treatment and cost of salt was the largest portion of the Snow budget
in fiscal year 2025. The Snow Team is still adjusting to the removal of the historical
winter parking ban. To provide the same level of service, the DPW has noted
increased work hours and difficulty meeting the same snow pushback limits.

When a snow event of any size is forecasted, the Department requests all residents
find off street parking to support snow fighting efforts, regardless of snow restrictions
being issued. This allows crews to conduct efficient snow clearing efforts and
minimizes the chances of damage. The Department encountered difficulty
maintaining the same level of service even after applying additional time and
resources due to the lack of the traditional winter parking ban.

We encourage you to review the Snow Emergency information at Marbleheadma.gov
for helpful tips and answers to frequently asked questions regarding snow situations.

Snow and Storm Operation Metrics

      Snow Emergency Events Called (Code Red)             0
      Days of Snow and Ice Operations                     7
      Plowable Events                                     1
      Days of Snow Removal Operations                     0

Message from the Director
The Department of Public Works continues to advance the development of a Master
Transportation Plan. This effort includes coordination of infrastructure improvements
that affect final paving and sidewalk upgrades. The DPW met quarterly with the
Water and Sewer Commission and National Grid to review capital improvement plans
and coordinate project sequencing in conjunction with the Town’s pavement
management plan.

In 2025, the Department implemented a revised staffing structure that established new
leadership roles and expanded cross-training among employees to create a more
efficient and unified DPW workforce. The Department also focused on knowledge
transfer from two employees who have planned retirements in 2026.

The Town has implemented new technology to improve tracking of accounts payable,
payroll, and accounts receivable. To address the increased administrative workload,
the part-time General Clerk position in the DPW office was upgraded to a full-time
Special Clerk position. I am pleased to announce that Katherine Person was promoted
to this role.

Over the past three years, the DPW has worked to maintain a level-funded operating
budget. Efforts have included revising job descriptions, restructuring staff
responsibilities, and delaying certain capital improvement projects and equipment
purchases. During this period, three positions have been eliminated from the
department.

Following the failure of the general override at the 2025 Annual Town Meeting, a
one-time transfer from the Street Opening Revolving Fund was used for FY26 to
support Section 116 (Rebuild/Maintain/Hot Top Pavement/Streets). Continued
reliance on revolving funds and position reductions is not sustainable while
maintaining the current level of service. Residents are encouraged to participate in
the annual budget review process conducted by the Finance Committee and to engage
in Town Meeting discussions regarding municipal services.

The DPW would like to acknowledge the retirement of Joe Fagundes, a dedicated
employee who started on the crew, who went on to serve as a lead HEO, and later
continued supporting the department as a mechanic. Thank you, Joe, for your years
of service to the Town of Marblehead.

I would like to thank all Tower Way employees for their continued commitment to
improving efficiency and delivering high-quality service. Their efforts support the

ENGINEERING DIVISION
In 2025, the Town of Marblehead’s Engineering Division continued to provide a wide
range of engineering services in support of the Town. Following its transition
integrating into the Department of Public Works (DPW), the Town Engineer
maintains offices at Public Works, 3 Tower Way, and at Abbott Hall, Room 301, 188
Washington St.

The Town Engineer provides the following services for the Town:
    • Engineering peer review,
    • Construction administration and inspections,
    • Engineering design for infrastructure improvement projects,
    • Field engineering services for roadway or drainage improvements,
    • Street re-number requests
    • Oversee and operate the Town’s internal GIS Mapping Services,
    • Information and records research,
    • Facilitate the implementation and coordination of the Town’s MS4 program,
    • Develop and maintain the Town’s Illicit Discharge Detection and
       Elimination (IDDE), and
    • Serving as the Town’s Floodplain Administrator.

The Town Engineer is responsible for maintaining and updating the records of Town
owned property and infrastructure. Individuals seeking records pertaining to roadway
layout, drainage, Town owned buildings and private property lines, were able to make
requests via email or in person. Surveyors, attorneys, architects, engineers, and
residents are the typical customers requesting this information.

Additionally, the Town Engineer serves on the following boards and committees:
Hazard Mitigation Plan Implementation and Monitoring Team; Complete Streets
Committee; Harbor Plan Implementation Committee; the Traffic Safety and Advisory
Committee, the Bike Plan Steering Committee, the Master Plan Committee, and the
Public Works Committee.

The Town Engineer led or supported the following projects in 2025:
    1. Design of the Harold B. Breare Bridge (Village Street Bridge, M-04-001
       2Q6) Replacement (Greenman-Pedersen, GPI) – Project management of
       25% design phase. Project reached the 25% design milestone on September
       5, 2025, which was subsequently submitted to MassDOT for technical
       review. 25% design phase funded through Chapter 90, to be submitted for
       TIP construction funded grant in 2026.

                          FINANCIAL SERVICES

The Financial Services department is responsible for the Town’s financial functions,
including accounting, assessing, payroll, revenue collection, technology, budgeting,
capital financing and retirement management. The Financial Services department is
overseen by the Town's Finance Director. The Finance Director also serves as the
Chief Financial Officer responsible for the management of information technology
for the Town. In addition, the Finance Director/CFO serves as an ex-officio member
of the Town’s Retirement Board and the Town’s Compensation Committee.

Within Financial Services, the Accounting office is responsible for the audit and
approval of all town expenditures and the preparation of various accounting and
financial reports required by the State’s Department of Revenue. The Assessors office
is responsible for determining all real and personal property valuation within the
town. The Treasurer/Collector's office is responsible for the distribution of payroll
and vendor checks and the collection of taxes and fees owed to the Town. The Payroll
office performs all payroll functions and administers various employee benefits,
including health insurance, dental insurance, flexible spending plans, 457 deferred
compensation plans, and life insurance. The Payroll office also manages
unemployment and workers' compensation benefits.

The Financial Services Department publishes a separate Annual Comprehensive
Financial Report (ACFR) for the Town on a fiscal year basis (July 1 to June 30) each
year. A copy of the latest ACFR (FY2024) is available on the Town’s website or by
contacting the Finance Department.

Following are the more significant accomplishments of the Financial Services
department during 2025:

    •    Working on the Certificate of Achievement for Excellence in Financial
         Reporting from the Government Finance Officers Association for the
         eighteenth year. This award is only given to government units whose annual
         comprehensive financial reports (ACFRs) achieve the highest standards in
         government accounting and financial reporting.

    •    Working closely with the independent CPA audit firm to prepare and issue
         the Town’s FY2025 ACFR.




                                        193
                                        197
                                      197
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


    •   In the context of financing, the Town has maintained its AAA bond rating
        by the independent rating agency Standard & Poor’s (S&P). Marblehead is
        one of 69 Massachusetts communities to receive the highest municipal
        rating.

    •   Completed the annual open enrollment process in April and May for
        employee and retiree health insurance. The Town began purchasing health
        insurance plans through the State’s Group Insurance Commission (GIC) in
        July 2012 and is currently in a three-year agreement that is effective through
        FY2026.

    •   Conducted an open enrollment in May for optional dental, disability, and
        vision insurance programs for employees and retirees. Under this program,
        employees and retirees pay 100% of the premiums for this insurance through
        a payroll deduction.

    •   Working on Tyler Munis ERP system conversion for the General Ledger.

    •   The Financial Forecast presented and completed the Tax Recap December.

    •   Working on the Department of Revenue reports within specified deadlines,
        including the balance sheet for Free Cash, and other accounting forms.

                             FIRE DEPARTMENT

To the Honorable Select Board and Citizens of Marblehead. As we reflect on the past
year, I am pleased to present the annual report of the Marblehead Fire and Rescue
Department. This report provides an overview of our activities, accomplishments, and
milestones, and serves as a testament to our unwavering commitment to the safety,
well-being, and resilience of the Marblehead community.

Throughout 2025, the Marblehead Fire Department faced a wide range of challenges
and opportunities. Through each, our members demonstrated exceptional
professionalism, dedication, and adaptability. This report reflects the collective
efforts of our highly trained firefighters, dedicated civilian staff dispatchers, and the
continued support of the community we proudly serve.

From emergency response and fire prevention to community outreach, training, and
preparedness initiatives, the following pages highlight the multifaceted role your fire
department plays in protecting lives, property, and the environment. As the demands
for emergency services continue to evolve, we remain committed to innovation,
continuous improvement, and proactive service delivery to meet the needs of our
town.

I invite you to review this report and learn more about the work accomplished over
the past year. On behalf of the men and women of the Marblehead Fire and Rescue
Department, I extend my sincere gratitude for the trust and support you place in us.
It is our privilege to serve this community. We look forward to another year of
continued collaboration, progress, and success.

MISSION:
The Marblehead Fire Department is a full-service, all-hazards fire and emergency
medical services organization committed to protecting life, property, and the
environment. We strive to deliver effective, efficient, and customer-focused life
safety services through comprehensive prevention and safety initiatives, emergency
medical care, fire suppression and rescue operations, emergency management, and
community engagement activities.

We are dedicated to providing residents and visitors with the highest level of
professional service through timely response, positive intervention, accessibility, and
public education. The Marblehead Fire Department is committed to maintaining a




                                          195
                                          199
                                        199
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


well-trained, well-equipped, and appropriately staffed organization, recognized for
outstanding customer service, operational excellence, and fiscal responsibility.

Guided by our core values of Teamwork, Integrity, Communication, Excellence,
and Transparency, we provide caring, compassionate, and competent service in all
that we do. This community puts its trust in us as emergency first responders and
expects that when we answer a “911” call we are prepared, professional and able to
perform as a team in order to prevent harm, preserve life, and protect property. It is a
tribute to everyone in the Department that day-in and day-out, we meet those
challenges and that we do so under conditions that can be demanding and dangerous.
I commend the men and women of the Marblehead Fire Department who remain
dedicated to providing quality and innovative fire, rescue, and emergency medical
services to our community.

ADMINISTRATION:
The administration function of the department is to ensure the effective delivery of
Fire Protection and Emergency Medical Services, by using sound management
principles for budget preparation, revenue collection, and human resource
management. Financial responsibility is paramount in the preparation and
expenditures of the $5,489,479.00 budget.
I have established the following goals for the upcoming year.

The Marblehead Fire Department will advocate to hire two new firefighter recruits
to help bring staffing levels up to an appropriate and sustainable level. Increasing
staffing will improve operational readiness and help reduce firefighter burnout,
ensuring the safety and well-being of both firefighters and the community.
     • The Training Division will continue to develop and deliver training
         programs that address the evolving threats and challenges firefighters face
         on a daily basis. These include the rise in lithium-ion battery fires, changes
         in modern structural materials, and an increase in civil unrest and active
         shooter incidents.
     • The Training Division will place a strong emphasis on the development of a
         mental health training programs for firefighters. This programs will
         address PTSD, anxiety, and other mental health challenges that result
         from repeated exposure to traumatic events.

The Marblehead Fire Department will also strive to replace outdated firefighting
gear with PFAS-free protective equipment, in an effort to maintain the highest level
of firefighter safety while reducing exposure to toxic PFAS chemicals.




                                         196
                                         200
                                        200
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


    •    The department will work collaboratively with the Town of Marblehead
         and the Firefighters Union to implement a coordinated and multifaceted
         effort aimed at reducing firefighter exposure to carcinogens and other
         occupational health hazards.

The Department will continue the rollout our new records management system First
Due throughout Spring; and optimization will continue throughout the year. ·

FIRE RESCUE & EMS OPERATIONS:
The Marblehead Fire Department consists of forty-two full-time personnel, when
fully staffed. Due to budget reductions over the past fiscal year, our staffing level has
fallen to thirty-nine firefighters, which are assigned to fire and emergency rescue.
The thirty-nine firefighters are divided into four groups of nine members, consisting
of one Captain, one Lieutenant and seven Firefighters. The four groups are
commanded by the Duty Captain, who is responsible for the oversight of all activities
occurring on their respective groups. Those responsibilities range from incident
management to customer service, with an end that ensures that we have mitigated
every situation safely, professionally, and most importantly to the satisfaction of our
customers. The fire department’s primary responsibility is protecting the lives and
property of the citizens and visitors, in addition to responding to routine calls for
assistance from the public as needed. Services provided include combating all types
of fires, providing emergency medical care to the sick and injured, containing and
mitigating the effects of leaks and spills of hazardous materials, rescuing those who
are physically trapped in such situations as motor vehicle accidents, industrial
accidents or collapsed structures, providing ice and water rescue, mitigating the
hazards associated from downed power lines or natural gas leaks and providing aid
in situations where those in the community need special assistance such as lock out/in
situations, or animal rescues. In addition, all of the men and women of the Marblehead
Fire Department assist in providing public fire safety and emergency preparedness
educational training and programs to the general public; safety inspections,
maintaining equipment, apparatus, facilities, participate in daily fire rescue and EMS
training. The fire department operates out of two fire stations with two Class Five
Ambulance Engine Companies and one Ladder Company, which provides 24/7
coverage. The fire stations are located at 1 Ocean Avenue and 1 Franklin Street.
Several specialized pieces of equipment (such as the public safety boats, DEP spill
trailer and pump trailer) make up the remaining resources at our disposal.

The Marblehead Fire Department remains an active partner with the Essex County
Mutual Aid Systems. The Department has always provided firefighting resources to
surrounding communities in their times of need and, in return, Marblehead has




                                          197
                                          201
                                        201
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


received valuable assistance during fires and other emergency situations within our
community. With today’s potential hazards and stressed staffing levels, as seen
throughout the State, this valuable assistance is truly essential for the purpose of
mitigating an emergency of a size or scope beyond the control of an individual
Department without the assistance of other surrounding departments.

FIRE RESCUE & EMERGENCY MEDICAL RESPONSE:
The Department responded to 3,552 calls for services in 2025, this reflects a 1.5
percent decrease of overall responses from 2024. Of the 3552 calls for service, 1,461
calls were for medical assistance. Emergency medical responses make up forty-one
percent of our total yearly call volume.

The Department currently maintains 39 Emergency Medical Technicians, certified at
the BLS level, including the Chief. Our two primary engines are licensed by the
Massachusetts Department of Public Health and the Office of Emergency Medical
Services as Class 5 Non-Transporting Basic Life Support Ambulances. Members
maintain their skills through regularly scheduled training conducted by the
Department’s Training Officers and EMS Coordinators in conjunction with
Cataldo/Atlantic Ambulance Company. All of our apparatus and staff vehicles are
equipped with state-of-the-art cardiac defibrillators.

The Marblehead Fire Department continues to be a leader and innovator in out-of-
hospital care. All of our in-service companies operate and are equipped at the Basic
Life Support level. This includes Defibrillators, Oxygen, Trauma supplies and
equipment, Epi-Pens, Aspirin, Narcan, Glucometers, Pulse Oximetry as well as the
Lucas CPR device.

All of our EMT’s are licensed through the Massachusetts Department of Public
Health, Office of Emergency Medical Services, and also hold certification in Basic
Life Support. Our EMT’s are required to complete a minimum of 40 hours of training
every two years.

The demand for emergency medical services continues to increase every year, and as
a result, medical aid responses have become the primary request for the services that
the Fire Department provides to citizens of Marblehead and visitors to our
community. Our Firefighter EMT’s strive to provide the citizens with excellent
emergency medical care and customer service, this is exemplified by the countless
hours of emergency medical training that all members participate in on a daily basis
and the quality of service provided by fire department personnel.

FIRE INVESTIGATION:
The Marblehead Fire Investigation Unit consists of six highly qualified and
extensively trained fire investigators: Captains Eric Ridge, McLaughlin, and J.
Thibodeau; Lieutenant Cerrutti; and Firefighter Lydon. The unit operates under the
leadership of Captain Eric Ridge, who directs and oversees all fire investigations for
the department.

The Fire Investigation Unit is responsible for determining the origin and cause of fires
within the Town of Marblehead. Unit members conduct post-fire interviews,
collaborate closely with law enforcement agencies, and serve as the primary liaison
with the State Fire Marshal’s Office and the District Attorney’s Office to ensure
thorough, coordinated investigations.

TRAINING AND SPECIAL OPERATIONS:
In 2025 each member of the department participated in 20 – 40 hours per month of
training. The Training Division is headed up by Captain Eric Ridge and Lieutenant
Liam Gilliland. They are responsible for the scheduling, coordination and record
keeping of all departmental training. Providing comprehensive training can be a
difficult task and the Training Division utilizes many resources, such as the
Massachusetts Firefighting Academy, Target Solutions, Prodigy EMS, and the
Massachusetts Interlocal Insurance Association (MIIA) to help provide props and
firefighter training that we do not readily have access to.

All of our firefighters are cross trained in emergency medicine, auto extrication
techniques, rescue operations, fire control and suppression, hazardous material
response, water and ice rescue and many other skills and techniques that we may be
called upon to perform in order to meet the needs of the community. Training our
firefighters is an ongoing challenge. Updates to current standards by the National Fire
Protection Association (NFPA), The Office of Emergency Medical Services
(OEMS), and industry best practices are constantly evolving, requiring our Training
Officers to continually revise and update training programs to match the departments
mission. Most of the firefighter’s skill training is conducted during the firefighter’s
regular work shift. Much of the training is conducted by the respective company
officers of each of the four groups. The Training Officers provide lesson plans for
specialized training such as Water and Ice rescue, Confined Space rescue, Mayday
Operations, Vehicle Extrication, etc. These types of classes are in addition to the daily
Company Level Training, that are assigned to each group.

The department has also made physical fitness a priority, in an effort to improve our
firefighter’s health and wellness and reduce injuries on the job.




                                          199
                                          203
                                        203
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



Throughout the year the Fire Department regularly conducts fire, rescue, and
emergency medical training, and all members participate in daily skills retention
training as well as annual comprehensive refresher training courses.

The department continues to benefit from donated used vehicles, and homes
scheduled for demolition. With the support of local property owners, we can conduct
valuable training in these homes before they are torn down. An ideal donation is a
house that is scheduled for removal by the property owner or real-estate developer.
The property still belongs to the owner, and after the training has been completed, the
structure can be demolished as originally planned. Our department will use these
houses to practice firefighting skills that are destructive in nature and would not
otherwise be possible to conduct. During training exercises, the Fire Department fills
the home with theatrical smoke to practice searching for a victim or lost firefighter.
Training like this does not include any live fire scenarios, but we can create a safe
smoke-filled environment, by use of a theatrical smoke machine to attain zero
visibility, which simulates the conditions encountered in a structure fire. The
Department also uses these structures to practice identifying building construction
methods, techniques for opening up ceilings and walls to search for hidden fire and
cutting holes in roofs to vent smoke. Firefighters also use these properties to practice
survival techniques, such as how to rescue a firefighter who has become trapped
inside.

This valuable training cannot be duplicated, and we very much appreciate the
generous use of these properties by the owners before the homes are demolished.

The vehicles that are donated are used to practice vehicle extrication, and new
techniques used to rescue victims who are trapped inside a badly damaged vehicle.
According to the National Safety Council, traffic fatalities continue to rise nationwide
- which highlights the need for emergency responders to be as well-equipped and
skillfully trained. Using actual cars allows firefighters to keep their skills proficient
and become more familiar with how their lifesaving cutting and spreading equipment
(such as the "Jaws of Life" hydraulic rescue tool) behaves in real-life situations.

EMERGING CONCERNS:
Emerging Fire Risks from Electric Vehicles, E-Scooters, and Home Battery
Systems
The growing presence of electric vehicles (EVs), e-scooters, and residential energy
storage systems—commonly referred to as “battery walls”—presents new and
significant challenges for the Marblehead Fire Department. Unlike conventional fires,




                                          200
                                          204
                                        204
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


incidents involving lithium-ion batteries can be exceptionally difficult to manage.
These fires can reignite, release toxic gases, and pose electrocution hazards to
responders. While EVs may statistically catch fire less frequently than gasoline-
powered vehicles, when incidents do occur, they require substantial water resources,
specialized training, and updated operational strategies. Many departments, including
those in Massachusetts, are still working to build full preparedness for these unique
hazards.

Key Concerns for Firefighters in Marblehead
   • Thermal Runaway: Lithium-ion batteries can enter a self-sustaining, rapid-
       fire condition known as thermal runaway. This makes suppression difficult
       and often requires thousands of gallons of water over extended periods to
       fully control.
   • Reignition: Even after a fire appears extinguished, battery packs can
       reignite hours or days later, creating a persistent hazard.
   • Toxic Fumes and Electrocution Risk: Fires involving lithium-ion batteries
       release hazardous gases, and damaged battery packs—particularly those
       connected to home charging systems or residential battery walls—can
       deliver dangerous electric shocks.
   • Unique Fire Behavior: Fires fueled by these energy storage systems
       progress differently than traditional fires, requiring the Marblehead Fire
       Department to reevaluate standard firefighting tactics and incident
       strategies.
   • Training and Equipment Gaps: Many fire departments, including
       Marblehead, are still developing specialized training, equipment (such as
       nozzles designed for EV battery fires), and protocols to safely and
       effectively manage these complex incidents, as noted by the National
       Transportation Safety Board (NTSB).

By recognizing and planning for these emerging risks, the Marblehead Fire
Department aims to maintain firefighter safety while protecting the community from
the unique hazards posed by modern electric energy systems.

Rising Cases of Active Shooter Events:
Active shooter incidents have become a significant and growing concern for the
Marblehead Fire Department and Emergency Medical Services (EMS). Traditionally,
fire and EMS personnel in Marblehead, as in many communities, operated primarily
in a “standby” role, awaiting law enforcement clearance before entering an active
threat scene. However, the increasing frequency and severity of events such as school
shootings, workplace violence, and acts of terrorism have required a reevaluation of




                                        201
                                        205
                                      205
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


this approach. Modern response protocols now emphasize a more proactive role for
Marblehead Fire and EMS personnel, including the ability to operate in or
immediately adjacent to the “hot zone” under law enforcement protection. Effective
management of these incidents depends on close coordination with local police and
regional law enforcement partners to address mass casualty situations, deliver rapid
medical care, and provide critical “warm zone” interventions. By adopting these
updated strategies, the Marblehead Fire Department enhances its ability to ensure
timely treatment for victims while maintaining the safety of responders and the
community.

FIRE PREVENTION DIVISION:
The Fire Prevention Inspector is located at 7 Widger Road, in the inspectional
services wing of the Mary Alley Municipal Building. The Fire Prevention Inspector
delivers a well-structured inspection program of commercial occupancies, residential
properties, schools, institutions, and other places of assembly. These building are
inspected with the intention of removing hazards and correcting unsafe conditions. In
addition, the Fire prevention Inspector works to ensure compliance with
Massachusetts general Laws, the Code of Massachusetts Regulations, and By-Laws
of the Town of Marblehead that are under the authority of the Fire department. The
Fire Prevention Inspector works to provide fire and life safety protection to the public
by confirming code compliance and enforcing fire prevention laws and codes. The
Fire prevention Inspector works closely with the Building Department to provide plan
reviews with architects, contractors, business owners, property owners and legal
representatives. These reviews focus mainly on life safety systems, and include the
inspection and testing of any installation, alteration, modification, or repair to any fire
alarm, sprinkler system or other suppression system.

As part of the department’s public education initiative, The Fire prevention Inspector
works closing with our SAFE Instructors and public and private schools and various
other children’s groups to schedule school visits and host the Fire Prevention Week
Open House. Working together in the pursuance of life safety and property protection,
citizens of the Town are encouraged to contact the Fire Prevention Office with any
concerns that pertain to fire protection and fire safety. The Fire Prevention Inspector,
Captain McLaughlin is committed to work hard to identify and reduce the risk of fire
in our community while maintaining the best possible customer service and highest
standard of professionalism. permitting processes are followed in the construction of
new buildings and the rehabilitation of older occupancies. This division is responsible
for the implementation and supervision of all fire and life safety inspection programs
within the Town of Marblehead.

COMMUNITY RISK REDUCTION:
The Marblehead Fire Department continues to take a very proactive approach in the
community. Members participate in many community events, such as community
meetings, Council on Aging activities, Anchor to Windward activities, Marblehead
Housing Authority safety training, and nursing home evacuation drills. Our Student
Awareness of Fire Education (SAFE) and Senior SAFE Instructors provide valuable
curriculum related to fire safety.

Because we believe in the value of fire safety education and public interaction as a
major component in creating a safer community. Firefighters Mark Tentindo, John
Lequin and Greg Lydon have taken the lead to act as the department’s Public
Educators and Community Liaison.

The Fire Department continues to work in cooperation with other town departments
and community groups to offer additional services to the community in concert with
our all-hazards approach to community risk reduction and safety. We have held
several CPR / First Aid classes at no charge to any community member or
organization. Additionally, the fire department offers residential safety audits at no
charge. Fire and life safety survey’s help reduce the risk of fires and other
emergencies in the home before they occur.

Another important service the department offers is child car seat installations.
Firefighter, Micah Alden-Danforth is the departments certified Child Passenger
Safety Seat Technician, and his expertise enables us to provide an invaluable service
to our community. Micah installed dozens of cars seats in 2025, at no cost to the
community.
