on the Board of Selectmen, was Commander of VFW Post 2005, and held roles as a
member of the Marblehead Historical Commission and as Shellfish Constable.
Known for his generosity, leadership, and unwavering dedication, Harry freely gave
his time, wisdom, and service to others.

Harry Christensen will be remembered for his courage, selflessness, and lifelong
devotion to his community and his country.

The department applied for the 2025 Emergency Management Performance Grant
(EMPG) and, for the seventeenth consecutive year, was successful in securing
$5,100.00 in funding. This year’s grant award was used to replace hose and associated
appliances, as well as to purchase new smoke ejectors.

In 2025, the Executive Office of Public Safety and Security (EOPSS), in partnership
with the Department of Fire Services (DFS), announced the availability of grant
funding for fire departments across every city and town in the Commonwealth to
support the purchase of firefighter safety equipment. Our department utilized these
grant funds to acquire ropes and high-angle rescue equipment, as well as to replace
several face pieces for our self-contained breathing apparatus (SCBA).

Regrettably, for the first time in 18 years, the department did not qualify for the
Student Awareness of Fire Education (SAFE) grant. The Department of Fire Services

threshold. Despite this outcome, the department continues to benefit from an
exceptionally dedicated and talented team of educators, and both the student
education initiative and the Senior SAFE program have been very well received
within the community.

The loss of the $8,100 grant would have significantly impacted our ability to continue
providing these vital educational programs to the town’s youth and senior population,
were it not for the generous support of the Marblehead Female Humane Society and
the Marblehead Masons, Philanthropic Lodge F. & A. M.

Lequin, for their unwavering commitment to educating the town’s youth and seniors

We invite and strongly encourage all Marblehead seniors to take full advantage of the
Senior SAFE program. This fire prevention and life safety education initiative is
designed to reduce fire-related risks among older adults in the Commonwealth of
Massachusetts by addressing the specific hazards most relevant to this population.
These risks include smoking-related fires, home oxygen use, cooking safety, and
electrical and heating hazards.

In addition to education, the program offers home safety assessments and the
installation of smoke and carbon monoxide detectors when needed. The Senior SAFE
Program fosters a collaborative partnership between older adults and the Fire
Department through established senior service providers, including the Council on
Aging, Elder Services, the Visiting Nurse Association, and Older Adult Living.

APPARATUS AND EQUIPMENT:
The standards established by the National Fire Protection Association (NFPA)
provide nationally recognized guidelines that govern nearly all aspects of fire service
operations. The Marblehead Fire Department is committed to adhering to these
standards to ensure the highest levels of safety, reliability, and performance for its
personnel and equipment.

The department employs two mechanics whose responsibilities include, but are not
limited to, annual testing and preventive maintenance of all fire apparatus, utility
vehicles, and small equipment. Their duties are critical to maintaining operational
readiness and compliance with applicable NFPA requirements. The following
outlines the primary responsibilities of the department mechanics.

Apparatus
The department operates a total of four fire engines, consisting of two frontline units
and two reserve units. These include a 1994 Spartan pumper, a 2006 Pierce Dash
pumper, a 2017 Pierce Enforcer pumper, and a 2022 Pierce Enforcer pumper. Each
engine is equipped with a 1,500-gallon-per-minute pump, hose, ladders, ventilation
fans, tarps, air bottles, tools, medical equipment, and other essential firefighting gear.

In accordance with NFPA 1911, each fire engine is required to undergo an annual
pump performance test. Each test averages approximately three hours to complete.
Should a pump fail to meet the required performance standards, repairs must be
completed and the unit retested to ensure compliance.




                                          207
                                          211
                                         211
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



Ladder One is a 2013 Pierce Arrow aerial apparatus carrying a wide range of
equipment, including hose, hydraulic rescue tools, ground ladders, smoke ejector
fans, saws, lighting equipment, and salvage and rescue gear. The apparatus is
equipped with a 100-foot aerial ladder, which is essential for accessing rooftops and
elevated areas with limited access.

Pursuant to NFPA 1914, the aerial ladder is required to undergo an annual inspection
conducted by certified third-party inspectors. This inspection averages approximately
five hours to complete. Ladder One will reach 13 years of service next year and will
be approaching the latter part of its expected frontline service life. The lead time for
the construction and delivery of a new ladder truck is approximately four years. As a
result, by the time a replacement ladder is placed into service, the current apparatus
will be 17 years old. For this reason, I plan to request funding authorization at the
2026 Town Meeting to ensure a timely and responsible replacement.


STATIONS & FACILITIES:
Fire Headquarters and the Franklin Street Fire Station continue to present significant
operational and infrastructure challenges. Fire Headquarters, constructed in 1958, has
undergone multiple modifications over the decades, resulting in a tired and aging
structure. While the department has invested in several capital improvements to
maintain functionality—such as replacing windows, installing energy-efficient LED
lighting, and upgrading garage doors—the building continues to experience age-
related deterioration.

Although the exterior is repainted on a five-year cycle, recent inspections have
revealed water infiltration behind windowsills and wooden trim, causing concealed
rot and structural degradation. Continuing to paint deteriorating wood is no longer a
cost-effective or sustainable solution. Additionally, the original wooden gutters have
begun to fail, allowing rainwater to cascade down the brick façade and erode mortar
joints. The plumbing infrastructure within the walls is also steadily deteriorating.

One notable improvement this year was the successful repair of the apparatus floor
drainage system, which has eliminated the longstanding issue of standing water on
the apparatus floor. However, ongoing budget constraints have limited the
department’s ability to renovate the bathrooms at Fire Headquarters. In addition to
being significantly outdated, the facility currently lacks restroom and shower
accommodations for female firefighters. Given the strong likelihood of appointing




                                         208
                                         212
                                        212
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


women to the department in the near future, this deficiency represents both an
operational and equity concern that must be addressed.

Despite these challenges associated with a facility that has surpassed its useful life
expectancy, department personnel consistently go above and beyond by performing
additional duties such as painting, minor carpentry, and basic plumbing repairs. Their
willingness to assist with small projects throughout the station helps stretch limited
budget resources and maintain day-to-day operations.

At the Franklin Street Fire Station, the window restoration project was halted this
year after the Town was notified by the Grant Coordinator that it was not selected for
funding through the Massachusetts Preservation Projects Fund (MPPF). Town
Meeting had previously approved $130,000 to serve as a matching contribution
should the grant be awarded. Following the grant denial, it was determined that these
funds would instead be used to install new clapboards on the east and west sides of
the building, as this work fell within the scope of the approved funding article.

Moving forward, I will continue to advocate for the Town to explore the feasibility
of acquiring property and constructing a modern, state-of-the-art Public Safety
Building that would house the Police Department, Fire Department, and 911 Dispatch
under one roof.

COMMUNITY PROJECTS & EVENTS:
The Fire Department continues our close partnership with the Elder Act Club and the
Marblehead Council on Aging to promote the lockbox program. In 2025, 35 new
lockboxes were installed. The program was established to provide at no cost to
Marblehead senior citizens and any individual with a disability, a secure lockbox that
would be mounted next to the main entrance of the occupants dwelling. The lockbox
secures a key to the home that can only be accessed by the Fire Department. There
have been more than two hundred lockboxes installed since the inception of the
program. Access to a key in the event of a medical emergency has saved countless
minutes in providing lifesaving care to Marblehead’s seniors and disabled.

The Marblehead Fire Department offers a free and voluntary Home Safety
Assessment to any resident that would like one. The Fire Department will assess your
living environment for many common life safety hazards. This includes providing an
assessment and advice on slip and fall hazards, working smoke and carbon monoxide
alarms, electrical hazards, home exit planning, kitchen fire safety, combustibles
located too close to heating appliances, the File of Life (medical information card),
key box access, as well as many other offerings.




                                        209
                                        213
                                       213
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



The program is available to any resident, regardless of age. However, the Marblehead
Fire Department works closely with the Council on Aging (COA) to provide the very
best array of services to our senior population. A representative from the COA can
accompany a member of the Fire Department during a survey of a senior's home in
order to share a whole host of resources and opportunities of which they may be
unaware. Any interested seniors should contact the COA to schedule an appointment.

Thank you for helping make the 2025 holiday season brighter for so many.

to thank the Marblehead High School administration for providing us the opportunity
to participate in this meaningful event.

On June 9, 2025, department members, joined by family, friends and dignitaries,
observed our twenty-fifth annual Firefighters Memorial, since the rededication of the
firefighter plot at Waterside Cemetery. This annual event honors and remembers
firefighters who have passed away or died in the line of duty. The day serves as a




                                          210
                                          214
                                        214
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


solemn occasion for the department and our community to pay tribute to those who
have sacrificed their lives while serving to protect others from fire and other
emergencies.

2025 was another busy year for providing CPR training to the public and private
sector, as well as providing CPR/ Defibrillator certification to our town employees.
In twenty 2025, we trained over 275 people in the use of CPR and Defibrillators. The
dedication and commitment of the public and the town employees has helped keep
Marblehead at Heart Safe Community. Since becoming a Heart Safe Community, the
number of defibrillators that been purchased and installed in buildings, both public
and privately has increased exponentially. The ratio of defibrillators to people is now
1 defibrillator per every 75 people in town.

On April 13, 2024, the Fire Department was honored to participate in the Recreation
and Parks Department’s Annual Easter Egg Drop Challenge held at the COA. This
engaging event brought together children, staff, and community partners for a fun and
educational experience that encouraged creativity, problem-solving, and teamwork.

The ingenuity and creativity demonstrated by the participants were truly impressive.
The children developed a wide variety of inventive designs to protect their eggs
during the drop, showcasing remarkable thoughtfulness, imagination, and enthusiasm
throughout the challenge. Their excitement and determination made the event both
entertaining and inspiring for everyone involved.

Thanks to the collaborative efforts of Recreation and Parks staff, Fire Department
personnel, and community volunteers, the event was exceptionally well organized
and ran smoothly. The Easter Egg Drop Challenge was a resounding success and
provided a memorable experience for all who attended. The Fire Department
appreciates the opportunity to be involved in such a positive community-focused
event and looks forward to continued partnerships that promote learning, creativity,
and community engagement.

The summer of 2025 in Marblehead closely mirrored the conditions experienced in
2024. Residents who favor hot weather certainly had ample opportunity to enjoy it,
with 14 summer days exceeding 90 degrees. On June 24, the town recorded a historic
high of 102 degrees, followed by a three-day heat wave.

In response to increasingly extreme summer temperatures in recent years, it has
become customary for the Fire Department to partner with the Recreation and Parks
Department during periods of excessive heat. Together, they provide residents with a




                                         211
                                         215
                                       215
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


welcome reprieve through the annual Beat the Heat spray-down event. The event
continues to be well attended and has grown in popularity each summer.

The fire department joined other town entities to participate in the Chamber of
Commerce fourth annual Marblehead Spring Celebration. Touch a Truck event. It
was well attended, and everyone had a good time.

The Marblehead Firefighters continued their longstanding commitment to charitable
service by proudly sponsoring the annual Firefighters Cooking for a Cure event on
April 12, 2025. This highly anticipated fundraiser brought together firefighters,
community members, and supporters for an evening dedicated to raising awareness
and funds for a vital cause. All proceeds from the event were donated to the Muscular
Dystrophy Association in support of its mission to advance research, provide care,
and improve the lives of individuals affected by neuromuscular diseases.

Firefighters from Marblehead and surrounding communities gathered at the
Danversport Yacht Club to participate in a friendly yet spirited culinary competition,
preparing and serving their signature dishes to attendees. Approximately 500 guests
were in attendance, enjoying the food, camaraderie, and opportunity to support an
important charitable effort.

The event was an outstanding success, generating an estimated $14,000 in donations
to aid the fight against Muscular Dystrophy. The Marblehead Firefighters extend their
sincere appreciation to all participating departments, sponsors, volunteers, and
community members whose generosity and support made this event possible. Their
continued involvement exemplifies the strong partnership between the fire service
and the community it proudly serves.

CONCLUSIONS:
As Fire Chief, I am continually reminded that the greatest strength of this department
lies in its people. Our officers, firefighters, and administrative assistant are the
foundation of everything we do. As we reflect on the past year, I extend my deepest
gratitude to the men and women of the Marblehead Fire Department for their
unwavering dedication, professionalism, and steadfast commitment to serving our
community. Through every challenge, they continue to rise to the evolving demands
of the fire service with skill, integrity, and selflessness. Their service is nothing short
of exceptional.

I would also like to recognize and sincerely thank our Civilian Dispatchers. Working
diligently behind the scenes, they play a critical role in ensuring the safety of both our

                             FIRE PREVENTION
The main objective of the Fire Prevention Office is the protection of life and property
through Community Risk Reduction. This is accomplished by effective code
enforcement through plan review, inspection and educating the public about fire
safety principles. The Fire Prevention office is located at the Municipal Building as
part of Inspectional Services, to ensure that new construction, renovations of
structures and existing structures are code compliant.

This year the Marblehead Fire Department transitioned to a new records management
system (RMS), known as First Due RMS. First Due includes fire prevention and
building preplan modules along with several other modules including incident
documentation. The new RMS allows for the integration of several separate systems
into one platform. The RMS allows electronic notification of inspection reports,
inspection tracking and follow-up notifications. 48 buildings were preplanned
(notable building features, after-hour contacts and any life safety hazards), allowing
real time notification to responding firefighters and dispatchers.

The Fire Prevention Office is using The Compliance Engine by Brycer for third party
review of all life safety system inspection reports. Brycer reviewed 395 inspection
reports in 2025 (up from 275 reports last year). All reports were reviewed,
deficiencies flagged and repair tracking with follow-up notifications were sent. The
deficiency rate was 8.76%.

Captain McLaughlin serves on the Massachusetts 527 CMR 1.00 Equipment
Committee representing the Fire Prevention Association of Massachusetts.
Massachusetts is set to promulgate an updated 527 CMR 1.00 Fire Code in 2026,
which resulted in several meetings throughout the year. He is also the Vice President
of Essex County Fire Chief’s Fire Prevention Division.            In 2025, Captain
McLaughlin attended training provided by the Massachusetts Department of Fire
Services including: the 10th edition of the Building Code, Presenting to the Appeals




                                         216
                                         220
                                       220
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Board, Municipal Hearing officer and Orientation to the National Emergency
Response System.

As part of Community Risk Reduction, the elderly home safety program continues to
provide access boxes and smoke/carbon monoxide detectors for private residences.
Firefighters spend time educating students in schools, camps and community events
about fire and life safety. On duty Firefighters conducted fire drills at educational
facilities in conjunction with Fire Prevention.
I would like to thank Chief Gilliland, who devoted a considerable amount of time
over the summer conduction inspections and Captain Ridge along with Lt Gilliland
who were instrumental in the rollout of the First Due RMS.

Training Report 2025
The mission of the Training Division is to provide quality training to our members
ensuring their readiness to respond to any emergency in the Town and surrounding
area. The safety of our firefighters and citizens is of the highest priority.

The Training Division serves to provide training resources and assist company
officers and their members in improving company operations. The goal is to maintain
a high level of proficiency, efficiency, and readiness. In addition, we strive to reduce
and prevent accidents, injuries, and occupational illnesses.

The Training Division coordinates all in-house training by developing a monthly
training calendar based on identified training needs and mandated training topics. The
company officers are responsible for conducting regular drills and classroom training
on the respective shifts. Outside agencies and independent instructors provide
additional knowledge and skills in areas that require specialized training. A goal for
2026 is the improvement of our department's ISO rating. Training is one part of this
process, and we will focus on ensuring our training meets the requirements to improve
our rating through proper allocation and documentation. The Training Division has
implemented training software that allows members to complete specific classes to
meet the ISO credentials.

Every Marblehead Firefighter is a Massachusetts licensed Emergency Medical
Technician (EMT). Emergency medical response is a large part of our job and
Firefighters train a minimum of 40 hours in a two-year cycle to maintain their
certification. The Training Division coordinates this training to ensure firefighters are
ready to expertly respond to any medical emergency above and beyond the minimum
standard.

All newly appointed firefighters are required to complete the ten-week recruit-
training program at the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy. Firefighters Jeremy
Gillis and Kyle Pettinelli attended the Massachusetts Fire Academy in Springfield
from October through December. Firefighters are encouraged to utilize the fire
academy after their graduation to hone their skills through the free courses and
training it provides.

Finally, structures that are due to be torn down can be utilized by firefighters to
practice tasks including but not limited to roof ventilation, hose movement, and
rescue scenarios. The Training Division is grateful to the citizens of Marblehead who
graciously donated the use of their structures for firefighting training. These




                                          218
                                          222
                                        222
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


structures provide an invaluable resource that cannot be substituted with training
props or mockups. Thank you.


Respectfully,
Captain Eric M. Ridge
Lieutenant Liam F. Gilliland


EMERGENCY MANAGEMENT

Introduction:
It is my privilege to present the annual report highlighting a portion of what the
Marblehead Emergency Management program has accomplished over the past fiscal
year. The information contained in this report provides a snapshot of the work
undertaken to strengthen relationships, enhance coordination, and manage threats,
hazards, incidents, and risks within the Town of Marblehead. Through daily
collaboration and ongoing projects with local, regional, and state partners, we
continue to build and sustain community resilience.

Emergency management in Marblehead can best be described as project management
operating at varying speeds, sometimes proactive and long-term through planning
initiatives, and at other times immediate and response-driven. Looking ahead, our
focus remains on further strengthening resilience through comprehensive planning,
training, exercises, public outreach, and continuous improvement, while remaining
responsive to the evolving needs of the Marblehead community.

I extend my sincere gratitude to the dedicated professionals within our department,
our public safety partners, town officials, volunteers, and community stakeholders.
Your commitment to service is the foundation of our success. Together, we will
continue working to enhance the safety, preparedness, and resilience of Marblehead.

Comprehensive Emergency Management Plan (CEMP):
The Marblehead Emergency Management Director, in close coordination with town
department heads, is actively engaged in strengthening and enhancing the Town’s
Comprehensive Emergency Management Plan (CEMP). This ongoing effort reflects
a shared commitment to preparedness, resilience, and the safety of our residents.
Through collaborative planning sessions, interdepartmental coordination, and a
thorough review of existing protocols, the team is identifying opportunities to
improve response capabilities, clarify roles and responsibilities, and incorporate




                                       219
                                       223
                                     223
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


lessons learned from recent events and evolving best practices. The updated CEMP
will further align local operations with state and federal guidance, strengthen
communication strategies, and ensure continuity of essential services during
emergencies. By working together across departments, including public safety, public
works, health, schools, and administrative leadership, Marblehead is proactively
enhancing its readiness to respond effectively to natural disasters, severe weather,
public health incidents, and other potential emergencies. This ongoing work
underscores the Town’s dedication to protecting lives, property, and the well- being
of the entire community.

Community Emergency Response Team (CERT):
For the coming year, it is the goal of the Marblehead Emergency Management
Department to work with the community to form a Community Emergency Response
Team (CERT). This initiative is designed to strengthen our community’s
preparedness, resilience, and ability to respond effectively during emergencies and
disasters The Marblehead CERT program will provide residents with hands-on
training in disaster preparedness, fire safety, light search and rescue, basic disaster
medical operations, and team organization. By equipping volunteers with these
essential skills, we aim to build a trained, organized group of community members
who can support first responders during large-scale incidents and assist neighbors
when professional resources may be delayed or overwhelmed. Marblehead has a
strong tradition of civic engagement and neighbor helping neighbor. The
establishment of a CERT team would build on that tradition by empowering
residents to take an active role in community safety. CERT volunteers will serve as a
vital link between the Emergency Management Department and the public, helping
with emergency response, public education, event support, and disaster recovery
efforts. We encourage interested residents to consider joining this important initiative.
Together, we can enhance Marblehead’s readiness and ensure our town remains
prepared, connected, and resilient in the face of any emergency.

Hazardous Mitigation Plan:
Marblehead’s Hazard Mitigation Plan is a long-term strategy that helps our
community identify natural hazards and reduce risks to people, property,
infrastructure, and the environment. Unlike emergency response plans, it focuses on
preventing or minimizing future disaster losses. Marblehead has recently updated our
plan in 2024 to comply with the Disaster Mitigation Act of 2000 and maintain
eligibility for federal disaster-mitigation funding through Federal Emergency
Management Agency (FEMA) and the Massachusetts Emergency Management
Agency (MEMA).




                                          220
                                          224
                                        224
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Purpose and Importance The plan:
    • Assesses risks from hazards such as coastal and inland flooding, high winds,
         severe storms, and extreme temperatures.
    • Estimates potential losses to guide decision-making.
    • Prioritizes actions that reduce risk before disasters occur.
    • Is required for access to certain federal and state hazard mitigation grants.
Without an approved plan, the town could lose access to critical funding for resilience
and infrastructure improvements.

Development Process The plan is developed through:
    1. Planning and Coordination – A town committee oversees the update,
        incorporating public input and coordinating with state and federal partners.
    2. Risk Assessment – Identifies likely hazards and evaluates potential impacts.
    3. Mitigation Strategies – Recommends projects and policies such as
        infrastructure upgrades, land-use planning improvements, and community
        education.
    4. Plan Maintenance – Establishes a process for ongoing monitoring and
        periodic updates.
Key Hazard Areas Based on past assessments, Marblehead’s primary risks include:
    • Coastal flooding and storm surge
    • Inland flooding from intense rainfall
    • Wind and winter storm impacts
    • Erosion and sea-level rise
These hazards threaten roads, homes, utilities, and public spaces.

Conclusion
Marblehead’s Hazard Mitigation Plan is a risk-based, regularly updated strategy
designed to reduce disaster losses, strengthen resilience, and secure continued access
to federal and state funding. Proactive planning helps the town make informed
investments that protect the community and reduce long-term costs.

Code Red:
For the past 18 years, the Town of Marblehead has utilized CodeRED as its primary
mass notification system. During that time, the platform successfully delivered
dozens of emergency alerts related to snowstorms, Causeway closures, and water
main breaks, as well as general community notifications regarding elections, road
paving, and trash collection.

In Fall 2025, the Town’s emergency notification capabilities were impacted when
OnSolve’s legacy CodeRED platform was targeted in a cyberattack. As a




                                         221
                                         225
                                       225
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


precautionary measure, the vendor permanently decommissioned the legacy system
and transitioned customers to a replacement platform, Crisis24, branded as
“CodeRED by Crisis24.” The vendor reports that a comprehensive security audit,
along with additional third-party security testing and hardening, has been completed.
However, the new platform has also experienced reliability concerns, and the Town
is actively working with Crisis24 to address ongoing notification and service issues.

According to the vendor, the cyber incident was contained within the CodeRED
environment and did not impact municipal networks or other Town information
systems. Importantly, 9-1-1 services were not affected. The vendor also advised that
system restoration relied on backups current through March 31, 2025. As a result,
registrations or updates made after that date may not have carried over, potentially
requiring residents to re-enroll or rebuild notification preferences.

The breach exposed certain user data, including phone numbers, email addresses, and
passwords. Subsequent forensic updates suggest that the scope of exposed data may
have been more limited than initially reported, and some affected datasets may not
have included active passwords. The investigation remains ongoing.

Out of an abundance of caution, residents were advised to:
    • Change any passwords that were reused from a CodeRED profile across
         other personal or business accounts.
    • Remain alert for phishing or scam messages impersonating the Town, public
         safety agencies, or notification vendors.
    • Ensure Wireless Emergency Alerts (WEA) are enabled on mobile devices,
         as WEA operates independently from CodeRED.
    • Monitor personal accounts for suspicious activity and report suspected fraud
         through appropriate channels.

It is vitally important that Marblehead maintains a reliable, secure, and resilient
emergency notification system moving forward. As we move into 2026, the town will
be transitioning over to a new mass notification system .

                                      226

GREEN MARBLEHEAD IMPLEMENTATION COMMITTEE

In 2025, the Green Marblehead Implementation Committee advanced several
initiatives supporting the Town’s Net Zero by 2040 goal, with a focus on municipal
electrification and energy efficiency planning, and sustainable infrastructure
improvements. A key milestone was adoption of the Town’s Zero-Emission Vehicle
(ZEV) First Procurement Policy by the Select Board in September 2025. The policy
establishes an electric-first approach for light-duty municipal vehicles beginning in
FY27. The Committee also supported development of an On-Street EV Charging
Grant, reviewed the Town’s Energy Reduction Plan, and reviewed public EV
charging data to inform future infrastructure planning.

The Committee made substantial progress toward Green Communities designation.
Working with PowerOptions and the Massachusetts Department of Energy
Resources, the Town developed a comprehensive Energy Reduction Plan establishing
a municipal energy-use baseline and identifying strategies to achieve a minimum of
20 percent energy usage reduction over five years. Municipal energy data were
consolidated in MassEnergyInsight, and the plan was presented to the Select Board
and Public Works Committee. Discussions also continued with the Municipal Light
Department regarding the Renewable Energy Charge as part of the designation
requirements.

Additional efforts in 2025 included support for municipal building decarbonization
and active transportation improvements. Heat pump installations and weatherization
projects advanced at several municipal facilities, including the State Street Restrooms
and the Mary Alley Building. The Marblehead Rail Trail Prototype Project was
completed with support by the MassTrails program, and a Boston MPO grant was
awarded to install bicycle parking at various locations throughout town. The
Committee also continued collaboration with the Old and Historic District
Commission through the Sustainable Heritage initiative to align preservation and
sustainability objectives.

The Committee partnered with the Old and Historic Districts Commission on the
Marblehead Sustainable Heritage Project, a grant initiative aimed at aligning the
Town’s long-standing commitment to historic preservation with its pledge to achieve
Net Zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2040. Recognizing that residential buildings
account for nearly half of Marblehead’s greenhouse gas emissions, many of which
are located within the Old and Historic Districts, the Committee worked
collaboratively with MAPC, Town staff, preservation professionals, and residents to




                                         226
                                         230
                                       230
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


address regulatory barriers that have limited the adoption of clean energy
technologies. Through comparative research, walking tours in Marblehead and
Salem, a joint session with the Old and Historic Districts Commission (OHDC),
expert review, and a well-attended public charrette, the project developed clear,
balanced draft recommendations for solar energy systems, air source heat pumps,
electric vehicle charging equipment, and energy-efficient windows. The resulting
recommendations provide a thoughtful path forward, which is the Committee is
advancing in partnership with the OHDC in 2026.

                         HARBORS AND WATERS

The Harbors and Waters Board is pleased to submit its annual report for the period
ending December 31, 2025.
The Harbormasters department has continued its dedication to its waters by
maintaining its training and knowledge of all rules and regulations in place. Public
safety is the department’s first responsibility and will maintain its preparedness for
any situation.

Main Harbor 2025
New permanent assignments                                                  39
Temporary assignments                                                      32
Approximate Main Harbor waitlist                                           2100

West Shore 2025
New permanent assignments                                                   61
Temporary assignments                                                       26
Approximate West Shore waitlist                                             284
Waitlist time                                                              2-3 years

2025 provided a great boating season with amazing weather and calm seas. The
boaters of Marblehead showed us that safe boating is a priority with our lowest year
for “calls for service” in my 8+ year tenure. Fourth of July festivities went off without
a hitch. Capital improvements may not have been noticed but general updates of
facilities and equipment were completed. These tasks included general dock repairs,
Safety Equipment updates, Software/Billing enhancements, Redecking of Stramski
Pier, and other misc. repairs.

The Harbormaster’s Patrol staff responded to various calls and rendered such aid as,
towing disabled vessels, dewatering vessels, providing medical assistance,
conducting search and rescue operations, investigating fuel and oil spills, maintaining
public access facilities and removing any hazards to navigation.

The Harbormaster and the Harbors and Waters Board extend its gratitude to The
Select Board, as well as all departments and their employees that provided service
and support to the Harbormasters operations.

Respectfully submitted,
Mark Souza Harbormaster Town of Marblehead

                          HUMAN RESOURCES

The Director of Human Resources, under the direction of the Town Administrator,
collaborates with department heads and office staff to assist with a wide range of
management responsibilities, including labor relations, contract administration, and
personnel management.

Human Resources (HR) responsibilities involve managing the entire employee
lifecycle, from finding and hiring talent (recruitment, onboarding) to training,
performance management, compensation, benefits, and ensuring legal compliance,
while also fostering a positive workplace culture, handling employee relations, and
overseeing strategic workforce planning to align people with business goals. Key
areas include recruiting, training, payroll, benefits administration, employee
relations, policy enforcement, and maintaining a safe work environment.

The following are the more significant accomplishments of the Human Resources
department during 2025:

We rolled out the annual open enrollment process in April and May for health
insurance through the:
    • Group Insurance Commission (GIC)
    • Altus Dental Insurance
    • Benefit Strategies Flexible Spending Plan
    • Boston Mutual Life Insurance
    • Voya Financial 457 Plan
    • Short Term Disability

Human Resources oversaw collective bargaining negotiations for both the
Massachusetts Municipal Employees Union and the Marblehead Coalition of Police
Local 437. Human Resources' role involves strategic preparation (analyzing market
trends), negotiations (representing the employer, balancing interests, drafting
agreements, ensuring legal compliance, resolving conflicts, and communicating
effectively), and post-agreement management (protecting the organization's interests
while fostering good employee relations, focusing on wages, benefits, safety, and
working conditions).




                                       230
                                       234
                                      234
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________




We are pleased to announce the launch of our Pay and Classification Study in
partnership with MGT, Inc. As part of our ongoing commitment to fairness,
transparency, and competitiveness, this initiative helped ensure that our job
descriptions are accurate, our pay structure is equitable, and we remain competitive
in attracting and retaining top talent. The focus of this study is for the MMEU and
administrative staff.

We rolled out Tyler/Munis for our new financial software. We have updated all
financial software to roll out a more updated payroll system. We are in the beginning
stages of rolling out the recruitment software.

Human Resources provides direct support to the Marblehead Compensation
Committee, which is established under Marblehead By-Law, Chapter 43. In this role,
HR works closely with the compensation committee, which consists of the Town
Administrator, Chief Financial Officer, and the Public Health Director to ensure that
compensation decisions are consistent, data-driven, legally compliant, and aligned
with the Town’s classification and pay framework.

HR’s support of the compensation committee includes:

    •    Preparing and reviewing job description updates.
    •    Conducting classification and reclassification analyses.
    •    Providing market data and internal equity comparisons.
    •    Ensuring compliance with collective bargaining agreements and Town
         policies.
    •    Coordinating implementation of approved compensation actions through
         payroll and personnel systems.

             MARBLEHEAD CULTURAL COUNCIL

                        The Marblehead Cultural Council is part of a network of
                        329 Local Cultural Councils (LCCs) sponsored by the
                        Massachusetts Cultural Council, which serves all 351
                        cities and towns in the Commonwealth. As the largest
                        grassroots cultural funding network in the nation, the
                        Local Cultural Council (LCC) program enriches the
                        cultural life of all of these cities and towns in
                        Massachusetts. Led by municipally appointed volunteers,
                        in 2025 LCCs awarded $7.5 million supporting over 7,300
                        community-based programs that included everything from
                        field trips to lectures, festivals, science programming,
                        workshops, music and dance performances.
Photo Credits: Jim Murphy

                         In 2025 the Massachusetts Cultural Council allotted a
                         little over $8,000 to the Marblehead Cultural Council to
                         fund projects in the arts, sciences, and humanities. Every
                         fall applications are reviewed and then awarded in the
                         early winter for the upcoming year. Twenty-four
                         applications were received and 13 awarded.

2. Colonial Craft Fair ($1529), Marblehead Museum and Historical Society
3. Headers in the Revolution ($1500), Marblehead 250
4. JCCNS Seacoast Singers, Jewish Community Center of the North Shore
5. Live Music Making History Live ($300), John Waterman
6. Meet Julia! ($300), The Delvena Theatre Company

The Marblehead Cultural Council solicits input from Marblehead residents requesting
feedback as well as preferred interests on events the Council might fund and sponsor
in the future. In 2025 this information was collected via an on-line survey available
on our website, and through a QR code link in the Marblehead Current and posted
throughout popular town establishments. This feedback directly informed the
application guidelines and priorities which are reviewed and updated at the end of
each summer. This year the survey had 135 respondents, the highest number in years.

In May the Council hosted an open reception at the garden of Abbot Public Library
to congratulate, meet, and honor the 2025 grantees. The North of Boston Program
Officer from the Massachusetts Cultural Council attended. This type of event is
specifically encouraged by the Massachusetts Council.

Marblehead Cultural Council members are appointed by the Marblehead Select
Board. In 2025 the Marblehead Cultural Council consisted of ten members including
Jeanie Stahl (Chair), Katherine Barker (Co-Chair), Aaron Liber (Co-Chair), James
Murphy (Treasurer), Marjorie Norman (Secretary), Joseph Dipoli, Alexandra
MacAaron, Mark Philip, Steve McClure, and Jennifer Uhl. The appointments of Lisa
Seltzer and Rose Gould ended mid-year.

Public meetings are generally held every two months at the town library with agenda
items posted via the town clerk’s office. Once approved, meeting minutes are posted
on the Marblehead Cultural Council website.

Contact and further information can be found at:

           MARBLEHEAD HISTORICAL COMMISSION


The Town of Marblehead has a long and important history, reflecting the early
settlement of New England, the establishment of the fishing trade, and the fight for
independence in the American Revolution. This history continues through the 19th,
20th and 21st centuries. It is the Marblehead Historical Commission’s mission to
identify, preserve and protect Marblehead’s historical assets – its places, artifacts and
stories, while making these resources accessible to all. We work to educate the public
about Marblehead’s proud history, working together with other organizations to help
achieve our mission.

The Commission has spaces in Abbot Hall to display and store historic artifacts. The
Commission maintains the Maritime Museum, the Sign Museum, the Gift Shop and
office space for research and cataloging items in the collection.

The Gift Shop, staffed by volunteers, is managed by Valerie Carlone. The Gift Shop
is open during the tourist season from late May through late October, and for
Christmas Walk in December. The Maritime Museum and the Select Board Room
are also staffed by docents during the season. The Maritime Museum is self-guided
and remains open year-round following Abbot Hall’s schedule.

The Collection Management operations are managed by Collection Manager Peter
Stacey. His work is supported by several volunteers with cataloging and archival
activities. They also answer numerous requests for information and images.

Following are the Historical Commission highlights of 2025:

•   The Commission appointed Associate Commissioners: Pat Franklin, Chris
    Johnston and Dotti Giles to one-year terms.
•   The Abbot Hall Gift Shop opened in June and closed in late December. The Gift
    Shop’s use of credit cards for purchases continues to result in increased sales.
•   The FY2025-26 priority for the Marblehead Historic Preservations Survey is the
    Historic District. This is the final area to be surveyed. The Historic District was
    surveyed about 50 years ago, but there is more to learn and better research access.
    Research work by John Clemson is scheduled to begin in the spring.
•   Cataloging work continued on newly received gifts and existing uncatalogued
    items led by Collections Manager Peter Stacey. He is assisted by volunteers
    Roberta Daniels and Carol Fullerton, Linda Harlan and Cory Crane.




                                          234
                                          238
                                        238
                  MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
    ______________________________________________________________


•   The Commission provided positions for eight Council on Aging Senior Tax
    Work-off program participants in the office, Gift Shop and Maritime Museum.
    The work these volunteers perform covers a wide range of activities and is
    extremely valuable to the Commission.
•   The Commission made repairs and improvements to the Archival storage area in
    Abbot Hall basement. Masonry repairs addressed a small leak in the outside
    corner wall. The humidifier was also replaced to maintain relative humidity
    levels in the storage space.
•   The new Sign Museum continues to be very well received, and additional signs
    have been donated, with approximately 90 signs on display now.
•   The Ten-footer shoe shed project continues. Chris Butler has completed
    renovations on the exterior of the building and work will begin soon to complete
    the interior display. Signage and interpretation plans are underway. A
    handicapped accessible path leading to the shed has been completed by Sean
    McLaughlin, Mason, which was funded by the Harold B .and Elizabet h L.
    Shattuck Fund.
•   Volunteer Dottie Giles has continued work on installing and maintaining the
    Abbot Hall display cases.
•   The Commission is part of a group working on plans to celebrate the 250th
    anniversary of the United States and has planned events and celebrations in 2024
    and 2025. Plans are underway for the 2026 celebrations, for the July 4th weekend
    and also in September,
•   Collaboration with the Swampscott Historical Commission for the preservation
    of the Glover farmhouse continues. Ed Nilsson serves as liaison to the Save the
    Glover committee.
•   The Commission supported the Marblehead Rail Trail Project with financial
    assistance to reconfigure the Clifton Station curbing in order to to retain its
    historic significance.
•   The Commission continued maintenance of its web site through the efforts of
    Commissioner David Krathwohl. Access the website at
    www.marbleheadhistory.org.

              MARBLEHEAD HOUSING AUTHORITY
The Marblehead Housing Authority (MHA) is pleased to submit our annual report for
the year ending December 31, 2025.

The Marblehead Housing Authority was established in 1948 to provide decent, safe
and sanitary housing for those of low income, including the elderly, families, and
those with disabilities.

For more than 70 years, the Marblehead Housing Authority has carried out this
mission, and provided affordable housing to some of the community’s most
vulnerable seniors and families.

Currently, more than 500 people call the MHA home.

The MHA manages 3 housing programs and maintains 307 units of housing located
at eight sites.

The developments are of various ages, sizes, and configurations, and are as follows:

                         1 bedroom      2 bedroom       3 bedroom        Total
 Family Housing:
 Barnard Hawkes Ct.      N/A            12              10               22
 Broughton Road          N/A            33              21               54
 New       Broughton     N/A            4               4                8
 Road
 Elderly Housing
 Powder House Court      38             N/A             N/A              38
 Green Street Court      32             N/A             N/A              32
 Farrell Court           64             N/A             N/A              64
 New Farrell Court       48             N/A             N/A              48
 Roads School            41             N/A             N/A              41
 TOTAL                   223            49              35               307

The Marblehead Housing Authority is a quasi-government agency authorized by
State Law and regulated by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts Executive Office
of Housing and Livable Communities (EOHLC).

The Board of Commissioners consists of three members elected by the Town, one
member (a resident) selected by the Select Board following an interview process and




                                        237
                                        241
                                      241
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


one member appointed by the Governor. All members of the Board serve a 5-year
term.

The Board usually meets on the third Tuesday of each month and conducts its Annual
Meeting and election of officers every June.

The Board of Commissioners is responsible for establishing policy and approving and
monitoring the MHA’s annual budget. The MHA Board has established policies and
procedures in accordance with the state regulations under which the Authority
operates and monitors the operations of the Authority on a regular basis.

Administration:
MHA contracts with the Salem Housing Authority to form a partnership through a
Management Services Agreement. The Salem and Marblehead Housing Authority
work in partnership with one Executive Director, Cathy Sheehan who is responsible
for the day-to-day management and operations of the agency and its personnel team.

MHA is eligible for subsidy under its contributions contract with EOHLC. The
Marblehead Housing Authority receives no funding from the Town of Marblehead.

Eligibility
Please feel free to access the MHA website at www.marbleheadha.org for the most
up to date information and links to apply for housing programs. In order to determine
eligibility for public housing, anyone wishing to apply for housing must submit an
application and supporting documentation. The MHA conducts a review of income
and assets to determine preliminary eligibility.

The Net Income Limits for determining eligibility for state-aided housing are listed
below and represent 80% area median income:

                 No. in Household:               Net Income Limit:
                     1 person                   $82,950
                     2 people                   $94,800
                     3 people                   $106,650
                     4 people                   $118,450
                     5 people                   $127,950
                     6 people                   $137,450
                     7 people                   $146,900
                     8 people                   $156,400




                                        238
                                        242
                                      242
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



Local preference is given to eligible applicants who provide sufficient
documentation they live or work in the Town of Marblehead.

Veteran’s Preference is given to family housing and to elderly applicants.

Applications

All eligible applicants are screened to determine whether or not they are qualified for
public housing. This includes a criminal record check, landlord history, and credit
report.

The Housing Authority wishes to thank its dedicated staff for all of their hard work
during the past year.

We value the partnership, co-operation and support from the town, community
groups, public agencies, private groups and the residents of Marblehead in our efforts
to provide safe, affordable housing for the tenants of the MHA.

The MHA wishes to express its sincere appreciation for the outstanding cooperation
of the Town departments and the people of Marblehead who supported and assisted
us in our efforts to provide quality affordable housing for the residents.

Respectfully submitted,
Cathy Hoog, Executive Director

             MARBLEHEAD HOUSING COMMITTEE

The Marblehead Housing Committee was established in 2025 with the merger of the
Fair Housing Committee and the Housing Production Plan Implementation
Committee to address the need for affordable housing within the town and across the
Commonwealth. The Committee’s mission works to expand access and fair
affordable housing for all residents through education, advocacy, and strategic
implementation. The Committee works to support the development and availability
of affordable housing opportunities in accordance with the state and federal
guidelines. Households earning 80% or less of the area median income may qualify
for such housing, typically made available through lotteries. Marblehead continues to
fall below the state’s 10% affordable housing goal, remaining at approximately 4%
in recent years. While distinct from the state-aided public housing managed by the
Marblehead Housing Authority, affordable housing remains a vital focus for the
Committee.

The Housing Committee meets regularly on the second Tuesday of each month. The
regular meetings are hybrid and meet in person at Abbot Hall and on Zoom. The
agendas are posted on the Town’s website and at the office of the Town Clerk.

In 2025, the Committee was active in discussion surrounding MBTA 3A Zoning Law,
Article 23, which aimed to bring Marblehead in compliance, and passed at the Annual
Town Meeting, but due to a referendum to overturn the results of town meeting,
MBTA 3A failed to pass in a town-wide special election, rendering the town
noncompliant. Committee members worked collaboratively with Town Officials, the
Planning Board and Town Planner, and new Community Development and Planning
Director, Brendan Callahan, to develop education strategies for residents. The town’s
newly structured planning functions-formed by the creation of the Department of
Community Development and Planning has supported the Committee’s efforts,
including finding strategies for zoning compliance and housing initiatives. The
Committee was active in the discussion surrounding the former Coffin School future
reuse. The Committee submitted a letter of recommendation to the Select Board.

The Marblehead Housing Authority (MHA) provided updates on the redevelopment
of the Broughton Road public housing site. Winn Companies was selected as
development partner and is working on the design and securing funding to advance
this project. The North Shore HOME Consortium director, Kevin Hurley, attended a
meeting to educate and promote HOME Funds available to the Town. HOME Funds
are allocated for the Town to assist in the development of affordable housing.




                                        240
                                        244
                                      244
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


In 2025, the Housing Committee furthered its commitment to affordable housing
through direct involvement in the implementation of the Housing Production Plan.
The Committee actively supports the use of HOME Funds, federal dollars allocated
to assist in affordable housing development, helping the town leverage these
resources for new housing opportunities. Additionally, the Housing Committee plays
a crucial role in advising and supporting the allocation of funds from the Marblehead
Housing Trust, ensuring that funding is directed toward projects that address the
community’s affordable housing needs. By prioritizing these efforts, the Committee
works to foster sustainable and equitable housing solutions for Marblehead residents.

Respectfully submitted, Marblehead Housing Committee

     MARBLEHEAD MUNICIPAL LIGHT DEPARTMENT

Our mission at the Marblehead Municipal Light Department (MMLD) is to deliver
reliable, cost-competitive power to our customers, while reducing the carbon
intensity of our portfolio and maintaining a safe and healthy operating environment
for employees and customers.

Key Statistics:
In 2025, MMLD saw a moderate increase in energy sales and significant increase in
system peak energy demand, relative to 2024.

            Annual Metric                         2025                    2024

 Energy Sales (MWh)                                    102,469                   98,811

 Energy Revenue ($)                               $21,692,029             $21,519,213

 System Peak Energy Demand (MW)                      34.2 MW                 31.1 MW



Noted Accomplishments:
In addition to satisfying our mission, MMLD noted several achievements in 2025:
     • In cooperation with the Municipal Electric Association of Massachusetts,
         MMLD conducted a customer satisfaction survey that confirmed strong
         customer support, relative to other public and investor-owned electric utility
         providers. Specifically, customers reported an average positive rating of
         82.5% across all measured organizational characteristics, with over 90% of
         customers indicating that MMLD meets their expectations "all" or "most of
         the time." Over two-thirds of customers (69.4%) viewed their electric rates
         as reasonable.
     • An internal employee survey was conducted to gain critical feedback and
         inform MMLD’s General Manager and Board of Commissioners on
         professional satisfaction, workplace culture, vision alignment, and priorities
         for improvement from the perspective of Department staff.
     • The MMLD Board of Commissioners commenced its search for a new
         General Manager in May by establishing a five-member Selection




                                         242
                                         246
                                       246
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


        Subcommittee comprised of Simon Frechette (MMLD Commissioner),
        Adam Smith (MMLD Commissioner), Alexa Singer (Marblehead Select
        Board Member), Greg Chane (MMLD Distribution Manager), and Tom
        Howard (Marblehead Human Resources Director). The Selection
        Subcommittee screened five candidates from nearly 100 applicants,
        ultimately referring three finalists to the Board of Commissioners. In July,
        the Board of Commissioners interviewed finalists in public session and
        selected Jonathan Blair as the next General Manager.
    •   A federal grant of $1.3 million was awarded to MMLD from the Department
        of Energy’s Grid Resilience Innovation Partnership to support planned
        fortification of Marblehead’s twin, 23kV sub-transmission supply lines.
        Phase 1 of the project will replace seven utility poles adjacent to the Railyard
        Substation in Salem with steel or composite alternatives, while Phase 2 will
        move the existing overhead conductor between Lead Mills and West Shore
        Drive underground. These improvements are expected to enhance system
        reliability and resiliency while also enabling future expansion of
        transmission capacity by providing spare conduit for a potential third supply
        line. The project is expected to be completed by the end of 2028.
    •   Progress continued on the new Village 13 substation, with most construction
        elements being completed and migration of electrical connections
        remaining. The project is expected to achieve commercial operation in 2026.

Power Portfolio:
MMLD procures power for the Marblehead community through a combination of
ownership of generation assets, contractual agreements with third parties to buy
power, and open market purchases through the Independent System Operator of New
England (ISO-NE) marketplace. While ownership and power purchase agreements
prescribe the source of generation, market purchases on the ISO-NE marketplace are
subject to the real-time regional power mix at time of delivery. In 2025, MMLD’s
power portfolio was over 75% carbon free.

        OLD AND HISTORIC DISTRICTS COMMISSION

In 2025 the Old and Historic Districts Commission (OHDC) reviewed 135
applications over 22 meetings and referred 5 to Public Hearings. The Commission
granted 129 Certificates of Appropriateness and denied one application. There were
no appeals to the Select Board. The Building Department administratively approved
17 roofs on behalf of the Commission. Violations were typically resolved
satisfactorily through OHDC hearings. However, Town authorities continue to pursue
the following violation of significance: 59 Front Street – Haley Scimone.

The Commission is currently staffed with 5 full-time members and 1 alternate
member. All members attend hearings regularly for current affair awareness,
deliberation consistency, and administrative duties.

The Commission worked with the Green Implementation Committee and the
Metropolitan Area Planning Council on the Marblehead Sustainable Heritage Project
which proposed recommendations to OHDC guidelines aimed to integrate clean
energy measures into the districts in historically appropriate ways. The work will
continue in 2026 to clarify OHDC guidelines concerning these measures.

The Commission strives to evaluate the merit of each application, both as an
individual property with particular circumstances as well as a member of the larger
historic community. The Commission welcomes all interest and is grateful to those
who recognize the value in participating. We are pleased to be a resource available to
all.

The Commission continues efforts to publicize exterior features which are regulated
within the District and therefore require a Certificate of Appropriateness (COA) as
well as exclusions that do not. Descriptions of both are included in the OHDC By-
laws that are available on the Town website http://www.ecode360.com/MA1991.

Administration of OHDC is coordinated by Lisa Lyons in the Community
Development and Planning Department at the Mary Alley Municipal Building. Her
tireless efforts on behalf of applicants and the Commission alike are critical to our
mission and greatly appreciated. Meeting minutes are posted on the Town website.




                                        249
                                        253
                                       253
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


The Commission would also like to acknowledge with gratitude the assistance of the
Building Inspection Department, Engineering Department, Zoning Board,
Conservation Commission, Assessors’ Department, Town Clerk’s Office and the
Select Board’s Office.


Respectfully submitted,

                              PLANNING BOARD

The Planning Board is an elected board comprised of five members, each with a three-
year staggered terms and one appointed associate member. The board members for
the year 2025 were Robert Schaeffner, Barton Hyte, Edward Nilsson, Andrew
Christensen, and Marc Liebman. Steven Leverone served as the board associate
member. The Town Planner, Alex Eitler, oversaw the board's administrative process
and provided technical assistance to prospective applicants and interested parties.

The Planning Board meets regularly on the second Tuesday of each month and as
needed at other times. The regular meetings were held remotely on Zoom for the first
half of the year and began meeting in person in the Select Board’s room during the
fall. The agendas are posted on the Town’s website and at the office of the Town
Clerk.

It is the responsibility of the Planning Board to approve different types of
development including divisions of land to create new lots, all new nonresidential
buildings over 700 square feet and all new residential construction and also additions
that are over 500 square feet within the shoreline and harbor front districts. In addition
to the site plan approval special permits and subdivision control approvals, the board
issues smart growth special permits, wireless communication special permits,
incentive zoning special permits and land disturbance permits for larger projects when
there is no conservation commission jurisdiction. State and local laws which govern
the process for these approvals are Massachusetts General Laws (MGL) Chapter 40A
(the Zoning Act); MGL Chapter 41, Sections 81K – 81GG (the Subdivision Control
Law); the Town of Marblehead Subdivision Rules and Regulations Chapter 258 of
the by-laws of the town of Marblehead, MGL Chapter 40A and the Town of
Marblehead Zoning Bylaws.

Permits issued in 2025 include three (3) Approval Not Required – Form A
applications (ANR) and seven (7) Site Plan Approval Special Permits.

At the 2025 Annual Town Meeting, the Planning Board sponsored an article to
establish a new zoning district that was compatible with the MBTA 3A state
requirements. The adoption of the proposed zoning district failed. The Planning
Board assisted with the research and development of this proposed zoning district and
provided key advice on how to best tackle this state requirement.




                                          251
                                          255
                                         255
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


The Board participated in discussions regarding the development of the Town of
Marblehead’s Master Plan and the Town Charter and reviewed and approved (with
conditions) an application from the Marblehead School Department for the use of
lights at the athletic fields at 2 Humphrey Street.

The Planning Board also prepared the following Town Bylaw amendments:
    • Amend the town’s accessory dwelling units zoning bylaw Article IX Section
        42 to bring the bylaw section into conformance with new state laws and
        associated amendments to sections related to the provision.
    • Amend the town’s flood zone bylaw Article XII to update and bring into
        conformance with new flood maps and amend related sections 200-8.
    • Introduce a new provision and map changes to adopt 3A MBTA
        Communities zoning overlay districts that would allow construction of
        multi-family housing as a matter of right in three areas: Tioga Way District,
        Pleasant Street District, and Broughton Road District.
    • A citizen sponsored zoning article to amend the zoning bylaw to add a new
        provision to allow fishing gear and lobster trap storage seasonally allowed
        as an accessory use with conditions.

The 3A MBTA article was passed at Town Meeting and was subsequently voted
down in a referendum vote.

The Planning Board thanks the Building Department for information on zoning
interpretation and other assistance.

The Board thanks Maggie Wheeler, the Town Engineer, who provides technical
assistance to the board and to prospective applicants. Planning Board applications
are processed in the Community Development and Planning Department. The board
extends special thanks to Lisa Lyons and Jenn Smith for their assistance in keeping
the board running smoothly. The Board would like to express our sincere gratitude to
Alex Eitler, Town Planner, for his invaluable technical assistance and thorough
review of applications. Alex, who vacated his position in December, has been
dedicated and integral part of our team. We wish him all the best in his future
endeavors and thank him for his contributions to the town.

    Robert Schaeffner, Chairman
    Andrew G. Christensen       Marc Liebman
    Barton Hyte                 Steven Leverone, Associate Member
    Edward O. Nilsson
    Alex Eitler – Town Planner

                          POLICE DEPARTMENT

The Marblehead Police Department (MPD) is pleased to submit to the citizens of
Marblehead our Annual Report for the year ending December 31, 2025.

The accomplishments highlighted in this report are the direct result of the
commitment to excellence by sworn police personnel, our Joint Dispatch
Communications Center, and MPD’s civilian support staff.

Our Mission: The MPD is committed to the protection of lives and property, and the
safety of the community we serve, both residents and visitors. We strive to maintain
the highest standard of professional and proven police practices and to preserve
individual rights and liberties afforded to all citizens under the law. We are dedicated
to a procedural, transparent and cooperative effort with the community to identify and
address their concerns, reduce the fear of, or, actual crime, participate in crime
prevention and social justice diversion programs and improve the quality of life of
our residents and visitors.

Operational Overview: Throughout 2025, MPD provided a range of traditional and
non-traditional police services, balancing calls for service, proactive patrol,
investigations, major event coverage, and community engagement. The Joint
Dispatch Communications Center logged 20,249 calls in 2025 and remained the
Town’s communications hub for police, fire, and ambulance emergency response,
while also supporting walk-in activity at the police station. Our highest number of
calls includes medical calls (1094); burglar alarms (447); parking complaints (867);
motor vehicle stops (732); motor vehicle accidents (336) and Property Checks
(5,582).

The Police Department focused considerable efforts and resources on major events;
community engagement; technology improvements; restorative justice work;
specialized training; policy review and development; employee health and wellness;
data analysis; grant writing; and operational functions.

The accomplishments highlighted in this report are the direct result of the
commitment to excellence by sworn personnel, our Joint Dispatch Center, and MPD’s
civilian support staff.

Accreditation (Reaccreditation): During 2025, MPD successfully achieved its
second reaccreditation, reaffirming the Department’s ongoing commitment to




                                         253
                                         257
                                        257
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


professional excellence, accountability, and best practices. In May 2025, MPD
underwent an on-site assessment by the Massachusetts Police Accreditation
Commission. The assessment was completed in two days (rather than the customary
three), reflecting the Department’s preparedness and organization. Following
reaccreditation, MPD began work to fully digitize the accreditation process to support
efficiency, accountability, and compliance with evolving state systems and mandates,
in advance of the Department’s next assessment cycle in 2028.

Court actions and Crime trends: Court Actions, Enforcement Activity, and Public
Safety Support. In 2025, MPD had: 37 arrests and 13 protective custodies, 114
criminal applications, resulting in 49 probable cause hearings before Magistrates at
Lynn District Court, 5 arrest warrants requested and 12 summonses applied for 50
restraining orders and 9 Harassment Prevention Orders issued by the Court, 87
discovery requests answered in coordination with the District Attorney’s Office
MPD continued to emphasize timely follow-up investigations, victim-centered
policing, and coordinated work with prosecution partners to ensure strong case
outcomes and accountability.

Our overall crime rate in Marblehead went down 22% in 2025! Property crime is
down 33%, while crimes against the person are down 41%. MPD continues to focus
on educating the public regarding active fraudulent schemes, crime sprees (car
breaks), while using technology and proactive patrols to reduce vandalism and other
crimes throughout Town. MPD Detectives and Patrol officers continue to conduct
thorough initial and follow up investigations, while proactively patrolling
neighborhoods to reduce theft, vandalism or other illicit activity.

                                Against      Against   Against    B
                                Property     Society   Persons    Crimes

 2024                           246          10        95         152        503
