JDP Program, Opiate Settlement Funds and Essex County Outreach
During 2025, our Jail Diversion Mental Health Clinician Gina Rabbitt was
responsible for (600) six hundred cases involving individuals suffering from either
mental health or substance abuse related issues. Mental health/substance use calls and
follow-ups increased substantially in 2025, nearly doubling, reflecting both higher




                                        254
                                        258
                                       258
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


demand and greater public awareness of available services. Program development
continued throughout the year, including Recovery Coach Academy training (CCAR
model), opioid-related funding initiatives, and planning for an Addiction Education
and Awareness series.

Five hundred and sixty-eight (568) of the cases were diverted from the Emergency
Department (ED). Five hundred and ninety-eight (598) of the cases were diverted
from the Justice System. Through the opiate settlement funds, our clinician dedicated
8 hrs. per week towards opiate and substance use disorders through direct follow up
and education community education efforts. MPD also partnered with Essex County
Outreach to further divert individuals from the criminal justice system, while finding
them resources and treatment for substance use disorders. MPD has also partnered
with Police Assisted Addiction and Recovery (PARI) to identify resources for officers
to utilize when needed and expand naloxone and fentanyl strip distribution.

Traffic / Parking Enforcement and Road Safety:
Traffic safety remained a year-round priority in 2025. 315 traffic accidents
investigated, 355 written citations issued and 443 verbal warnings documented, 11
arrests and 46 criminal applications involving Chapter 90 violations, 22 civil motor
vehicle hearings. MPD issued 1,578 parking tickets issued town wide for a total of
$48,500 in fines. There were 11 traffic studies conducted with 692 selective
enforcement directed patrols to problem locations, MPD continued to use
enforcement, education, and data-informed deployment to address speeding, roadway
hazards, and neighborhood safety concerns.

MPD has also worked with the Traffic Safety Advisory Committee to address the
traffic change requests that have been received, as well as help facilitate the
improvement of traffic safety in Town. Several neighborhood traffic concerns were
discussed through this forum, identifying and clarifying needs, while providing Town
residents with options and data, needed to help make real time decisions and for
planning in the future. TSAC secured funding for and identified eight locations for
the installation of rapid flashing beacons pedestrian activated warning systems.

Community Services and Engagement: MPD is proud of every one of our efforts
to connect with the community. In 2025, we added and continued several programs,
while continuing our unique collaboration with the Council on Aging through the
Citizen Police Academy, TRIAD, Coffee with a Cop, High School community
service day, Car Seat Install Day held at the MPD and work with the Make a Wish
foundation. We held one Citizen Police Academy, our Tenth!, while finishing the
planning for a follow up “master” CPA program which will start in January of 2026.




                                        255
                                        259
                                       259
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


Janice Salisbury-Beal leads our TRIAD team (TRIAD is a collaborative effort among
local police, Fire, Council on Aging, Sheriff’s department and interested individual
seniors) and the monthly meetings. MPD personnel attended regular community
meetings (70 attended by patrol – dozens by the Detectives, SRO and administrative
staff) with a diverse cross section of civic groups and neighborhood associations, to
include both Rotary organizations, cub scouts, boy scouts, girl scouts, the LOWV,
Fairwinds Child Day Care Center, the Marblehead Racial Justice Team, School
PCOs, TFAD, MMHTF, the ADL’s North Shore advisory board, and The
Massachusetts ADL Law Enforcement committee.

MPD strengthened partnerships with the Council on Aging / Elder Affairs, the
Marblehead Housing Authority, and service providers supporting residents at risk
(including individuals facing mental health crises, substance use challenges,
cognitive impairment, social isolation, or housing instability). These efforts increased
non-enforcement visibility, strengthened relationships with vulnerable populations,
encouraged early reporting before issues escalated, and reinforced MPD’s role as a
proactive resource for residents. Officers provided presentations focused on scam and
fraud prevention, personal safety and situational awareness, elder abuse and neglect
recognition and reporting, and resources available through the Police Department and
partner agencies. Residents demonstrated increased willingness to seek assistance,
share information, and participate in collaborative solutions. MPD collaborated with
the Salem Police Department’s Community Impact Unit (CIU). MPD officers joined
Salem’s CIU for over a month and experienced non traditional approaches to typical
quality of life issues.

Department staff participated in several civic engagements; to include Thanksgiving
Dinner organized by the COA at the Masonic Temple; the Breast Friends Cancer
Walk, Special Olympic Cruiser Convoy and Law Enforcement Torch runs, attending
the Essex County District Attorney Summer Camp, the Doug Flutie Foundation for
autism awareness and helped fulfill a local youth’s wish through Make a Wish. In
solidarity with our Jewish and other Faith Based communities, MPD attended several
events and trainings to address the rise in antisemitism and hate and hosted active
threat trainings while helping create security plans. Special Events: The Marblehead
Police facilitated several events throughout the year, that included: the Fourth of July
fireworks, Memorial Day Parade, Veteran’s Day memorial and 911 ceremonies, the
Horribles Parade, the Christmas Walk and tree lighting, Menorah Lighting, and the
Marblehead Festival of Arts Concert series and activities.

Technology: Technology, Special Units, and Operational Improvements




                                         256
                                         260
                                        260
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


UAS (Drone) Unit: The UAS Unit supported public safety operations throughout
2025, providing security support for large events and operational assistance during
incident response, including an apprehension where drone deployment assisted in
locating and capturing an individual.

UAS deployments supported Town events including the Horribles Parade,
Independence Day events, Christmas Tree Lighting, Santa’s Landing, Christmas
Walk, and Menorah Lighting.

IT / Infrastructure Improvements: In coordination with municipal partners and
internal staff, MPD advanced technology modernization and operational resilience,
including transition to a new IP phone system, workstation upgrades, replacement of
main network switches, and continued improvements to infrastructure and
cybersecurity safeguards.

Licensing: Firearms Licensing Activity: In 2025, MPD issued 300 firearms
credentials. Liquor licenses: Sworn personnel serve as licensing officers as appointed
by the Select Board responsible for making notifications of violations.

Evidence and Drug Destruction: In 2025, the Property and Evidence Room
processed 291 items, a 7.9% increase from 2024. Items included found/booking
property and evidentiary items for criminal prosecution. $6,504.13 was deposited into
the Town’s general fund through unclaimed property procedures. Approximately 930
pounds of prescription medications safely disposed of through the CVS Medication
Disposal for Safer Communities Program (an 18% increase from 2024) $1,200
program award used to offset processing fees and purchase destruction materials

Grants: MPD is committed to applying for and managing competitive grants to
improve our department. In FY 25, we were awarded approximately $345,000 in
grant funds. These include the Federal Ballistic Vest Reimbursement fund, State 911
Training and Support Grants ; Municipal Road Safety Grant Program; for extra
patrols geared at impaired driving and speeding, along with a speed feedback sign
and traffic data box, MPD was awarded $121,310 from Massachusetts Department of
Mental Health (DMH) for our civilian mental health clinician and training. Our local
State representatives secured funding of $35,000 for a major equipment upgrade at
MPD. MPD assisted nonprofits in their applications for grant funds under the
Executive Office of Public Safety and Security Nonprofit security grant program.

Training: Training is an area that MPD has committed to expand and increase the
average hours each officer is trained each year. All active duty, sworn police officers,




                                         257
                                         261
                                        261
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


have been certified by the POST Commission. Additionally, all dispatchers met
yearly dispatch certification requirements. MPD staff also attended several
specialized training courses that are recommended but not required. These trainings
were focused on: Accident reconstruction and analysis, Freedom and Hate Speech,
Suicide intervention and prevention, Electronic Controlled Weapons, Health,
Wellness and Resilience, internal affairs investigations, National Center for
Biomedical Research and Training, Recovery Coaching, Emergency Management
Planning for Schools and Leadership. I’d like to highlight the individual efforts of
sworn personnel (Lt. Dave Ostrovitz and Sgt. Tim Morley) to become MPTC
instructors in several areas (ECW, Laws and Procedure, Legal Updates, Health and
Wellness), and desire to teach internally. MPD employees contributed maximum
points to the Town’s MIIA insurance training program. MPD implemented a formal
Field Training Officer program in 2021. In 2025 we had two officers (Nolasco and
Boutillier) graduate from the Police Academy and complete their FTO training.

Personnel: There were notable personnel changes in 2025. Captain Matt Freeman
retired and was replaced by Acting Captain Jonathan Lunt. Sgt. Osattin was promoted
to Lieutenant and Officer Andrew Dimare was promoted to Sergeant. One new hire,
Boutillier, joined the department and is working full-time. Ruth Curtis, Calvin
Powers, Deborah Frongillo and Joe Dipoli retired from their posts as crossing guards
after decades of combined service. Our long-time animal inspector, Daniel Proulx
also moved on.

Joint Dispatch Center: The MPD/MFD Dispatch team stood out again in 2025. The
Dispatch “GIS Dashboard” has greatly expanded and provides a valuable tool to
Town departments. This interactive ArcGIS Online Dashboard provides instant
access to vital information that Dispatchers use when dispatching first responders and
other personnel.

Miscellaneous: I’d like to identify a few other services provided by MPD in 2025
that can go unnoticed: MPD processed 993 Public Records requests, and 70 Notary
Public requests. MPD works tirelessly with stakeholders to address those suffering
from substance use disorders and mental health issues. We continue to support
community conversations on substance use disorder awareness and education using
opiate settlement funds and with community partners. MPD stands out against all
forms of hate and bias and works with our community partners to prepare for and
react to incidents of violence, or threats of violence. Chief Dennis King received
ADL’s Law and Education Day Community Leadership award. Many of these efforts
are with the TFAD, whose members are dedicated to addressing all forms of




                                        258
                                        262
                                       262
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


discrimination and hate. At MPD, our backup generator project is complete and a
major flooring project was completed on the first floor.

I would personally like to take this opportunity to thank the officers and staff, both
sworn and civilian, of the MPD for their dedication and professionalism in carrying
out their duty throughout the year. It does not go unnoticed, with positive feedback
and letters of accolades coming in from residents regularly.

Respectfully Submitted,
Dennis A. King
Chief of Police


                            ANIMAL CONTROL
ACOs:

The following is the report of the Animal Control Department for the year 2025:

Dogs Licensed 3010, Tickets issued: Failure to Leash 32, Pooper Scooper 10,
Excessive Barking 8, Failure to License 24, Seasonal Beaches and Parks 13, Failure
to Vaccinate 1. These figures do not include verbal warnings that are generally given
as a first offense.

11 dogs were brought to the shelter. Nine dogs were claimed by their owners. This
information does not include the dogs that were picked up wearing tags or
microchipped that were directly brought home. The two dogs that went unclaimed
were adopted through Friends of Marblehead Abandoned Animals. 3 cats were
brought to the shelter. One was claimed and the other two were again adopted through
the shelter. Quarantines issued: 8 dog on dog; 4 dog to person; 3 cat to person; 4 cat
unknown/wildlife; 2 dog unknown/wildlife.

There were Two Dangerous dog hearings, and the Animal Control Office directly
received approximately 538 phone calls.

Lastly, we are working with the community to educate them on living with our
wildlife neighbors and have taken a pledge on solving conflict with wildlife in a
humane nonlethal matter. There is an increase in our need to help orphaned wildlife
as their habitat continues to dwindle.




                                        259
                                        263
                                       263
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


We are seeing a huge increase in secondary poisonings to our raptor population as
more and more rodenticides are being used by the public. We are hoping to educate
the public on the use of nontoxic, non-lethal, cruelty free methods of controlling
the rodent population.
Respectively Submitted
Animal Control Officer
Betsy Cruger

                    PUBLIC WORKS COMMITTEE

The Public Works Committee was adopted by Article 20 of the March 9, 1970, Town
Meeting. The Committee historically met reviewed and reported on subjects from
efficiency, personnel policies, capital equipment requests, recommendations on
department structure and function. As Town Government has been reconfigured over
time, most duties of the Committee are now completed by Human Resources,
Compensation Committee, the Town Administrator, and the Town Administrator’s
Department Head Meetings.

While reviewing By-laws to prepare for The Water and Sewer Commission meeting
with the Town Charter Committee, I came across the Public Works Committee
Chapter 24-15 Article VII of the Town By-Laws. Since the Public Works Committee
had not been amended since 1989 nor removed by Town Meeting, the Committee
reconvened on October 27, 2025. The first order of business was to appoint the
Committee Chair, Amy McHugh, DPW Director, and Secretary, Andrew Petty,
Director of Public Health. This meeting also reviewed duties of the Committee and
discussed the Committees revised mission going forward. The Committee has been
meeting monthly since October, as required by the By-Laws. The lively discussion
around the duties of the Committee resulted in Committee members’ desire to keep
the Public Works Committee with amended duties and revising the Committee
members to address the proposed mission and duties of the Committee.

Revisions to the By-Law were discussed at every meeting, and a proposed Article for
Town Meeting was reviewed and approved by the Committee. The intended purpose
of the Article is as follows:
         1. Remove those duties that are completed by the Human Resource
              Department and the Town Administrator, which did not exist in 1969
              or 1989.
         2. Add Department Heads who oversee work in the public way, public
              property, and public buildings.
         3. Create a list of duties
                    a. that speak to efficiency of departments,
                    b. Provide platform to review and make recommendations to the
                         appropriate Town Officials on Projects within the Public
                         Ways/Buildings
                    c. Review Warrant Articles that involve the Public Ways/
                         Properties/ Buildings.
         4. To accept public comment on Committee work




                                       263
                                       267
                                     267
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


        5.   Propose that the Committee meet quarterly and as needed, since the
             revised duties of the Committee differ from the original By-Law.

The Committee is not intending to replace Department Heads Meetings, but to
continue with Open Meetings of designated Department Heads who fall under various
Elected Boards and Commissions, including the Select Board, Municipal Light
Commission, Water and Sewer Commission, Recreations and Parks Commission,
Cemetery Commission, Board of Health. Additionally, critical public safety
departments Police and Fire, were not seated on the on the Public Works Committee
and the Committee is proposing to add these entities.

On December 1, 2025, the Committee also reviewed presentations from Recreations
and Parks proposed Reynolds Playground Project and the Sustainable Coordinators
Energy Reduction Plan. All meetings were hybrid and had public attendance.
Meeting Agendas and Minutes are posted on the Town website under the Highway
Department Documents.

I am pleased to report that the reconvened Public Works Committee meetings in 2025
were well attended, with members demonstrating commitment and enthusiasm for the
recommendations that the Committee could provide. The continued existence of the
Public Works Committee preserves a valuable forum for staff to share operational
insight and technical expertise, to develop recommendations ensuring sound policy
decisions, and effective management of public resources.

I hope the residents see the value in the Public Works Committee and approve the
proposed Article to amend By Law 24-15.

Respectfully submitted

Amy McHugh
Chair
Public Works Committee.

                       RECREATION AND PARKS

OVERVIEW
The Recreation & Parks Commission submits this report for the year ending
December 31, 2025.

The Commission is made up of five residents who are elected annually at a general
town election in June. The Commission’s scheduled meetings are generally held on
the first and third Tuesday of each month at 5:30 PM in the Community Center at 10
Humphrey Street. These meetings consist of reports, the appearance of persons or
groups on the agenda, the review of correspondence submitted for consideration,
discussions of plans and objectives, and reviews of policies, rules, and regulations as
needed. The Commission reviews and approves the hiring of personnel when
recommendations are presented. Budgets are reviewed by the Commission annually
and they act as an advocate with the Finance Committee and at Town Meeting.

2025 SUMMARY AND HIGHLIGHTS

A. PARKS

Fields and School Athletic Facility Maintenance

The Department maintained the fields and is responsible for the field preparation,
including lines, for the following sports:
     • High School (Varsity, Junior Varsity, and Frosh) football, baseball, softball,
         lacrosse, soccer, and field hockey;
     • Youth Sports baseball, softball, lacrosse, soccer, and football;
     • Men’s softball and soccer, and co-ed softball.

Fields opened for the season in late March. The spring sports required the lining of
fields for varsity school sports first. Then followed by youth sports, mid-April
through the summer; adult softball through September; followed by Varsity/JV
soccer, field hockey, and football at the end of August. In September, October, and
November, all varsity, junior varsity, and middle school football, soccer, field hockey
fields, and youth sports fields were maintained for their seasons.

The mowing and trimming of athletic fields is done on weekdays with school in
session due to extensive usage on afternoons, evenings, and weekends. Line marking
with latex paint and/or non-caustic chalk is also done while classes are inside the




                                         265
                                         269
                                       269
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


schools. Mowing of properties began in April and continued through November on a
five day per week schedule. Leaf cleanups ran October through December, and spring
cleanups ran March through May.

Early spring and late fall field scheduling depended on when fields were dormant,
wet, frozen, or overused in any one area. Fall remains the best time to do seeding and
sod work on fields but was frequently in conflict with the demands of the activities
scheduled and the department’s continual shortage of funds, materials, and manpower
versus those demands.

All line marking, mowing, and field schedule changes were coordinated by
Superintendent Peter James. In the summer and fall, employees maintained the lines
on high school and youth sports fields. These efforts continually earned praise from
the school athletic department and youth sports leagues. The field paint budget
continues to be strained by additional field requests and rising paint costs.

During the year the Sports Committee met at the Community Center with Shelly
Bedrossian and Chris Kennedy acting as liaison for the Commission twice during the
year. Throughout the year, there was an open line of communication with the Public
Schools, Board of Health, and sports organizations to help ensure various town sports
teams and coaches could minimize conflicts and maximize usage. This included
opening additional indoor gym space to youth leagues to enable practice and play.

The Departments budget was level-funded but needs for services to fields and athletic
fields exceeds budget allotments. Budget and staff limitations, requests for extra
work, and changes on short notice remained a continual problem.

Field and Property Permitting and Management

The Commission received numerous requests for non-profit, town organizations,
fundraising, and business use of the Community Center and the parks. These requests
must be approved by a vote of the Commission as required in the Town By-laws.

Permit Application Process:
The application and insurance permitting process, as well as the Kayak, Float and
Pram Permit process continue to be streamlined online for Marblehead Recreation &
Parks properties including Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Chandler Hovey Park,
Community Center room rentals, Riverhead Beach, Stramski, and other department
properties and special requests.

Building, Parks, and Facility Remodel and Maintenance

Reynolds Field (Green Street) Refurbishment Project Marblehead Recreation &
Parks is leading the Reynolds Field and Outdoor Rink Refurbishment Project. With
the funds from the town’s Larz Anderson Trust, which currently totals 3.2 million,
the Commission is working with an engineering and design firm on the options for
refurbishing the Reynolds Field.

Three engineering and design firms responded to the RFQ for the Reynolds Field
Refurbishment project and the commission interviewed firms in September. On
September 30, 2025, the Recreation and Parks Commission unanimously voted at its
public commission meeting to accept the RFQ from CHA Associates. CHA
Associates' specialty in sports facilities, coupled with their in-house resources and
extensive municipal experience, present the best long-term value for the town.

On October 22, at the Select Board meeting, the Marblehead Recreation & Parks
Commission requested that the Select Board approve the project planning stage and
the disbursement of funds from the Larz Anderson Trust which currently totals 3.2
million.

During the month of September, the Recreations & Parks Commission also
coordinated a series of five community and public engagement sessions – both in
person and online – to provide residents the opportunity to contribute ideas or discuss
challenges before the project development phase began. The most discussed topics
were parking, the dog park, microplastics, drainage, traffic, and area safety.

Future stages of the project – slated for 2026 – include final design development for
all three phases, community coordination and presentations, continued community
engagement, final approval from the Select Board, and development of construction
documents for Phase 1.

Stramski Building Renovation
The Recreation & Parks Department continues overseeing renovations on the
Stramski Building for recreation and sailing programs. Work highlights of the year
included completed carpentry finishes and both rough electrical and plumbing by
Essex Tech programs. New water lines were run to the building and the Department
continues to winterize the property each fall with construction underway. The
renovated building will have an event space with a catering kitchen and two meeting
rooms on the second floor.




                                         269
                                         273
                                       273
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



Devereux and Riverhead Beach Upgrades
The Department continues to manage the properties at Devereux Beach and
Riverhead Beach. The Department leases part of the Riverhead Beach property each
summer to qualified vendors for seasonal on-the-water programming and SUP
rentals. In the winter, the Department manages float storage for individuals at
Riverhead Beach. In the summer, the department maintains and manages the parking
fees at Devereux and the kayak rentals at Riverhead Beach. New upgrades for these
properties this year, include:
     • Building and installing new kayak and pram racks for a capacity of more
          than 150 vessels at Riverhead Beach, in addition to installing and upgrading
          the pram racks at Stramski’s beach property.
     • The Town received an ADA grant to upgrade the accessibility of Devereux
          Beach. New Mobimats were installed, thresholds to bathrooms were
          lowered, and accessible access from the ADA parking spaces to the pavilion
          was created.

Tennis & Pickleball Courts
The Department continues to maintain and update its Outdoor Racket Court Policies
based on community usage of courts and feedback. The Department manages an
online court reservation system through SuperSaaS, which allows the Department to
track trends and data on court usage and community needs.

Court Reservation Systems:
   • Tennis Drop-In Courts
   • Pickleball Drop-in Courts
   • Badminton Drop-in Indoor Courts

The Department continues to initiate the Veterans Middle School & Seaside Park
Pickleball Courts Open Play system. Open Social Play is available daily to the
community.

Memorial Benches – NEW Memorial Bench Assessment and Policy
The Department spearheads the maintenance and upkeep of memorial benches. A
moratorium on new memorial benches continues to be in place. However, the
Department is underway with a new assessment of the conditions of existing benches
and opportunities for new bench locations and an updated policy on steps to create
new memorial opportunities. Current benches maintained by The Department are
located at Chandler Hovey, Castle Rock, Memorial Park, Crocker Park, Seaside Park,
Gatchell’s Playground, Fountain Park, Stramski and Redd’s Pond.




                                        270
                                        274
                                       274
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



Rail Trails
The department continues to work collaboratively with the Marblehead Conservancy
to maintain 5 miles of trails and 150 acres. The Department provides labor, support
and tools as requested and needed through January 25, 2032.

Jacobi Community Center
The Marblehead Recreation & Parks Department executed a contract for cleaning of
the Community Center for public use, and Council on Aging and Recreation &
Parks programming.

Additional Park and Property Renovations and Upgrades
Renovations continue at several properties in town. The department also made
upgrades or repairs throughout the year as needed to park and town properties,
including:
   1. Fountain Park survey and developing structural upgrades site plan.
   2. Green Street, light replacement and fix
   3. Gatchell’s field lights and poles replaced in partnership with Marblehead
        Youth Baseball. New field lights enable Marblehead Youth Baseball to host
        up to six games per weeknight at Gatchells Park and up to 10 games on
        weekends.

Building and Comfort Station (Restroom) Maintenance

In addition to the Community Center, the Department oversees other buildings and
facilities throughout the town. The Department continuously maintained a workshop
and equipment storage at Devereux Beach, maintenance garage on Vine Street,
Reynolds Playground storage room, Gerry (Stramski’s) locker room and sailing
classroom, Hamond Nature Center, and the Seaside Park grandstand storage area.

The department-maintained comfort stations (restrooms) at the following locations:
Chandler Hovey Park, Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Fort Sewall, Reynolds
Playground, Gerry Playground, Gatchell’s Playground, and Hamond Nature Center
on a seasonal basis. Water and sewer costs, electricity, cleaning supplies, and paper
goods came from the department budget. Restrooms at Devereux Beach, Chandler
Hovey, Fort Sewall and Crocker Park were maintained throughout the season by an
external cleaning company on the weekends. Throughout the day, restrooms were
maintained by Department Staff. The Department completed the winterizing of all
comfort stations in early November.

4.   SUMMER Programs – Indoors and Outdoors (June – August)

     Summer Program Participants by the Numbers

     • Youth Sailing: 275

     Summer Long Programs

     Playground Program
     The Marblehead Recreation & Parks Department’s signature Playground
     Program welcomed more than 150 children each week, with more than 1,350
     participants throughout the summer—running from June 30 through August
     21, 2025. The Director of the Playground Program and counselors provided
     programming and staffing for 1,350 kids aged 4.5-Grade 8 over eight weeks.

     Sailing Programs – Adult and Youth
     The Marblehead Recreation & Parks Department offers sailing programs for
     more than 275 adults and youth grades 3-9. It’s designed for sailors of all
     levels of sailing experience to build confidence to feel comfortable and enjoy
     being out on the water! Class curriculum includes on land and on water
     coaching that teaches water safety and basic sailing techniques. The youth
     program is offered for seven weeks 9–3 PM and the adult programs are
     offered on evenings throughout the summer.

     Tennis Programs
     The Department Tennis Programs served up fun and skills for more than 250
     youth participants throughout the summer.

     Tennis 4 Kids: our beginner tennis program is a fun and engaging way to
     introduce kids to the sport of tennis for ages 4–grade 4.

2025 Public Events & Initiatives
The Department launched a series of new and annually beloved events and programs
in 2025 to meet the needs of our residents and families highlighted below:

NEW! Chinese New Year – January 24
Marblehead Recreation & Parks welcomed community members to celebrate the
coming year of the snake! More than 50 children and families gathered to celebrate
with hands-on craft stations and ceremonial festivities.

Ski Bradford – Weekly Ski & Ride Program 2025
The Recreation & Parks Department launched the annual six-week program on
Tuesday afternoons to Bradford Ski Mountain. Every Tuesday, 40 kids in grades 3-7
packed onto a Healey Bus, charted by Marblehead Recreation & Parks. The
Department was able to lead and kick off the winter 6-week program because of high
interest in our one-day ski programs as well as the driver availability of charter school
buses through Healey Bus company. Each week Jan-March, 40 participants, 1 Rec &
Park staff member and 3 parent volunteers traveled to the mountain for an evening of
energetic skiing and outdoor winter fun!

Sweetheart Dance – Friday, April 4
Nearly 400 children and their special sweetheart attended this sold-out event with a
live DJ, dancing, dessert decorating and more! This annual, beloved event sells out
each year and is a highly-coveted, affordable, family soiree!

An Egg-citing Egg Drop Challenge with Marblehead Fire Department – Sunday,
April 13
The Marblehead Recreation & Parks Department and the Marblehead Fire
Department teamed up to provide the widely popular “Fire Truck Egg Drop
Challenge”, during which children designed contraptions to protect a raw egg as it
was dropped from the top of a 50-foot ladder fire truck. The event drew nearly 80
children ages 3 – grade 6 to enter the egg drop challenge.

No School Day @ Gatchell’s! – April 22
Marblehead Public School students did not have the full April break in 2025 due to
making up school days. However, for the one “no school day” on Tuesday,
Marblehead Recreation & Parks staff provided a fun and safe day of games, sports,
and arts and crafts!

Bug Out – July 11
Young children joined Marblehead Recreation & Parks and Hands on Nature this
summer to BUG OUT! Participants discovered some of our fascinating local bugs -




                                         283
                                        283
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


such as beetles, worms, slugs, ladybugs, butterflies and caterpillars! The event
included hands on learning stations: close-up observations of preserved North
American insect specimens, views through bug-glasses; hands on build-a-bug
activities, an insect-based craft project, a butterfly tent, and more!

Haunted Hamond: Summer Camp Gone Wrong! – October 25
Nearly 500 children and community members experienced the terror of summer camp
gone wrong through a lite scare or extreme scare preference!

2025 Scarecrow Stroll – October 18
Nearly 900 children, families, and community members strolled and trick-or-treated
through festive scarecrow designs. The 2025 Scarecrow Stroll drew the most
scarecrows yet – more than 70 - from businesses, scout groups, and individuals.
Highlights included a giant horse scarecrow from Crosby’s, sports program themed
scarecrows, timely K-pop demon hunters scarecrows and eager children enjoying
cider from the Landing Restaurant.

natural resources and recreation opportunities. The following people worked for the
Department during the past year:

                         SHELLFISH CONSTABLE

The contiguous beach which ranges from the end of Gallison Avenue to the end of
the Veterans Memorial Causeway, known as Devereux, Tucker’s, and Goldthwait
beaches, were opened for the harvesting of surf clams on October 1, 2025.
Marblehead is issued a conditional license to open our fishery by the Division of
Marine Fisheries on an annual basis. The clamming season in Marblehead is
historically during the months of October through April. The areas may open later
and/or close temporarily during the season when storms and environmental issues
arise. As of the writing of this report, there have been 60 licenses issued, 4 fewer than
in 2024.

Monitoring clammers, checking water quality and testing of the clams is performed
regularly by the Massachusetts Division of Marine Fisheries and residents that have
volunteered to serve as Shellfish Constables. Because of the diligence of our
Constables, we have a very healthy fishery with many undersized clams paving the
way for “keepers” in the following years. The primary shellfish in this location is the
Atlantic Surf Clam (Spisula solidissima).

A license is required for anyone wishing to harvest shellfish and licenses must be
obtained through the Town Clerk’s office at Abbot Hall.

Respectfully submitted,
Jack Attridge, Chief Shellfish Constable

            TASK FORCE AGAINST DISCRIMINATION


                          In the summer of 1989, a series of hate crimes horrified
                          Marblehead and resulted in community outrage and
                          condemnation. A concerned citizen requested that the
                          Board of Selectmen appoint a task force in response. As a
                          result, on August 2, 1989, the Marblehead Task Force
                          Against Discrimination (TFAD) was established.

The TFAD updated mission statement is: “The Marblehead Task Force Against
Discrimination is committed to ensuring that Marblehead is a respectful, supportive,
welcoming, and inclusive community for its many diverse citizens and visitors.”

To accomplish its mission, TFAD responds to acts of discrimination, bigotry, hate,
and intolerance; expresses the community’s condemnation of such acts; collaborates
with community groups to provide programs that combat discrimination and promote
respect; and seeks to support those affected by discrimination and hatred in
Marblehead.

The Task Force would like all citizens to know about TFAD's work and how to reach
out for help if needed. For this reason, the Task Force continues to be grateful for the
Town Moderator’s permission to display Marblehead’s No Place for Hate banner at
Town Meeting.

In May, Asian American and Pacific Islander Heritage Month at the High School was
chaired by student representative Maren Potter. There were several craft activities
for students to try, including origami, paper lantern-making, and Chinese letter
writing. The event was met with great enthusiasm and was instrumental in forging
connections within the school, highlighting the importance of diversity and cultural
appreciation while honoring the local Asian community.




                                         291
                                        291
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________



As part of its ongoing efforts to raise awareness, TFAD
had a booth at the Marblehead Farmers Market on August
23rd, where members distributed leaflets detailing its
mission and resources to help citizens and visitors in
Marblehead reach out for help and report hate crimes and
violence. TFAD’s “No Place for Hate” stickers were
distributed by TFAD members who were available to
speak with community members and visitors about TFAD
and its work in the community. Additionally, there was an opportunity to participate
in a craft project developed by the Racial Justice Team, which was supported by the
Task Force. The project was offered to youngsters to color a drawing, “Walk In My
Shoes”.

The Task Force Against Discrimination continues to hold monthly meetings. The
Task Force is fortunate to have an Observer from the League of Women Voters at
each meeting, who records the minutes and submits them to the local press. The
Marblehead Task Force Against Discrimination and the Marblehead Racial Justice
Team would like to thank the citizens of this magnificent town for promoting
diversity, equity, and inclusion. During this past year, the citizens have held a town-
wide celebration of the LGBTQIA+ community, raised the Juneteenth flag over our
Town Hall, and honored transgender members of our community for their courage in
advancing visibility and acceptance. Marblehead Pride stickers, which were created
by Task Force member Reece Dahlberg, were also made available.

On November 18, 2025, in conjunction with the Jewish Community Center of the
North Shore, the Task Force Against Discrimination hosted a discussion with Alice
Hoffman about her newest book, When We Flew Away: A Novel of Anne Frank
Before the Diary, for students at the Veterans Middle School. Books were generously
donated by the Lappin Foundation, Marblehead Public Schools, and other community
members to the students of Veterans Middle School, Marblehead Community Charter
Public School, and the Epstein Hillel School.

In January, in honor of Rev. Martin Luther King Day, TFAD supported the
Marblehead Racial Justice Team’s Community Breakfast. Additionally, along with a
proclamation by the Select Board, TFAD observed the Third Annual International
Holocaust Remembrance Day, a commemoration declared by the United Nations in
2005. Jared Kaplowitch, a Marblehead High School Senior, shared the story of his
survivor great-grandparents at noon at Abbot Hall and in the evening at Temple
Emanu-El.




                                        292
                                       292
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


There continues to be an unprecedented rise in antisemitic incidents. Chief King and
Marblehead law enforcement have been vigilant in protecting the Jewish institutions
and houses of worship. We appreciate all that TFAD member (ex officio) Chief of
Police Dennis King, along with the officers on his staff, have accomplished in
protecting our citizens and in investigating the incidents that have occurred
throughout the year.

Marblehead is a “No Place for Hate” community, a designation earned through the
Anti-Defamation League. Incidents of racism, ageism, sexism, anti-Semitism,
ableism, Islamophobia, and homophobia run counter to that designation. Marblehead
citizens will find our “No Place for Hate” stickers displayed on over 100 businesses
in town and on the doors of schools, places of worship, and other public spaces. These
stickers are a reminder that hatred, bigotry, and discrimination of any kind will not
be tolerated.

We would like to remind all our fellow citizens that, in addition to the Task Force
Against Discrimination, there are many resources available if you witness an incident
or are the target of hatred of any kind. These resources include:

         Police: suspected violations of the hate crimes law should be reported to the
         police in the first instance, so you can be informed of your rights. Please
         contact the Marblehead Police at 781-631-1212.

         Attorney General Andrea Campbell: The AG has established a hotline
         managed by attorneys and staff from the AG’s office. While not every
         incident will be appropriate for legal action, the AG’s office tracks reports
         and may refer appropriate matters to local law enforcement or the Attorney
         General’s Criminal Bureau at 1-800-994-3228.

         The Task Force Against Discrimination: Please reach us through the
         Selectmen’s office at 781-631-0000 or wileyk@marblehead.org.

TFAD acknowledges the tremendous participation of member Maren Potter for her
role as Marblehead High School student representative and wishes her well as she
moves on to Northeastern University. The Task Force welcomed a new member,
Veterans Middle School teacher, Maura Dartley-Rocco.

                           TOWN MODERATOR

Marblehead’s form of government is Open Town Meeting, a tradition that places
legislative authority directly in the hands of its citizens. Every registered voter is
invited to participate as a Legislator, with the right to attend, speak, and vote on
matters that shape the Town’s future. Residents may also submit articles for
consideration while the warrant is open.

Town Meeting serves as Marblehead’s lawmaking body. Its primary responsibilities
include reviewing and approving the annual operating budget, considering capital
expenditures, adopting or amending bylaws, and acting on other essential articles
brought before the Town. Through this process, Marblehead affirms its commitment
to civic engagement, ensuring that the collective voice of its citizens guides the
policies, priorities, and stewardship of the community. Open Town Meeting
embodies the democratic values that have defined Marblehead for centuries, offering
voters a direct role in decisions that sustain the Town’s character and well-being.

The overwhelming turnout at our last recent Town Meeting, which required
relocation to the Marblehead High School Fieldhouse, was both historic and inspiring.
It reflected a deep and genuine interest in the Town’s affairs and in the Open Town
Meeting process itself. I am sincerely grateful for the support shown by the
community and for the seriousness with which residents approach this responsibility.

As Town Moderator, I remain available to any citizen who has questions about Town
Meeting or who is interested in participating. Whether you are submitting an article,
attending for the first time, or simply seeking to better understand the process, I
encourage you to reach out. An informed and engaged electorate is the foundation of
Open Town Meeting, and your participation is essential to its continued strength and
success.

Jack Attridge
Town Moderator

                            VETERANS’ AGENT
                     MISSION STATEMENT
  THE MARBLEHEAD VETERANS SERVICES OFFICE WILL ASSIST AND
   SERVE ALL VETERANS AND THEIR FAMILIES WITH ALL VETERAN
   RELATED MATTERS. THE VETERANS OFFICE WILL GO OVER AND
       ABOVE TO HELP VETERANS/THEIR FAMILIES IN NEED IN
                        MARBLEHEAD.

Marblehead Veteran Services assists in applying for federal benefits through the
Veteran’s Administration. These benefits include enrolling in the VA HealthCare
System, applying for a service-connected disability or VA Pension, Aid and
Attendance, and much more. In some cases, there will be benefits for the surviving
family members when their Veteran passes. The Commonwealth of MA offers state
Veteran benefits, through MGL Chap. 115. Chapter 115 Benefits are financial,
medical, and service benefits to qualified Veterans and their dependents who meet
the income guidelines. Other local benefits include property tax exemptions and
abatements, education, annuities, job training and mental health counseling, to name
a few. We also assist with Food Gift Cards to help Veterans cover the ever-rising
cost of groceries. Currently, there are 5 Veterans who receive Chap. 115 Benefits in
Marblehead. In the last year, this office has completed and submitted approximately
over 125 VA Claims for Veterans or their surviving family members for disabilities
stemming from the Veteran’s time in the Armed Forces. This doesn’t include the
many other benefits that have been applied for, such as Education, Burial Assistance,
and more.

This office also assists and conducts funeral services along with local chaplains and
VFW Post 2005. All those who served on active duty are entitled to Military Honors.
Yearly, this Agent attends all required Federal, State and Association trainings and
passes all required Certification Exams. Executive Office of Veteran Services has
implemented a “Learning Management Training” curriculum for agents to complete
and pass.

This office has, at a minimum two mandatory ceremonies per year: Memorial Day
and Veterans Day. This office will continue to keep the traditions Marblehead holds
near and dear to their heart for these ceremonies, as well as bringing in some new
ones.

Due to the passing of two very important pieces of Legislation, The PACT Act and
The HERO Act, the number of Veterans and their families who we serve has grown.




                                       296
                                      296
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


We have teamed up with the COA to do regular get-togethers for Veterans, usually,
a monthly Coffee, which has helped to spread the word about the increase in VA
Benefits. We have also outreached to the various other organizations and resources
Marblehead has, to better assist our Veterans.

In 2025, the VA made monthly payments to 221 Veterans/Family Members. Here is
the breakdown:

Respectfully submitted,
Roseann Trionfi-Mazzuchelli

                 WATER AND SEWER COMMISSION

The Water and Sewer Commission is a five-member elected board, members of which
serve three-year alternating terms. Monthly public meetings are typically held on the
first Tuesday of each month. A public rate setting hearing is convened every year. In
2025, the Public Rate Hearing was held in June. The Commission oversees the Water
Department and the Sewer Department, both of which are enterprise funded.

                                  The Commission

                              The Water and Sewer Commission has suffered a great
                              loss of an exemplary leader with the passing of F.
                              Carlton Siegel (Carl). Carl began his tenure on the
                              Water and Sewer Commission in 1989, starting a 36-
                              year endeavor serving the rate payers of Marblehead.
                              Carl served as the chair for 23 consecutive years since
                              2002.

                              Carl’s tenure on the Water and Sewer Commission can
                              be considered the longest time served since the
                              combining of the separate Water Commission and
                              Sewer Commission in 1980. He will be remembered for
                              his practical wisdom, ingenuity, passion, and
                              generosity in sharing his prowess and skillset regarding
all things water and sewer.

As a retired Professional Engineer who worked for General Electric, every project
benefited from Carl’s review. His ability to read blueprints and question how certain
components were constructed or interacted with systems was second to none. He
proudly served our Country in the U.S. Navy as a machinist where he developed a
talent that he was more than gracious in sharing with the Town. His tireless efforts
were recognized for quality and workmanship by his colleagues and the employees
of the Water and Sewer Commission. Simply put, the Town, and in particular, the
ratepayer, benefited from a better conclusion to all initiatives Carl was involved with,
thanks to his dedication, and long-term vision.

Since 1989, the water department repaired 401 water breaks. Carl was onsite for most,
if not all of these repairs. While onsite, you would always hear Carl offering advice
and suggestions to the crew, often saying “measure twice, cut once”.




                                         298
                                        298
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


His compassion for Marblehead residents was evident, especially at the annual rate
study. Under his leadership, meticulous consideration of the water and sewer systems
were contemplated to develop equitable rates for the safe and efficient operation of
the Commission.

During his years on the board, he sat through 36 budget cycles, each requiring a
minimum of 3 review sessions; reviewed by the Commission, Finance Committee,
and Town Meeting. At every budget review session, Carl would open with “we will
review water first and sewer last, we bring it in and then we send it out”.

Carl’s professionalism showed, as he was the ideal Commissioner. He has the ability
to ask questions, collect information, and apply high expertise in so many areas so
that he could guide the departments without micromanaging or becoming entrenched
in daily operations of the departments. He spoke to the employees in a way that was
unique to each one of us. His compassion for his workforce will be missed.

Carl understood the roles and responsibility of his elected seat and became a
consummate Commissioner. Under Carl’s leadership, the Commission focused on the
improvement and maintenance of water and sewer infrastructure that has been in
place sine early 1900’s. With emphasis on safety, Carl followed the philosophy,
“work smarter, not harder”.

The Commission has developed two highly technical and efficient departments. As
Chairman of the Board, Carl was always mindful of the Rate Payer. The majority of
projects were completed on a pay as you go philosophy. Regarding larger projects,
Carl advocated for ways to spread the cost over time, thereby keeping a steady rate
and expediting constriction time. The Sewer Relief Fund was utilized for the
Marblehead Sewer Force Main Replacement under Salem Harbor’ and at Carl’s
suggestion, it was voted by the board to take a 10 year loan saving the Rate Payer a
million dollars in interest. The MWRA local assistance program, 0% interest loan,
was used for watermain upgrades. A FEMA Grant for phase II of the Pleasant Street
Drainage Project was awarded to the Commission saving the Town over 2 million
dollars. During the 23 years Carl was at the helm, the Commission received seven
awards of excellence. The staff at the water and sewer departments will miss his
leadership, support, and most of all, his friendship.

                                Highlights of 2025

                                   Water
Marblehead received approximately 40.74 inches of rain during 2025. As you all
remember, Marblehead experienced long durations of no rainfall during the hottest




                                       299
                                      299
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


months of the year. This resulted in increased water use which can be attributed to
the many properties that utilized irrigation systems. The Commission encourages all
residents to review their lawn maintenance plans and use best practices to conserve
water. GREENSCAPES North Shore Coalition is an excellent resource for lawn care
and gardening guidance: www.greenscapes.org

The water crew supported the major water main rehabilitation project that took place
from March to October by operating water main gates for planned shutdowns, testing
procedures, flushing of water mains, and providing support for the contractor
whenever needed. Water gates that were needed for construction were tested during
overnight shutdowns and replaced where required. 254 homes were connected to the
temporary bypass for this water project. The water department was present at every
appointment for both connection and disconnection, which often happened over the
weekend.

The water crew also assisted 678 homeowners with the determination of water service
line materials through onsite inspections.

                                        Sewer

The sewer department continues to make strides in the infiltration and inflow removal
program. Inflow is where clear groundwater enters the sanitary sewer system through
illegal connections. These connections are made directly to the sewer through sump
pumps, floor drains, roof leaders or indirectly to the sewer through hoses into slop
sinks. In 2025, twenty-five sump pumps were found. This number of pumps can
theoretically put as much water into the system while they are working as 625 homes
discharging wastewater. We all need to work together to eliminate these connections
and if you are not sure please give us a call and we will inspect your system and let
you know if the setup is correct at no cost to you. The cost of collecting and treating
storm water that goes into sanitary system is passed on to all sewer rate payers.

If you would like further information on how you can minimize potential issues
caused by Infiltration and inflow, visit www.RESUMP.org for more information.

The Sewer Crew supported and reviewed all work completed by the contractor during
the sewer main rehabilitation project.

The Sewer Employees should be commended for their ability to be proactive during
times of extreme wet weather and pump station operating restriction by deploying
Emergency Generators and Bypass Pumps preventing possible sanitary sewer
overflows.

                         Capital Improvements Projects

                                         Water
Contract 190 – Water Main Improvements
In 2025, the Commission continued the multiyear water main improvement project
with N Granese & Sons. This year, the Commission completed a combination of pipe
replacement and cleaning and lining; both with the goal of improved water quality
and increased fire-flow. Replacement areas focused on upgrading dead end water
mains and elimination of these dead ends through looping where possible. Cleaning
and lining areas focused on restoration of existing pipe to increase water quality and
fire flow to those areas. 2025 was a large construction year for the Water Department.
Overall, almost 2 miles of pipe was rehabilitated.

ductile iron pipe. Bubier Road, 2,000 feet of 6” unlined cast iron pipe replaced with

In 2025, the Water Department began draining the Burkes Hill water tank to prepare
for its scheduled cleaning. While it was drained, the Commission made the decision
to install new sample taps at 3 varying heights within the water tank. This will give
the Commission the ability to better track the water quality that is within the tank and
promote improved water quality through turnover.

Contract 191 – Sewer Rehabilitation
Following the infiltration and inflow plan developed in response to an EPA

procured a multi-year sewer rehabilitation contract which was awarded to National
Water Main Corporation. This contract includes the spot repair and lining of sewer
mains and manholes in the Clifton Pump Station District. Year 1 of the contract was
completed in early 2025 with the following work: 4,521 feet of 8” sewer main lined
with cured in place liner, 2,643 feet of 10” sewer main lined with cured in place liner,

to the pumping stations.

                             Regulatory Requirements

                               Water - PWS ID 3168000
In 2025, the Water Department continued its review of material for the 7,982 water
service lines in Marblehead following the EPA revisions to the Lead and Copper
Rule. To date, we have successfully identified 6,970 water services through a
combination of record review and field inspections. We thank all 1,337 residents who
have completed the self-reporting method of service identification. We are happy to
report that no lead service lines have been identified and the majority of service lines
in Marblehead are copper. The Commission is still working on identifying the
remaining 1,012 services through continued record review and field inspections. If a
property’s water service line material is classified as unknown or is one of the 4
properties in town with a water service that is galvanized requiring replacement, a
letter was sent to the property notifying that a further inspection was required to
record the service line material. The Commission has taken a 0% interest loan with
the MWRA which includes grant value for service replacement that meet certain
criteria. The Commission recommends that you contact the water department (email:
water@marbleheadma.gov) to confirm the year of installation and the material used
for the water service. Should your service material be galvanized iron, the water
department will be happy to discuss the procedure for water service line replacement.
A grant under the Drinking Water State Revolving Fund (DWSRF) was awarded to
the Commission that provided 100% of the cost reimbursement for developing the
water service line inventory and lead service line replacement plan. The Department
continued to work with Haley ward to complete the tasks of reviewing existing
records that contain information on material on the public and/or private side of each
service, created an inventory form for the public to complete a self-investigation of
the private side of their service while also offering appointments for the water
department to complete this survey, excavation at multiple water service line
locations of undocumented material were also completed.

In addition to daily monitoring of water quality, the Water Department collected over
300 water samples that were tested for coliform and other bacterial organisms to
ensure that the quality of water provided meets U.S Environmental Protection Agency
(EPA) and Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection (Mass DEP)
drinking water standards.

Each year Marblehead tests 20 homes for lead and copper levels. The results were
all below the Action Levels for lead and copper set by Massachusetts Department of
Environmental Protection (MassDEP).

Along with residential homes tested, the Commission annually selects two schools to
participate in the Lead and Copper Program. In 2025, one lead sample from the four
samples collected tested above the action limit of 15ppb. The school immediately
took the fixture out of service, replaced the fixture, and completed repeat sampling.
Results will be received and reviewed prior to the fixture being put back in service.
All schools in Marblehead were tested in 2016. Sample results from 2016 to present
can be found by using the links provided on the Marblehead water department’s web
page: www.marbleheadma.gov/water.

                                        305

The Sanitary Sewer Overflow (SSO) Notification Plan included the purchase and
installation of 10 Sign Boards containing information about beach and water quality.
The Board of Health and Sewer Department were awarded a grant for this educational
resource. The locations of these sign boards coincide with swimming areas,
stormwater outfall locations, and sewer pump station locations.

The main reason for the installation of these sign boards is to provide the public with
notification of beach closures and Sanitary Sewer Overflows (SSO’s). An SSO is the
accidental discharge of sewerage from the municipal sanitary sewer system. When an
SSO occurs and it reaches the waterways, these sign boards provide a location for the
Commission to notify the public. Simultaneously, these sign board provide a location
for public education and resources about the sanitary sewer system, the stormwater
system, and beach information.

SSO’s are most commonly caused by blockages in the sanitary sewer system or the
surcharging of the system during wet weather events. How can you help stop SSO’s?
By being conscious of what you flush and remove any illicit sump pump connections
from your home. If you do not know if you have an illicit sump pump connection,
please call the Commission for an inspection or go to www.resump.org to request an
inspection.

If you find yourself at the location of one of these sign boards, enjoy learning more
about the Town’s sewer and drainage systems and how you can help to stop SSO’s
and protect the local waterways.




                Figure 6: Completed Sign Board

Reports were submitted to Mass DEP, EPA, Division of Marine Fisheries and
Marblehead departments on four sanitary sewer overflows.

                             WATER DEPARTMENT
Marblehead received an average of 1.80 million gallons/day (MGD) of water from
Massachusetts Water Resource Authority (MWRA) in 2025. This is higher than the
pervious year’s average of 1.74 million gallons/day (MGD). The department operates
and maintains the distribution system, which consists of over 100 miles of pipe, two
pump stations, and a water storage tank. The eight-member crew repairs water main
breaks, completes hydrant maintenance on over 890 hydrants, conducts biannual
hydrant flushing, read and installs water meters, collects water samples, and responds
to all emergencies and customer service calls 24/7. The crew assists residents with
water usage issues and responds to any questions they may have concerning their
water. Employees trained in water sampling monitor the water entering the system
365 days a year.

Emergency Response
The Water Department responded to and completed 6 water main break repairs. The
Water Department’s emergency planning and training provided limited to no
interruptions of water delivery during the repair work.

In 2025, the Water Department responded to 131 emergency dig safes requests. This
requires marking out all water infrastructure in the requested area within 1 hour.
Requests are received 24 hours a day/ 7 days a week.

The crew responds to all requests for pipe identification from contractors excavating
in Marblehead.

                            SEWER DEPARTMENT
The sewer department operates and maintains the sewer collection system, which
consist of 28 pump stations, over 100 miles of pipe, and over 3,000 manholes. The
eight-member crew inspects, repairs, and maintains the entire collection system;
including sewer main cleaning, root control, fats oil and grease removal, daily pump
station inspections, and preventative maintenance. The crew responds to all
emergencies and customer service calls 24/7.

Marblehead sent an average of 1.78 million gallons per day (MGD) of sewage with
an average of 79 lb/day of Total Suspended Solids loading and an average of 2,833
lb/day Biological Oxygen Demand loadings to South Essex Sewerage District.
Following the Infiltration and Inflow plan, the Commission has inspected 6,582
properties and 550 illegal sump pump connections have been removed.

Emergency Response
The sewer department completed root removal and resealing of a manhole on Bartlett
Street. This manhole was a significant source of infiltration in the sewer system.
Responded to all SCADA Alarms. Sargent Road pump station had restricted
operations during a period where Pump #3 required repair.

The department responded to and corrected four SSO’s in 2025. 4/22/25 339 Atlantic
Ave. Cause: contractor bypass failure during sewer lining project. 4/22/25 357
Atlantic Ave. Cause: contractor bypass failure during sewer lining project. 6/25/25
Washington Street. Cause: Contractor broke sewer main. 8/6/25 Beach Street. Cause:
Mechanical failure of Vac Truck during maintenance operations.

In 2025, the Sewer Department responded to 120 emergency dig safes requests. This
requires marking out all sewer infrastructure in the requested area within 1 hour.
Requests are received 24 hours a day/ 7 days a week.

  REPORT OF THE SOUTH ESSEX SEWERAGE DISTRICT

The South Essex Sewerage District (“SESD”) facility and staff provide the region
essential wastewater treatment, keeping our waterways clean and protecting public
health. The treatment facility fulfilled its mission throughout 2025 and performed
these services to the Town of Marblehead within the approved budgeted amount. The
Marblehead Water and Sewer Commission would like to recognize the employees of
the SESD and commend them for their dedication to the member communities.

SESD celebrated the Centennial Anniversary of our wastewater treatment utility.
From 1925 to 2025, SESD has served local municipal sewer departments by
collecting and treating wastewater. In support of this essential community service, we
process approximately 25 million gallons of wastewater per day, maintain 29 miles
of interceptor and force main pipes, and serve approximately 190,000 residents and
businesses from our member communities of Beverly, Danvers, Marblehead,
Peabody, and Salem.

SESD is subject to MGL Chapter 59 Section 20B (Prop 2 ½). SESD is facing a crisis
situation in 2027 and needs relief from this situation for two reasons.
          1. Projected Operations and Maintenance Cost Increases.
          2. Costs and debt association with capital upgrades.

SESD has prepared a Centennial Plan which identifies the capital improvements
required to keep the treatment plant running efficiently, establishes a projection of
costs, locates advantageous funding sources, and creates a schedule. For more
detailed information on the Centennial plan, please contact SESD or visit
www.SESD.com.

The Town of Marblehead and the SESD are committed to protecting our surrounding
waterways and the environment and providing outstanding service to the residents of
Marblehead.

We would be pleased to answer any questions concerning the Town’s wastewater
collection system or the related SESD facilities.

Respectfully submitted,

Amy McHugh
SESD Board Representative

                      ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS

The Zoning Board of Appeals met in eleven regular sessions in 2025 from January to
November, via Zoom, at which it took the following actions: approved 45 and denied
1 special permit request, granted 2 extension requests, allowed the withdrawal of 5
applications without prejudice, and sustained a building commissioner’s
determination as part of one Appeal petition.

Labrecque, and William Moriarty. The alternates were Bruce Krasker, William
Rudolph, Christopher Casey, Marc Liebman. Special thanks to Lisa Lyons and Town
Planner, Alex Eitler in the Community Development and Planning Department. The
Board wishes him well as he undertakes new challenges abroad.

MARBLEHEAD PUBLIC SCHOOLS

                 Marblehead Public Schools Mission Statement
Our Mission is to foster in our students a passion for learning, and to provide a safe
nurturing environment in which they can develop the values, knowledge and skills
needed to achieve full potential in their personal, social and work lives, and to become
contributing members of society.

                  Marblehead Public Schools Vision Statement
A model school district, exemplary in its student engagement and academic
excellence, where all students meet their highest potential with the support of
outstanding instructional leadership and in partnership with the community.

Aligning with this mission and vision, our primary budgetary goals are to provide
students with the highest quality administrative and instructional staff, curriculum
and assessment tools, and an environment conducive to the potential of greatest
student achievement. Long and short-term strategic planning, and the budget process,
drives the prioritization of these goals with student achievement and growth always
at the forefront of planning.

Central Administration
The 2024-2025 school year presented some challenges as a new leadership team
began the work of aligning the district mission and vision to ensure relationship-
building and trust were the focus of moving forward successfully. The administrative
team worked with the school committee and the union to progress through collective
bargaining, a strike, and into the balance of a school year where stakeholders began
to come together in positive ways to ensure our students were being educated in safe,
nurturing and appropriate environments; setting them up for academic and
social/emotional success.

The Office of Teaching and Learning
The goal of the Marblehead Public Schools Office of Teaching and Learning is to
fully align teaching and learning, grades preschool through twelve, within a multi-
tiered systems of support framework to ensure all students meet or exceed academic
and social-emotional learning expectations. The focus throughout the year was on
ensuring students and staff had the resources needed to refine, broaden, and deepen
curriculum and instruction alignment within Marblehead Public Schools.
The leadership team and instructional coaches continued to work collaboratively with
educators to plan professional development, which allowed for alignment among our
Professional Learning Communities in support of instructional excellence and student
achievement.

Student Services
The Student Services Department administrative team was new this year and focused
primarily on establishing systems, expectations, and compliance with regulations.
Professional development opportunities were provided to ensure appropriate training
was in place and that staff had the resources necessary to service students. The
department strived to build consistent processes and procedures so that there was
consistency across all grade levels. The Student Services Department continued to
collaborate with other administrative leaders and stakeholders such as the Special
Education Parent Advisory Council (SEPAC) to discuss bringing improvements to




                                       321
                                      321
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2025
   ______________________________________________________________


the Department and the overall service delivery for students receiving special
education.

The Lucretia and Joseph Brown Elementary School and the Glover Elementary
School serve students in grades Prekindergarten through grade 3. The total number
of students enrolled at the Lucretia and Joseph Brown School for the year ending June
of 2025 was 447. The total number of students enrolled at the Glover School for the
year ending June of 2025 was 319. The Village Elementary School serves students in
grades 4 through 6. The total number of students enrolled at the Village School for
the year ending June of 2025 was 513. The Veterans Middle School Serves students
in grades 7 and 8. The total number of students enrolled at the Veterans Middle School
for the year ending June of 2025 was 405.The High School serves students in grades
9 through 12. The High School also serves a population of postgraduate students. The
total number of students enrolled at the High School for the year ending June of 2025
was 851.
